  In words: Chapter 1 starts with a real pain (1.1) -- hours lost hunting
  for information across scattered tools -- and answers it with Backstage's
  4 Pillars (1.2-1.9), already proven at companies like Spotify and Netflix
  (1.6). By the end, easypj's own jobpost app has a concrete destination:
  it will become a Component, an API, and a Resource inside the Catalog
  (1.12), and you know the two separate ways it relates to Kubernetes
  (1.11) before you ever deploy anything.


Chapter 2: Backstage Architecture Deep Dive 🎯 CBA 22% (Infrastructure)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  [META] Difficulty: Intermediate . Estimated time: ~75 min . CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

  [WHY THIS MATTERS]
       When jobpost's entity page takes three seconds to load, or a plugin
       silently fails to register, you need to know exactly which of the
       three layers (frontend/backend/database) broke. This chapter is the
       map of Backstage's own internals -- the thing you're deploying and
       later have to debug.

  [OBJECTIVES] After this chapter you can:
       - name Backstage's 3 layers and what each owns (2.1)
       - trace a real click (browser -> React Router -> API -> auth ->
         backend plugin -> DB) end-to-end (2.2)
       - describe the plugin lifecycle: install -> configure -> register
         -> run (2.3)
       - name the core database tables and who owns them (2.4)
       - locate any given setting inside app-config.yaml's top-level
         sections (2.5)
       - name the 5 core backend services and what each does (2.6)
       - explain how backend plugins actually communicate (HTTP APIs and
         extension points, not a hidden event bus) (2.3)

  [CBA RELEVANCE]
       Chapter 2 is tagged CBA 22% (Infrastructure) -- questions about
       where a setting lives, which layer owns which behavior, and
       app-config.yaml section names come directly from 2.1, 2.5 and 2.6.

  [SECTION MAP -- you are here]
       2.1 three layers -> 2.2 frontend -> 2.3 backend/plugins -> 2.4
       database schema -> 2.5 app-config.yaml -> 2.6 5 core services ->
       2.7 practice Qs.

    2.1  The Three-Layer Architecture
         - Frontend (React/TypeScript UI) — What users see
         - Backend (Node.js/Express API) — Business logic, database
         - Database (PostgreSQL/SQLite) — Persistent storage
         [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: 3-layer stack]

         [ARCHITECTURE LAYERS — Visual Stack]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                      BACKSTAGE ARCHITECTURE                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  🖥️  FRONTEND (React + Material UI)                    │   │
         │  │  • Plugin UI components                                │   │
         │  │  • Software Catalog browser                            │   │
         │  │  • Template creation wizard                            │   │
         │  │  • TechDocs reader                                     │   │
         │  │  • Dashboard with customizable cards                   │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          ↕ REST API                         │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  ⚙️  BACKEND (Node.js + Express)                       │   │
         │  │  • Plugin backend services                             │   │
         │  │  • Catalog ingestion & processing                      │   │
         │  │  • Scaffolder (template execution)                     │   │
         │  │  • Search index management                             │   │
         │  │  • Auth & permission checks                            │   │
         │  │  • TechDocs generation pipeline                        │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                          ↕ SQL                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  💾  DATABASE (PostgreSQL / SQLite)                    │   │
         │  │  • Catalog entities                                    │   │
         │  │  • User preferences                                    │   │
         │  │  • Template execution history                          │   │
         │  │  • Plugin state                                        │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.2  Frontend Architecture — React App Deep Dive
         - Material UI (MUI v5) — The component library
         - Plugin mounting system — How UI plugins integrate
         - Theming and customization — White-labeling Backstage
         - Routing and navigation — How pages are composed
         - Component registry — How plugins register UI
         [DIAGRAM: React component tree]

         [FRONTEND COMPONENT TREE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  <App>                                                        │
         │  ├── <AppRouter>                                              │
         │  │   ├── <Sidebar>           ← Navigation                     │
         │  │   ├── <Header>            ← Top bar with search            │
         │  │   └── <Routes>                                             │
         │  │       ├── <CatalogPage>   ← /catalog                       │
         │  │       ├── <CreatePage>    ← /create                        │
         │  │       ├── <DocsPage>      ← /docs                          │
         │  │       ├── <SettingsPage>  ← /settings                      │
         │  │       └── <PluginPages>   ← Plugin-registered routes       │
         │  └── <PluginProvider>        ← Plugin context/API             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [BACKSTAGE REQUEST FLOW — From Browser to Database]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🌐 FULL REQUEST LIFECYCLE                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  1️⃣  🌐 Browser                  https://backstage.easypj.com│
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼  GET /catalog/default/component/jobpost               │
         │  2️⃣  🔀 React Router            Matches route to Catalog    │
         │      │                           Plugin                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  3️⃣  🖥️  Catalog Plugin UI       Renders EntityPage component│
         │      │                           Calls useApi(catalogApiRef) │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  4️⃣  🌐 API Client (fetch)       POST /api/catalog/entities  │
         │      │                           by-name                      │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  5️⃣  🔐 Auth Middleware          Validates JWT token         │
         │      │                           Checks permissions           │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  6️⃣  ⚙️ Catalog Backend Plugin   Processes request           │
         │      │                           Queries database             │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  7️⃣  💾 PostgreSQL DB            SELECT * FROM entities      │
         │      │                           WHERE name = 'jobpost'      │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  8️⃣  ⬆️ Response flows back:                                │
         │      DB → Backend → API → Frontend → React → Browser        │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⏱️ Total time: ~50ms (with proper caching)                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.3  Backend Architecture — Plugin System
         - Plugin lifecycle: install → configure → register → run
         - Backend plugin types: Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search, etc.
         - Database layer: knex.js for SQL, database migrations
         - API Router: How backend plugins expose REST endpoints
         - Inter-plugin communication: backend plugins run INDEPENDENTLY and talk to each other only "over the wire" via HTTP APIs — the official architecture docs do not describe a generic internal pub/sub event bus; cross-plugin extensibility instead happens through extension points and modules
         [DIAGRAM: Backend plugin lifecycle]

         [PLUGIN COMMUNICATION — HTTP APIs & Extension Points]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  📡 BACKSTAGE PLUGIN COMMUNICATION                                      │
         │                                                                         │
         │                    ┌──────────────────┐                                 │
         │                    │  Scaffolder      │                                 │
         │                    │  Plugin          │                                 │
         │                    └────────┬─────────┘                                 │
         │                             │  1. POST /api/catalog/entities            │
         │                             │     (register the new entity it just      │
         │                             │      scaffolded)                          │
         │                             ▼                                           │
         │                    ┌──────────────────┐                                 │
         │                    │  Catalog         │                                 │
         │                    │  Plugin          │                                 │
         │                    └────────┬─────────┘                                 │
         │                             │  2. Catalog processes it, emits it        │
         │                             │     into the search index                 │
         │                             ▼                                           │
         │                    ┌──────────────────┐                                 │
         │                    │  Search          │                                 │
         │                    │  Plugin          │                                 │
         │                    └──────────────────┘                                 │
         │                                                                         │
         │  💡 Plugins call each others REST APIs directly (as shown above), or    │
         │     extend one another through explicit Extension Points/modules --     │
         │     there is no hidden pub/sub event bus wiring them together.          │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [PLUGIN LIFECYCLE DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔌 PLUGIN LIFECYCLE                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  📦 Install ──▶  ⚙️ Configure ──▶  🔌 Register ──▶  ▶️ Run  │
         │  (npm/yarn)     (app-config)     (createPlugin)   (active)   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐          │
         │  │ @backstage/  │  │ app-config  │  │ createPlugin│          │
         │  │ plugin-xxx   │  │   .yaml     │  │   ( {       │          │
         │  │              │  │             │  │     id,     │          │
         │  │ npm install  │  │ catalog:    │  │     routes, │          │
         │  │              │  │   locations │  │     apis    │          │
         │  │              │  │             │  │   } )       │          │
         │  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.4  Database Schema Overview
         - Core tables: entities, final_entities, locations
         - Plugin-specific tables: search_index, scaffolder_tasks, user_settings
         - Migration system: How Backstage handles DB upgrades
         [KEY DATABASE TABLES — What Lives Where]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TABLE               PURPOSE                              OWNED BY        │
         │  ─────────────────   ──────────────────────────────────   ───────────     │
         │  entities            All catalog entities (raw)             Catalog       │
         │  final_entities       Processed/stitched entity data         Catalog      │
         │  locations            Where to find entity YAML files        Catalog      │
         │  search_index         Full-text search index                 Search       │
         │  scaffolder_tasks     Template execution history/status       Scaffolder  │
         │  user_settings        Per-user preferences                    Core        │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.5  Configuration System — app-config.yaml
         - The central configuration file
         - Environment variable substitution
         - Per-environment configs (app-config.local.yaml, app-config.production.yaml)
         - Secret management (environment variables, not in YAML)
         [CODE BLOCK: Example app-config.yaml skeleton]

         [app-config.yaml ANATOMY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  app-config.yaml — Central Configuration                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ app:                                                      ││
         │  │   title: "easypj Developer Portal"                       ││
         │  │   baseUrl: http://localhost:3000                         ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ organization:                                            ││
         │  │   name: "Easypj Corp"                                   ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ backend:                                                 ││
         │  │   database:                                              ││
         │  │     client: pg  # or better-sqlite3 for dev              ││
         │  │     connection:                                          ││
         │  │       host: ${POSTGRES_HOST}                            ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ auth:                                                    ││
         │  │   providers:                                             ││
         │  │     github:                                              ││
         │  │       development:                                       ││
         │  │         clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}                   ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ catalog:                                                 ││
         │  │   locations:                                             ││
         │  │     - type: url                                         ││
         │  │       target: https://github.com/easypj/catalog         ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.6  Backstage Services — The 5 Core Services
         - Catalog Service — Entity ingestion, storage, querying
         - Scaffolder Service — Template execution engine
         - TechDocs Service — Documentation generation & serving
         - Search Service — Full-text search across entities
         - Auth Service — Authentication & authorization
         [DIAGRAM: 5 service circles connected to central Backstage hub]

         [5 CORE SERVICES DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │               ┌──────────────┐                               │
         │               │  📦 Catalog  │ ← Entity mgmt, ingestion     │
         │               └──────┬───────┘                               │
         │                      │                                       │
         │     ┌──────────────┐ │ ┌──────────────┐                      │
         │     │  🚀 Scaffolder│─┼─│  📖 TechDocs │                      │
         │     │  Templates    │ │ │  Docs-as-Code│                      │
         │     └──────┬───────┘ │ └──────┬───────┘                      │
         │            │         │        │                              │
         │            └────┬────┴────────┘                              │
         │                 │    ☸️ Backstage Hub                        │
         │            ┌────┴────────────┐                               │
         │            │                 │                               │
         │     ┌──────┴───────┐ ┌──────┴───────┐                        │
         │     │  🔍 Search   │ │  🔐 Auth     │                        │
         │     │  Full-text   │ │  SSO/RBAC    │                        │
         │     └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘                        │
         │                                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [UNDER THE HOOD]
       2.3 already corrects a common misconception worth dwelling on:
       backend plugins are NOT secretly wired together by an internal
       pub/sub event bus. Each backend plugin (Catalog, Scaffolder,
       TechDocs, Search, Auth) runs as its own independent unit and is
       only reachable "over the wire" via its own HTTP endpoints (2.3,
       2.6). When one plugin needs to react to another's output, it does
       so through explicitly declared extension points and modules -- not
       a hidden broadcast channel. This matters operationally: if the
       Scaffolder finishes a task and the Catalog doesn't immediately show
       the new entity, you are not looking for a broken "event" -- you are
       looking for whichever explicit integration (extension point, or
       the Catalog's own periodic refresh) is supposed to bridge the two.

  [KEY TAKEAWAYS]
       - 3 layers: Frontend (React+MUI) / Backend (Node.js+Express) /
         Database (Postgres or SQLite) (2.1).
       - Every user action is really a REST round trip: Frontend -> auth
         middleware -> the right backend plugin -> DB (2.2).
       - Plugins follow install -> configure -> register -> run, and each
         is independently deployable (2.3).
       - Core catalog tables: entities, final_entities, locations; the
         Catalog service owns them (2.4).
       - app-config.yaml's top sections (app, backend, catalog,
         scaffolder, techdocs, auth, proxy, integrations) each own one
         job (2.5).
       - The 5 Core Services are Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search,
         Auth (2.6).
       - Backend plugins talk via HTTP APIs through extension points and
         modules, NOT a generic internal event bus (2.3).

  [EXAM GOTCHAS]
       - Assuming Backstage has a generic internal pub/sub "event bus"
         wiring all plugins together -- it doesn't; they talk via HTTP
         plus explicit extension points (2.3).
       - Confusing entities with final_entities -- the latter is the
         processed/stitched result, not the raw ingested row (2.4).
       - Forgetting that yarn dev's transpile-only mode is NOT the same
         as a type check -- bugs from bad types can slip through dev mode.
       - Editing app-config.yaml but forgetting which of its 8 sections
         actually owns the field you want (2.5).

  [SPEED TIPS]
       - Memorize the "which app-config.yaml section do I edit?" table in
         2.5 -- it answers most config questions instantly.
       - When debugging a slow page, mentally walk the 8-step request
         flow in 2.2 and bisect: is it frontend render, network, auth, or
         DB?

  [COMMANDS / CONFIG TO REMEMBER]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ BACKSTAGE ARCHITECTURE — CHEAT CARD                                           │
         │                                                                               │
         │ 3 Layers: 🖥️ Frontend (React+MUI) <-> ⚙️ Backend (Node.js/Express) <-> 💾 DB │
         │ 5 Core Services: Catalog · Scaffolder · TechDocs · Search · Auth (2.6)        │
         │ Plugin lifecycle: Install (npm/yarn) -> Configure (app-config) ->             │
         │                   Register (createPlugin) -> Run                              │
         │ Key app-config.yaml blocks: app: / organization: / backend.database: /        │
         │                             auth.providers: / catalog.locations:              │
         │ Cross-plugin comms: independent plugins talk over HTTP APIs via               │
         │                     extension points/modules — not a generic event bus        │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PRACTICE DRILL -- 10 minutes]
       - In 5 minutes, draw the 3-layer stack and label what lives in
         each layer, using jobpost as the example entity.
       - Without looking, name all 5 Core Services and one job each.
       - Open app-config.yaml's skeleton and point to where you'd add a
         new GitHub token.

    2.7  CBA Exam: Chapter 2 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Architecture components, config, services, DB schema

  [VISUAL SUMMARY -- Chapter 2]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ CHAPTER 2 IN ONE PICTURE — a click, from browser to database and back     │
  │                                                                           │
  │   🖥️ Frontend (2.2)        ⚙️ Backend (2.3)         💾 Database (2.4)    │
  │   React Router     ──▶    Auth middleware   ──▶     PostgreSQL/SQLite     │
  │   matches /catalog/..     checks JWT + RBAC          entities, locations, │
  │   calls useApi()          Catalog backend plugin      search_index,       │
  │       │                   queries the DB               scaffolder_tasks   │
  │       └── ~50ms round trip, wired by app-config.yaml (2.5) ──────────┘    │
  │                                                                           │
  │   💡 One sentence: every click is a REST call that flows Frontend -> auth │
  │      -> the right backend plugin (one of the 5 core services, 2.6) -> the │
  │      database and back — and app-config.yaml is the single file that      │
  │      wires database, auth providers, and catalog sources together.        │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: Chapter 2 is the skeleton behind everything you'll click in
  this guide. The 3-layer split (2.1) is realized as a React frontend
  (2.2) that never talks to the database directly, a Node.js backend
  built from 5 independent core services (2.3, 2.6) that DO talk to it,
  and a Postgres/SQLite database (2.4) holding entities, locations, and
  search state. app-config.yaml (2.5) is the one file that wires all of
  it together, and it is worth returning to this chapter every time a
  bug's root cause is unclear -- ask which layer, not which feature.


Chapter 3: Core Concepts & Terminology 🎯 CBA 24% (Dev Workflow)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  [META] Difficulty: Beginner . Estimated time: ~60 min . CBA Domain: 24% (Dev Workflow)

  [WHY THIS MATTERS]
       Every chapter from here forward assumes you can read a
       catalog-info.yaml file cold. Chapter 3 is the dictionary chapter --
       Entity, Kind, Annotation, Location -- and jobpost's own
       catalog-info.yaml (3.5) is the exact file you'll write for real in
       Chapter 6.

  [OBJECTIVES] After this chapter you can:
       - define Entity, Kind, Spec, Location, Annotation, Plugin and
         Template precisely (3.1)
       - list all 9 Entity Kinds and their required spec fields (3.2)
       - trace an entity through its lifecycle from Location to Search
         index (3.3)
       - name the annotations that unlock the GitHub card, TechDocs tab,
         and Kubernetes tab (3.4)
       - write a complete, valid catalog-info.yaml for a Component and a
         Resource (3.5)
       - state the one-line rule that separates Labels from Annotations
         (3.4, 3.5)

  [CBA RELEVANCE]
       Tagged CBA 24% (Dev Workflow) -- this is the single most heavily
       tested chapter for terminology recall and YAML-reading questions
       on the CBA exam.

  [SECTION MAP -- you are here]
       3.1 terminology -> 3.2 entity kinds -> 3.3 entity lifecycle -> 3.4
       annotations -> 3.5 catalog-info.yaml format -> 3.6 practice Qs.

    3.1  Key Backstage Terminology
         - Entity: Any thing in your Catalog (service, API, user, team, etc.)
         - Kind: The type of entity (Component, API, User, Group, System, etc.)
         - Spec: The YAML definition of an entity (catalog-info.yaml)
         - Location: Where to find entity definitions (Git URL, file path)
         - Annotation: Metadata on entities (backstage.io/*)
         - Plugin: Extensible module adding features to Backstage
         - Template: Scaffolder blueprint for creating new projects
         [TERMINOLOGY GLOSSARY TABLE — Your 3.1 Cheat Sheet]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TERM         ONE-LINE DEFINITION                       WHY IT MATTERS                     │
         │  ──────────   ────────────────────────────────────────  ─────────────────                  │
         │  Entity       A "thing" tracked in the Catalog           Everything starts here            │
         │  Kind         The TYPE of an entity (9 kinds exist)       Picks the right YAML shape       │
         │  Spec         Type-specific properties of an entity        Different per kind              │
         │  Location     A pointer to where entity YAML lives          No location = no entity        │
         │  Annotation   key:value metadata linking to a tool           Powers plugin integrations    │
         │  Plugin       An installable feature module                    Extends the UI or backend   │
         │  Template     A blueprint for scaffolding projects              Standardizes new services  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ENTITY ANATOMY — What makes up a Backstage Entity]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  📦 ENTITY ANATOMY                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1                       │ │
         │  │ kind: Component                                         │ │
         │  │ metadata:                                               │ │
         │  │   name: jobpost                  ← UNIQUE IDENTIFIER    │ │
         │  │   description: Job posting app   ← Human description   │ │
         │  │   annotations:                                          │ │
         │  │     github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost  ← Links  │ │
         │  │     backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.         ← Docs   │ │
         │  │   labels:                                               │ │
         │  │     language: python                                    │ │
         │  │     tier: backend                                       │ │
         │  │   tags: [django, postgres, rest-api]                    │ │
         │  │   links:                                                │ │
         │  │     - url: https://jobpost.easypj.com                   │ │
         │  │       title: Production                                 │ │
         │  │ spec:                     ← TYPE-SPECIFIC PROPERTIES   │ │
         │  │   type: service                                        │ │
         │  │   lifecycle: production                                │ │
         │  │   owner: team-platform                                  │ │
         │  │   system: easypj-system                                │ │
         │  │   providesApis: [jobpost-api]                          │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.2  Entity Kinds — Complete Reference
         - Component: A piece of software (service, website, library, etc.)
         - API: An API definition (REST, GraphQL, gRPC)
         - System: A collection of Components + Resources
         - Domain: A grouping of Systems (business domain)
         - Resource: Infrastructure (database, queue, bucket, etc.)
         - User: A person in the organization
         - Group: A team, squad, or organizational unit
         - Template: A scaffolder template definition
         - Location: A pointer to where entity YAML files live
         [ALL 9 ENTITY KINDS — Complete Reference Table]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  KIND        REQUIRED spec FIELDS              EXAMPLE (easypj/jobpost)        │
         │  ──────────  ───────────────────────────────   ──────────────────────────      │
         │  Component   type, lifecycle, owner              jobpost (type: service)       │
         │  API         type, lifecycle, owner, definition  jobpost-api (type: openapi)   │
         │  System      owner                               easypj-system                 │
         │  Domain      owner                               hr-domain                     │
         │  Resource    type, owner                         postgres-db (type: database)  │
         │  User        memberOf                            jane.doe                      │
         │  Group       type                                team-platform                 │
         │  Template    owner, type, parameters, steps       django-service               │
         │  Location    target, type                        github.com/easypj/jobpost     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.3  The Entity Lifecycle
         - Registration: How entities are added to the Catalog
         - Processing: How Backstage ingests and validates entities
         - Stitching: How relationships between entities are resolved
         - Discovery: How entities are found via search and browsing
         - Deprecation: How entities are marked as deprecated
         [FLOW DIAGRAM: Entity lifecycle stages]

         [ENTITY LIFECYCLE — From YAML to Searchable]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔄 ENTITY LIFECYCLE                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. LOCATION       2. INGESTION      3. PROCESSING           │
         │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐             │
         │  │ Git URL  │────▶│ Fetch    │────▶│ Validate │              │
         │  │ File path│     │ YAML     │     │ & Parse  │              │
         │  │ URL      │     │ from src │     │ entity   │              │
         │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘             │
         │                                          │                   │
         │                                          ▼                   │
         │  4. STORAGE         5. STITCHING      6. SEARCH              │
         │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐             │
         │  │ Save to  │◀────│ Link      │◀────│ Index    │             │
         │  │ Database │     │ relations │     │ for      │              │
         │  │ (PG/SQL) │     │ (owner,   │     │ search   │              │
         │  └──────────┘     │  APIs)    │     └──────────┘             │
         │                   └──────────┘                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.4  Annotations Reference
         - backstage.io/* annotations and what they do
         - github.com/project-slug: Link to GitHub repository
         - backstage.io/techdocs-ref: Where TechDocs live
         - backstage.io/view-url, backstage.io/edit-url: Source links
         - kubernetes.io/* annotations for K8s integration
         - Custom annotations for your organization
         [COMMON ANNOTATIONS — What Each One Unlocks]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ANNOTATION                        WHAT IT UNLOCKS                         │
         │  ────────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  github.com/project-slug           GitHub repo card + README on entity     │
         │  backstage.io/techdocs-ref         The "Docs" tab (TechDocs)               │
         │  backstage.io/kubernetes-id        The "Kubernetes" tab (pod health)       │
         │  backstage.io/view-url             Custom "view source" link               │
         │  backstage.io/edit-url             Custom "edit this entity" link          │
         │  jenkins.io/job-full-name          Jenkins CI/CD status card               │
         │  pagerduty.com/service-id          On-call schedule card                   │
         │  sentry.io/project-slug            Error tracking card                     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.5  The YAML Format — catalog-info.yaml
         - Structure: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec
         - Multi-entity files: Using --- to separate entities
         - Relationships: owner, system, dependsOn, providesApis, consumesApis
         - Labels vs Annotations: When to use each
         [COMPLETE catalog-info.yaml — jobpost Component]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1                      │
         │  kind: Component                                        │
         │  metadata:                                              │
         │    name: jobpost                                        │
         │    description: Job posting and management application  │
         │    annotations:                                         │
         │      github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost            │
         │      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.                   │
         │      backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost                │
         │    labels:                                              │
         │      language: python                                   │
         │      tier: backend                                      │
         │    tags: [django, postgresql, rest-api]                 │
         │  spec:                                                  │
         │    type: service                                        │
         │    lifecycle: production                                │
         │    owner: team-platform                                 │
         │    system: easypj-system                                │
         │    providesApis: [jobpost-api]                          │
         │    dependsOn: [resource:postgres-db]                    │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [COMPLETE catalog-info.yaml — postgres-db Resource]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1               │
         │  kind: Resource                                  │
         │  metadata:                                       │
         │    name: postgres-db                             │
         │    description: PostgreSQL database for jobpost  │
         │  spec:                                           │
         │    type: database                                │
         │    owner: team-platform                          │
         │    system: easypj-system                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [LABELS vs ANNOTATIONS — The One-Line Rule]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🏷️  LABELS = for HUMANS to filter/search in the Catalog UI     │
         │  🔗 ANNOTATIONS = for MACHINES (plugins) to find external tools  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [UNDER THE HOOD]
       3.3's lifecycle diagram names "stitching" as its own stage for a
       reason: an entity's relations (its owner, the APIs it provides,
       the resources it depends on) are not known at ingestion time --
       they only become resolvable once ALL related entities have been
       ingested and their names cross-referenced. That is why a
       freshly-registered jobpost Component might briefly show no owner
       or no APIs immediately after registration but self-corrects once
       processing catches up -- annotations and spec.owner are read at
       ingestion, but the two-way relationship (e.g. team-platform owning
       jobpost) is only written during stitching.

  [KEY TAKEAWAYS]
       - Entity = a THING, Kind = its TYPE, Spec = its kind-specific
         properties, Location = where its YAML lives (3.1).
       - 9 Kinds exist: Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User,
         Group, Template, Location (3.2).
       - The lifecycle is Location -> Ingestion -> Processing -> Storage
         -> Stitching -> Search (3.3).
       - Annotations are machine-readable pointers (github.com/
         project-slug, backstage.io/techdocs-ref, backstage.io/
         kubernetes-id) (3.4).
       - Every catalog-info.yaml needs apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec --
         nothing else is required (3.5).
       - Labels = humans filtering in the UI; Annotations = machines and
         plugins finding external tools (3.4, 3.5).

  [EXAM GOTCHAS]
       - Mixing up Labels (for humans) and Annotations (for machines) --
         the exam tests this distinction directly (3.4, 3.5).
       - Forgetting a Kind's REQUIRED spec fields (e.g. a Resource still
         needs type + owner, not just a name) (3.2).
       - Assuming an entity appears in the Catalog the instant you write
         its YAML -- it must first be registered as a Location and pass
         ingestion (3.3).
       - Using dependsOn without the resource: or component: prefix on
         the target reference (3.5).

  [SPEED TIPS]
       - When reading exam YAML, find metadata.name and spec.type /
         lifecycle / owner FIRST -- those 4 alone answer most
         identification questions.
       - Recite the one-sentence rule from 1.9 whenever Entity, Kind and
         Annotation blur together.

  [COMMANDS / CONFIG TO REMEMBER]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ catalog-info.yaml — FIELDS CHEAT CARD                                      │
         │                                                                            │
         │ Required top-level: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec (3.1, 3.5)            │
         │ metadata: name (unique), description, annotations, labels, tags, links     │
         │ spec (varies by kind): type, lifecycle, owner (+ system, providesApis,     │
         │                        dependsOn for Components/APIs)                      │
         │ 9 Kinds: Component · API · System · Domain · Resource · User · Group ·     │
         │          Template · Location (3.2)                                         │
         │ Must-know annotations: github.com/project-slug · backstage.io/techdocs-ref │
         │                         · backstage.io/kubernetes-id (3.4)                 │
         │ Rule: Labels = for HUMANS to filter; Annotations = for MACHINES/plugins    │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PRACTICE DRILL -- 10 minutes]
       - From memory, write the 4 required top-level fields of any
         catalog-info.yaml.
       - List all 9 Entity Kinds in 60 seconds.
       - Write jobpost's catalog-info.yaml Component entry from memory,
         then check it against 3.5.

    3.6  CBA Exam: Chapter 3 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Terminology, entity kinds, lifecycle, YAML structure

  [VISUAL SUMMARY -- Chapter 3]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ CHAPTER 3 IN ONE PICTURE — from YAML file to a searchable Entity              │
  │                                                                               │
  │   📝 catalog-info.yaml (3.5)   🔄 Entity Lifecycle (3.3)   📦 In the Catalog  │
  │   kind: Component        ──▶  Location -> Ingest    ──▶   jobpost shows up,   │
  │   metadata.name: jobpost       -> Process -> Stitch        stitched to its    │
  │   spec.owner: team-platform    -> Search index (3.3)       owner + APIs       │
  │   annotations: {...} (3.4)                                  + Resources (3.2) │
  │                                                                               │
  │   💡 One sentence: an Entity is a THING (3.1) described by a Kind-specific    │
  │      spec (3.2) inside catalog-info.yaml, whose annotations (3.4) point       │
  │      plugins at the right external tool, and which the lifecycle (3.3)        │
  │      ingests, links to its relations, and makes searchable.                   │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: Chapter 3 is the dictionary you carry into every later
  chapter. A catalog-info.yaml (3.5) declares a Kind (one of the 9 in
  3.2) with a Spec and Annotations (3.4); the lifecycle (3.3) then takes
  that file from a Location on disk or Git all the way to a searchable,
  fully-linked Entity in the Catalog. Once you can name every field on
  sight, Chapter 4's hands-on setup and Chapter 6's real build will feel
  like filling in a form you already understand.


  [PART 1 KEY TAKEAWAYS]
   - Backstage is an open source developer-portal FRAMEWORK (Spotify
     origin, CNCF Incubating) built from 4 Pillars: Catalog, Templates,
     TechDocs, Plugins (Ch1).
   - It runs on a 3-layer architecture -- React/MUI frontend, Node.js/
     Express backend, PostgreSQL/SQLite database -- coordinated through
     5 core services (Ch2).
   - Every entity in the Catalog is described by a catalog-info.yaml with
     apiVersion/kind/metadata/spec, one of 9 Kinds, and machine-readable
     Annotations (Ch3).
   - A real instance is scaffolded with npx @backstage/create-app, run via
     yarn dev, and compiled/type-checked via yarn tsc / yarn build inside a
     Yarn workspaces monorepo (Ch4).
   - Configuration is a 3-file merge (app-config.yaml -> .production.yaml
     -> .local.yaml), with real secrets always in an env-var placeholder,
     never hardcoded (Ch5).
   - A working easypj instance is built in 6 concrete steps, then verified
     with a checklist -- never assumed from a clean yarn dev (Ch6).
   - The top first-time mistakes (SQLite-to-prod, token scope, wrong-branch
     Locations, missing TechDocs files) are avoidable with the checklist
     in 6.3-6.4.

  [PART 1 VISUAL SUMMARY]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PART 1 IN ONE PICTURE — from confusion to a running easypj portal        │
  │                                                                          │
  │   😰 THE PROBLEM          🔮 BACKSTAGE CONCEPTS      🛠️ HANDS ON        │
  │   scattered tools    ──▶  4 Pillars: Catalog,   ──▶  Ch4 install locally │
  │   2.5h/day lost            Templates, TechDocs,       Ch5 master CLI +   │
  │   (Ch1)                    Plugins (Ch1-2)             app-config.yaml   │
  │                            Entity/Kind/YAML (Ch3)      Ch6 build it for  │
  │                                                         easypj/jobpost   │
  │                                                                          │
  │   RESULT: a working Backstage instance at localhost:3000 showing the     │
  │   jobpost Component, its Postgres Resource, and its API — in minutes,    │
  │   not weeks.                                                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: Part 1 takes you from the 2.5-hour-a-day tooling problem (Ch1)
  through Backstage's own architecture and vocabulary (Ch2-3), then puts
  those ideas to work with your own hands -- installing (Ch4), configuring
  (Ch5), and finally building a real, verified easypj portal (Ch6). Nothing
  in Part 2 onward makes sense without this picture in your head first.

PART 2: GETTING STARTED — YOUR FIRST BACKSTAGE INSTANCE                🟢
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 2 INTRO]
  Part 1 gave you the concepts and the vocabulary; Part 2 is where you stop
  reading about Backstage and start running it. Chapter 4 gets a real
  Backstage app alive on your own machine -- the exact npx
  @backstage/create-app scaffold, yarn dev, and the Yarn workspaces
  monorepo it lives in. Chapter 5 turns that raw install into something you
  can actually operate: every CLI command's job, every app-config.yaml
  section, and the 3-file config merge that keeps secrets out of Git.
  Chapter 6 is the payoff -- it takes everything from Ch1's "what is
  Backstage" through Ch5's config precedence and applies it, in order, to
  build ONE real thing: a Backstage portal that shows jobpost, its
  database, and its API, verified against a concrete checklist instead of
  just "it didn't crash."

  This is still Dev Workflow territory (Ch4-5 both carry the CBA 24% Dev
  Workflow tag) -- the exam tests whether you know what each command
  actually does, not just that you can copy-paste it.

  ANALOGY. Think of Part 2 as moving into a new house. Chapter 4 is
  getting the keys and turning the utilities on -- the place exists and the
  lights work, but it's still empty and default-furnished. Chapter 5 is
  learning where the breaker box, thermostat, and water shutoff actually
  are -- so you can run the house like an owner, not a guest. Chapter 6 is
  moving your own furniture in: by the end of it, this isn't "a Backstage
  install," it's easypj's own developer portal, with jobpost's Component,
  API, and Resource actually visible inside it.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT -- Part 2 map]
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Read the map as a sentence: first you INSTALL a real Backstage app and
  learn to run it day to day (Ch4), then you CONFIGURE it properly --
  every section of app-config.yaml, real secrets kept out of the YAML
  (Ch5) -- and finally you BUILD one real, verified instance for easypj
  (Ch6). Each chapter is a prerequisite for the next -- Ch6's six-step
  build assumes you already know every command from Ch4-5.

  [PART 2 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION -- read before the chapters]
  By the end of Part 2 you will have gone from zero to a real, running
  Backstage instance: a scaffolded easypj-portal (Ch4) running on
  localhost:3000, configured with a proper 3-file app-config.yaml merge and
  real secrets kept in environment variables, never hardcoded (Ch5), and
  finally a fully built, checklist-verified portal showing jobpost's
  Component, its PostgreSQL Resource, and its API (Ch6). Implementation
  thread: easypj goes from "a URL for npx create-app" to "a real portal at
  http://localhost:3000 you configured and verified yourself."

Chapter 4: Setting Up Backstage Locally 🎯 CBA 24% (Dev Workflow)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  [META] Difficulty: Intermediate . Estimated time: ~90 min . CBA Domain: 24% (Dev Workflow)

  [WHY THIS MATTERS]
       This is the chapter where easypj stops being a diagram and becomes
       a real, running process on your machine. Every command here is one
       you'll run again for real in Chapter 6 when you build the actual
       easypj portal.

  [OBJECTIVES] After this chapter you can:
       - verify your machine meets Backstage's real prerequisites (Node
         LTS, Yarn via Corepack) (4.1)
       - scaffold a brand-new Backstage app with npx
         @backstage/create-app (4.2)
       - navigate the generated directory structure and say what each
         top-level folder does (4.3)
       - run Backstage in dev mode vs build-and-start it for production
         (4.4)
       - apply a custom MUI theme to brand the portal for easypj (4.5)
       - explain the difference between yarn dev (transpile only), yarn
         tsc (type-check) and yarn build (compile and bundle) (4.6)
       - manage dependencies safely in a Yarn workspaces monorepo (4.7)

  [CBA RELEVANCE]
       Tagged CBA 24% (Dev Workflow) -- hands-on setup, TypeScript
       compilation behavior, and Yarn workspace commands are frequently
       tested as "what does this command actually do" questions.

  [SECTION MAP -- you are here]
       4.1 prerequisites -> 4.2 create-app -> 4.3 directory structure ->
       4.4 dev vs prod -> 4.5 theming -> 4.6 TypeScript compiling -> 4.7
       Yarn dependency management -> 4.8 practice Qs.

    4.1  Prerequisites — What You Need
         - Node.js — Active LTS release; official docs currently recommend Node 22 or 24 (e.g. `nvm install 22` / `nvm install 24`) — Node 18 is no longer the documented baseline
         - Yarn — Backstage currently pins Yarn 4.4.1 via Corepack (`corepack enable` then `yarn set version 4.4.1`), not classic v1.22 or a generic "v3+"
         - Git installed and configured
         - Docker (optional, for PostgreSQL)
         - 8 GB+ RAM recommended
         - Operating System: Windows, macOS, or Linux
         [PREREQUISITE CHECKLIST - Verify Before You Start]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☐ node --version    shows v22.x or v24.x         │
         │  ☐ yarn --version    shows 4.4.1 (via corepack)   │
         │  ☐ git --version     any recent version           │
         │  ☐ docker --version  installed (for PostgreSQL)   │
         │  ☐ Free RAM          8 GB+ available              │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.2  Creating Your First Backstage App
         - Using npx @backstage/create-app
         - Step-by-step: The create-app wizard
         - What gets created: Directory structure overview
         - First run: yarn dev and what to expect
         [STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH WITH SCREENSHOTS]

         [CREATE-APP WORKFLOW — 4 Steps]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🚀 CREATING YOUR FIRST BACKSTAGE APP                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1: Create the app                                      │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ $ npx @backstage/create-app@latest                       ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ ? Enter a name for the app [required] easypj-portal      ││
         │  │ ? Select database [required] SQLite (for dev)            ││
         │  │                                                          ││
         │  │ Creating the app...                                      ││
         │  │ ✔ Installed dependencies                                 ││
         │  │ ✔ Created app structure                                  ││
         │  │ 🎉 All done!                                             ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2: Navigate into the app                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ $ cd easypj-portal                                       ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3: Install dependencies                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ $ yarn install                                           ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 4: Start the dev server                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ $ yarn dev                    # Starts at localhost:3000 ││
         │  │ $ open http://localhost:3000                             ││
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.3  Directory Structure — Every File Explained
         - packages/app: Frontend application
         - packages/backend: Backend services
         - app-config.yaml: Central configuration
         - catalog-info.yaml: Your Backstage's own entity
         - package.json: Dependencies and scripts
         - plugins/: Custom plugins directory
         [DIRECTORY TREE DIAGRAM WITH ANNOTATIONS]

         [ANATOMY OF A BACKSTAGE PROJECT]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  easypj-portal/                                              │
         │  ├── .github/              ← CI/CD workflows                 │
         │  ├── packages/                                                │
         │  │   ├── app/              ← 🖥️ FRONTEND (React UI)          │
         │  │   │   ├── src/                                             │
         │  │   │   │   ├── App.tsx         ← Main React component      │
         │  │   │   │   ├── apis.ts         ← API client config         │
         │  │   │   │   ├── components/     ← Shared UI components      │
         │  │   │   │   └── index.tsx       ← Entry point               │
         │  │   │   └── package.json                                     │
         │  │   ├── backend/           ← ⚙️ BACKEND (Node.js API)       │
         │  │   │   ├── src/                                             │
         │  │   │   │   ├── index.ts         ← Backend entry point      │
         │  │   │   │   ├── plugins/         ← Backend plugins           │
         │  │   │   │   └── types.ts         ← TypeScript types         │
         │  │   │   └── package.json                                     │
         │  │   └── README.md                                            │
         │  ├── plugins/              ← 🔌 CUSTOM PLUGINS                │
         │  ├── app-config.yaml       ← ⚙️ CENTRAL CONFIG                │
         │  ├── app-config.local.yaml ← Local overrides                 │
         │  ├── catalog-info.yaml     ← 📦 Your portal's entity         │
         │  ├── package.json          ← Root dependencies               │
         │  └── yarn.lock                                                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [REAL-LIFE: Creating the easypj Portal — Step by Step]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ mkdir easypj-portal && cd easypj-portal                   │
         │  $ npx @backstage/create-app@latest                          │
         │  ? Enter a name for the app [required] easypj-portal         │
         │  ? Select database [required] SQLite (recommended for dev)   │
         │  Creating the app... this may take 2-3 minutes               │

         │  $ yarn tsc && yarn dev   # Start dev server                │
         │  [0] Server running on http://localhost:3000                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.4  Running Backstage — Dev vs Production
         [REAL-LIFE: Dev vs Production Commands]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  MODE        COMMAND          WHAT HAPPENS                   │
         │  ─────────── ──────────────── ────────────────────────────── │
         │  Development  yarn dev        Hot reload; frontend :3000,    │
         │                               backend :7007, SQLite dev.db   │
         │  Production   yarn build      Compiles TS, bundles React,    │
         │               yarn start      Serves on :7007 (single port)  │
         │  Docker       docker build    Multi-stage: install → tsc →   │
         │               -t backstage .  copy dist → run node :7007     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [REAL-LIFE: Customizing app-config.yaml for easypj]

         │  organization: { name: Easypj Corp }                         │
         │  backend: { baseUrl: http://localhost:7007,                  │

         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.5  First Customization — Changing the Theme
         - app-config.yaml: Changing title, logo, colors
         - Custom theme: Creating a custom MUI theme
         - Dark mode / Light mode toggle
         [MUI THEME CUSTOMIZATION — Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/app/src/theme/easypjTheme.ts                   │
         │  import { createTheme } from '@material-ui/core/styles';    │
         │                                                             │
         │  export const easypjTheme = createTheme({                   │
         │    palette: {                                               │
         │      type: 'dark',              // dark mode default        │
         │      primary: { main: '#326ce5' },   // easypj brand blue   │
         │      secondary: { main: '#ff6b6b' }, // easypj accent       │
         │      background: { default: '#0d1117', paper: '#161b22' },  │
         │    },                                                       │
         │  });                                                        │
         │                                                             │
         │  // packages/app/src/App.tsx — apply it                     │
         │  import { easypjTheme } from './theme/easypjTheme';         │
         │  const app = createApp({                                    │
         │    apis,                                                    │
         │    themes: [{ id: 'easypj', title: 'easypj Dark',           │
         │               variant: 'dark', Provider: (props) => (       │
         │                 <ThemeProvider theme={easypjTheme}>         │
         │                   {props.children}                          │
         │                 </ThemeProvider>) }],                       │
         │  });                                                        │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.6  Compiling a Backstage Project with TypeScript
         - Backstage is 100% TypeScript — every package has its own tsconfig.json
         - yarn tsc TYPE-CHECKS the whole monorepo — it does NOT produce runnable
           output. In dev mode (yarn dev), ts-node/webpack transpile on the fly
           WITHOUT type-checking for speed — types are only enforced by tsc
         - yarn build DOES produce runnable output: compiles + bundles for prod
         - Project references: each package's tsconfig.json extends the root
           tsconfig.json and lists its dependencies under references — this is
           what makes incremental builds possible
         - Incremental builds: TypeScript caches compile state in .tsbuildinfo
           files (one per package) — only CHANGED packages (and their dependents)
           recompile, not the whole monorepo every time

         [TYPESCRIPT COMPILATION — What yarn tsc Actually Does]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  yarn dev (development)              yarn tsc (type-check)          │
         │  ─────────────────────────           ─────────────────────────      │
         │  ts-node/webpack TRANSPILE            TypeScript compiler CHECKS    │
         │  on the fly — strips types,           types across ALL packages     │
         │  does NOT verify correctness          using project references      │
         │  ⚡ Fast startup, unsafe               🐢 Slower, but catches every  │
         │                                       type error before you commit  │
         │                                                                     │
         │  yarn build (production)                                            │
         │  ─────────────────────────                                          │
         │  Full compile + bundle. Runs tsc, THEN webpack/esbuild bundles      │
         │  the output into packages/*/dist for yarn start to serve.           │
         │                                                                     │
         │  🔑 RULE: yarn dev can run with type errors present. yarn build     │
         │     and CI's yarn tsc will FAIL the pipeline if any exist.          │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [COMMON TYPESCRIPT COMPILE ERRORS — And Their Fixes]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ERROR                                   LIKELY CAUSE & FIX              │
         │  ───────────────────────────────────     ──────────────────────────────  │
         │  TS2307: Cannot find module '...'         Missing dependency — run       │
         │                                           yarn install or add the pkg    │
         │  TS2322: Type 'X' is not assignable       Wrong shape passed to a prop   │
         │          to type 'Y'                      or function — check the types  │
         │  TS2339: Property 'X' does not            Typo, or the type needs a      │
         │          exist on type 'Y'                custom interface extension     │
         │  TS6059: File is not under 'rootDir'      Import reaches outside the     │
         │                                           package — use a workspace dep  │
         │          instead                                                         │
         │  Project references errors                Run yarn tsc --build --clean   │
         │  ('referenced project may not             then rebuild — stale           │
         │  disable emit')                           .tsbuildinfo cache             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] 💡 Speed tip: yarn tsc re-runs full project-reference
         checking. For a single package during active development, run
         yarn workspace app tsc --noEmit to type-check just that package
         — much faster feedback while iterating.

    4.7  Downloading & Installing Dependencies with NPM/Yarn
         - Backstage pins an exact Yarn version (currently 4.4.1) via Corepack, not npm, and not classic v1 or a loose "v3+" range
         - A fresh Backstage app is a YARN WORKSPACES monorepo — packages/app,
           packages/backend, and every plugins/* folder are separate workspaces
           sharing ONE root node_modules (hoisted) and ONE yarn.lock
         - yarn.lock pins EXACT versions across the whole monorepo — never hand-
           edit it, and never delete it to "fix" a problem (that changes every
           dependency's resolved version, which can silently break Backstage)

         [BACKSTAGE MONOREPO — How Yarn Workspaces Fit Together]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  easypj-portal/                     (root workspace — yarn.lock lives here)  │
         │  ├── package.json         ← workspaces: ["packages/*", "plugins/*"]          │
         │  ├── yarn.lock             ← ONE lockfile for the ENTIRE monorepo            │
         │  ├── node_modules/         ← HOISTED deps shared by all workspaces           │
         │  ├── packages/                                                               │
         │  │   ├── app/package.json      ← its own deps, still resolved centrally      │
         │  │   └── backend/package.json  ← its own deps, still resolved centrally      │
         │  └── plugins/                                                                │
         │      └── easypj-dashboard/package.json                                       │
         │                                                                              │
         │  💡 Adding a dependency to ONE workspace still updates the ROOT              │
         │     yarn.lock — that's expected, not a mistake.                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [YARN COMMANDS — Dependency Management Cheat Sheet]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  COMMAND                                      WHAT IT DOES                   │
         │  ───────────────────────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────      │
         │  yarn install                                  Install everything from       │
         │                                                 yarn.lock (or resolve new)   │
         │  yarn install --frozen-lockfile                CI-safe install — FAILS if    │
         │                                                 yarn.lock would change       │
         │  yarn workspace app add @mui/icons-material     Add a dep to ONE workspace   │
         │  yarn workspace backend add pg                  (here: packages/app, then    │
         │                                                 packages/backend)            │
         │  yarn workspace app remove some-pkg             Remove a dep from one        │
         │                                                 workspace                    │
         │  yarn why <package>                            Explain WHY a package is      │
         │                                                 installed (debug conflicts)  │
         │  yarn upgrade-interactive                       Interactively bump           │
         │                                                 versions across workspaces   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] ⚠️ CI/CD pipelines should ALWAYS use
         yarn install --frozen-lockfile, never plain yarn install.
         Frozen-lockfile install fails loudly if package.json and
         yarn.lock have drifted — plain install would silently rewrite
         yarn.lock, hiding a real dependency problem until later.

  [UNDER THE HOOD]
       4.6 already states the key mechanism: each package's tsconfig.json
       extends the root and lists its dependencies under references, and
       TypeScript caches per-package compile state in .tsbuildinfo files.
       This is WHY yarn tsc gets faster after the first run and why
       deleting a stray .tsbuildinfo (or running yarn tsc --build --clean
       per 4.6's error table) is the standard fix for a "referenced
       project may not disable emit" error -- you are forcing TypeScript
       to rebuild its dependency graph from scratch instead of trusting a
       stale cache.

  [KEY TAKEAWAYS]
       - Real prerequisites are Node LTS (22 or 24) and Yarn 4.4.1 via
         Corepack -- not Node 18 or Yarn classic (4.1).
       - npx @backstage/create-app@latest scaffolds packages/app
         (frontend) and packages/backend (4.2, 4.3).
       - yarn dev = hot reload dev servers on :3000 (frontend) and :7007
         (backend) with SQLite (4.4).
       - yarn build + yarn start = compiled, bundled production serving
         on one port, :7007 (4.4).
       - yarn dev transpiles WITHOUT type-checking; only yarn tsc / yarn
         build catch every type error (4.6).
       - A fresh app is a Yarn workspaces monorepo -- ONE yarn.lock, ONE
         hoisted node_modules (4.7).
       - CI must use yarn install --frozen-lockfile, never plain yarn
         install (4.7).

  [EXAM GOTCHAS]
       - Believing yarn dev "type-checks" your code -- it does not; only
         yarn tsc / yarn build do (4.6).
       - Hand-editing or deleting yarn.lock to "fix" a dependency problem
         -- it silently changes every resolved version (4.7).
       - Running plain yarn install in CI instead of --frozen-lockfile,
         hiding a real package.json/yarn.lock drift (4.7).
       - Forgetting that adding a dependency to ONE workspace still
         updates the ROOT yarn.lock -- that's expected (4.7).

  [SPEED TIPS]
       - Use yarn workspace app tsc --noEmit while iterating on one
         package -- far faster than a full yarn tsc (4.6).
       - Use yarn why <package> the moment you see a version conflict,
         before touching yarn.lock by hand (4.7).

  [COMMANDS / CONFIG TO REMEMBER]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ COMMANDS TO REMEMBER — Chapter 4                                             │
         │                                                                              │
         │ npx @backstage/create-app@latest     # scaffold a brand-new app (4.2)        │
         │ cd easypj-portal && yarn install     # enter it, install deps (4.2, 4.7)     │
         │ yarn dev                              # hot-reload dev :3000 + :7007 (4.4)   │
         │ yarn build && yarn start              # compile + serve prod on :7007 (4.4)  │
         │ yarn tsc                              # type-check the whole monorepo (4.6)  │
         │ yarn workspace app tsc --noEmit       # type-check ONE package, faster (4.6) │
         │ yarn workspace app add <pkg>          # add a dep to one workspace (4.7)     │
         │ yarn install --frozen-lockfile        # CI-safe install, never plain (4.7)   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PRACTICE DRILL -- 15 minutes]
       - From a clean machine, run npx @backstage/create-app@latest and
         get yarn dev serving at :3000 in under 10 minutes.
       - Deliberately introduce a type error; confirm yarn dev still runs
         but yarn tsc fails.
       - Add one dependency to packages/app with yarn workspace app add
         and confirm yarn.lock changed at the root.

    4.8  CBA Exam: Chapter 4 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Setup, directory structure, create-app, configuration, TypeScript,
           and Yarn dependency management

  [VISUAL SUMMARY -- Chapter 4]
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ CHAPTER 4 IN ONE PICTURE — from npx to a running localhost:3000             │
  │                                                                             │
  │   npx create-app (4.2)  ──▶  packages/app + packages/backend (4.3)          │
  │         │                         │                                         │
  │         ▼                         ▼                                         │
  │   yarn install (4.7)  ──▶  yarn dev (4.4)  ──▶  yarn tsc / yarn build (4.6) │
  │   (ONE yarn.lock,             hot reload,          type-check or bundle     │
  │    hoisted node_modules)      SQLite dev.db          for production         │

  │   RESULT: easypj-portal running at localhost:3000, themed (4.5), ready      │

  │      whose frontend and backend you run with yarn dev day-to-day, and       │
  │      which yarn tsc / yarn build turn into a verified production bundle.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: Chapter 4 turns an empty folder into a running, themed
  easypj-portal. npx create-app (4.2) scaffolds the Yarn workspaces
  monorepo (4.7) described in 4.3; yarn dev (4.4) serves it with hot
  reload while yarn tsc and yarn build (4.6) are the two commands that
  actually verify and package it for production. By the final line of
  this chapter, localhost:3000 is real -- Chapter 5 teaches you to run
  and configure it like a professional.

  [WHY THIS MATTERS]

       like a professional: knowing every CLI command's job, where each
       config value should live, and how to keep real secrets out of Git
       entirely before you ever point this at production easypj data.

  [OBJECTIVES] After this chapter you can:

         backstage-cli create-plugin/migrate) and when to run it (5.1)

         catalog, scaffolder, techdocs, auth, proxy and integrations
         sections (5.2)

         placeholder syntax (5.3)

         never in app-config.yaml itself (5.3)

         and .local.yaml disagree (5.4)

  [SECTION MAP -- you are here]
