Chapter 12: Template Actions & Customization 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~55min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
    Chapter 11 showed the 3-action skeleton (fetch → publish → register) that
    covers most easypj templates. But real organizations eventually need
    something the built-ins don't do — post to Slack when a service is
    created, call an internal ticketing API, enforce an org-specific naming
    check. Chapter 12 goes one level deeper: the complete built-in action
    catalog, the skeleton/ directory that fetch:template actually copies, and
    how to write and debug your own custom action when nothing built-in fits.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ list the complete built-in scaffolder action catalog (fetch:*,
       publish:*, catalog:*, fs:delete, roadiehq:utils:fs:replace) and each
       one's key inputs/outputs;
    ✅ trace how a step's output becomes available to a later step via
       ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }};
    ✅ read a template's spec.output.links/text and explain what a user
       actually sees after a scaffolder run finishes;
    ✅ describe the skeleton/ directory layout and how {{ values.X }}
       placeholders get replaced during fetch:template;
    ✅ write a minimal custom action with createTemplateAction, including its
       input schema and handler;
    ✅ decide when a custom action is actually needed versus when a built-in
       action already covers the job;
    ✅ run a template locally and diagnose the 3 most common template errors.

    CBA RELEVANCE: Template Actions still lands in the Customizing domain
    (🎯 32%) — expect exam tasks that ask you to add or fix a step, wire an
    output reference, or diagnose why a placeholder was never replaced.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    12.1 Built-in Actions (Complete Reference) → 12.2 The Template Skeleton
    (./template Directory) → 12.3 Custom Template Actions → 12.4 Template
    Testing & Debugging → 12.5 CBA Exam: Chapter 12 Practice Questions.

    12.1 Built-in Actions — Complete Reference
         - fetch:template — Template from local directory
         - fetch:plain — Copy files from URL
         - fetch:cookiecutter — Cookiecutter templates
         - publish:github — Create GitHub repo
         - publish:gitlab — Create GitLab repo
         - publish:bitbucket — Create Bitbucket repo
         - publish:azure — Create Azure DevOps repo
         - catalog:register — Register entity
         - catalog:write — Write catalog entity
         - fs:delete — Delete files during template run
         - roadiehq:utils:fs:replace — Find and replace
         - debug:wait — Pause for N seconds (testing/demos only)
         [ALL BUILT-IN ACTIONS — Inputs, Outputs, Examples]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ACTION                 KEY INPUTS              OUTPUT                                         │
         │  ---------------------  ----------------------  ----------------------                         │
         │  fetch:template          url, values              (files written to disk)                      │
         │  fetch:plain              url, targetPath           (files written to disk)                    │
         │  fetch:cookiecutter        url, values, extract       (files written to disk)                  │
         │  publish:github              repoUrl, defaultBranch    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl              │
         │  publish:gitlab                repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl           │
         │  publish:bitbucket                repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl        │
         │  publish:azure                       repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl     │
         │  catalog:register                       repoContentsUrl,          entityRef, catalogInfoUrl    │
         │                                        catalogInfoPath                                         │
         │  catalog:write                            entity                     (writes YAML to disk)     │
         │  fs:delete                                  files                      (removes files)         │
         │  roadiehq:utils:fs:replace                   files, replace             (edits file contents)  │
         │  debug:wait                                   seconds                    (pauses the task)     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - How actions actually chain together at run time
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SCAFFOLDER ACTION EXECUTION FLOW — django-service Template                      │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  Developer fills out the form (name=jobpost-worker, owner=team-platform)         │
         │                              │                                                   │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "fetch"    action: fetch:template                                          │
         │                  reads ./skeleton, replaces {{ values.name }} -> jobpost-worker  │
         │                  OUTPUT: files written to a temp workspace                       │
         │                              │  (workspace path passed to the next step)         │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "publish"  action: publish:github                                          │
         │                  pushes the temp workspace to a new GitHub repo                  │
         │                  OUTPUT: repoContentsUrl, remoteUrl                              │
         │                              │  (repoContentsUrl passed to the next step)        │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "register" action: catalog:register                                        │
         │                  reads catalog-info.yaml from repoContentsUrl, registers it      │
         │                  OUTPUT: entityRef, catalogInfoUrl                               │
         │                              │                                                   │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  Scaffolder UI shows: "Open in Catalog" -> jobpost-worker Component page         │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  KEY RULE: each step's OUTPUT becomes available to every later step as           │
         │  {{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — this is how publish knows nothing about       │
         │  fetch, yet register can still reach back to what publish produced.              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - spec.output — what the user sees after the run finishes
         [TEMPLATE OUTPUT — Links & Text Shown to the User]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    steps: [...]                                              │
         │    output:                                                   │
         │      links:                                                  │
         │        - title: Open in catalog                              │
         │          url: ${{ steps.register.output.entityRef }}         │
         │        - title: Repository                                   │
         │          url: ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}          │
         │      text:                                                   │
         │        - title: Next Steps                                   │
         │          content: |                                          │
         │            Your service is live at                           │
         │            ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}             │
         │                                                              │
         │  RULE: output.links/output.text is what renders on the       │
         │  scaffolder "Task" completion page — without it the user     │
         │  only sees raw step logs, no clickable link to their repo.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.2 The Template Skeleton — ./skeleton Directory
         - Directory structure for template files
         - Using ${{ values.name }} for placeholders
         - Template file naming conventions
         - Including catalog-info.yaml in the template
         - .github/workflows for CI/CD generation
         [TEMPLATE SKELETON — Directory Structure]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  templates/django-service/                                                │
         │  |-- template.yaml              <- the Template entity itself             │
         │  |-- skeleton/                  <- copied + placeholder-replaced by       │
         │      |-- catalog-info.yaml       <- fetch:template                        │
         │      |     (name: ${{ values.name }})                                     │
         │      |-- Dockerfile                                                       │
         │      |-- requirements.txt                                                 │
         │      |-- manage.py                                                        │
         │      |-- .github/workflows/ci.yml                                         │
         │      |-- k8s/                                                             │
         │          |-- deployment.yaml                                              │
         │          |-- service.yaml                                                 │
         │                                                                           │
         │  RULE: every file inside skeleton/ can use {{ values.X }}                 │
         │  placeholders — fetch:template replaces them with the user's form input.  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.3 Custom Template Actions
         - Creating a custom action with createTemplateAction
         - Input schema and validation
         - Output schema
         - Error handling
         [CUSTOM TEMPLATE ACTION — Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/scaffolder-backend-module-easypj/src/actions/notifySlack.ts     │
         │  import { createTemplateAction } from '@backstage/plugin-scaffolder-node';  │
         │                                                                             │
         │  export const createNotifySlackAction = () =>                               │
         │    createTemplateAction<{ channel: string; message: string }>({             │
         │      id: 'easypj:notify-slack',                                             │
         │      schema: {                                                              │
         │        input: {                                                             │
         │          required: ['channel', 'message'],                                  │
         │          properties: {                                                      │
         │            channel: { type: 'string' },                                     │
         │            message: { type: 'string' },                                     │
         │          },                                                                 │
         │        },                                                                   │
         │      },                                                                     │
         │      async handler(ctx) {                                                   │
         │        ctx.logger.info('Notifying channel: ' + ctx.input.channel);          │
         │        // call your Slack webhook here                                      │
         │        ctx.output('notified', true);                                        │
         │      },                                                                     │
         │    });                                                                      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Deciding whether you even need a custom action
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IF YOU NEED TO...                              REACH FOR...                           │
         │  ----------------------------------------------  --------------------------------      │
         │  Copy files, replace {{ values.X }} placeholders   Built-in fetch:template (12.1)      │
         │  Create a repo on GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket/Azure     Built-in publish:* (12.1)          │
         │  Register the result in the Catalog                  Built-in catalog:register (12.1)  │
         │  Notify a channel, call an internal API,               Write a custom action — no      │
         │   ping a ticketing system, run a org-specific check     built-in covers this (12.3)    │
         │                                                                                        │
         │  RULE OF THUMB: reach for a custom action only when the built-in catalog               │
         │  (12.1) has no action for what you need — most templates need zero custom code.        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.4 Template Testing & Debugging
         - Running templates locally
         - Debug mode: yarn dev with template debugging
         - Common template errors and fixes
         - Testing template output
         [TEMPLATE DEBUGGING — Commands and Common Errors]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Validate template.yaml syntax before running it                         │
         │  yarn backstage-cli repo schema openapi validate ./template.yaml           │
         │                                                                            │
         │  # Run locally with debug logging                                          │
         │  BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug yarn dev                                        │
         │                                                                            │
         │  # Common errors:                                                          │
         │  'Input required property owner' -> a parameter marked required: [owner]   │
         │                                     wasn't filled in the form              │
         │  'Template not found'             -> wrong templateRef, or not registered  │
         │                                     in catalog.locations (with rules:      │
         │                                     [{allow: [Template]}])                 │
         │  Placeholder left unreplaced       -> typo in {{ values.X }},              │
         │  (literal {{ }} visible in          double-check the parameter's exact     │
         │  the output)                         property name                         │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
    Every action registered with the scaffolder backend is, under the hood,
    just a function that reads ctx.input, does its work, and calls
    ctx.output(name, value) to publish results. The scaffolder engine strings
    these functions together in the exact order listed under spec.steps,
    passing a shared workspace directory between them — the same temp
    workspace fetch:template writes into and publish:github later reads from.
    Because every action shares this one interface, a custom action like
    easypj:notify-slack (12.3) can sit in the same steps: list as a built-in
    action like publish:github with zero special-casing by the engine.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
     🔑 The built-in action catalog covers fetching (fetch:template,
        fetch:plain, fetch:cookiecutter), publishing (publish:github/gitlab/
        bitbucket/azure), and catalog registration (catalog:register,
        catalog:write), plus small file utilities (fs:delete,
        roadiehq:utils:fs:replace).
     🔑 Every action's OUTPUT is addressable by later steps as
        ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — the wiring that lets register
        reach back to what publish produced without either action
        referencing the other by name.
     🔑 The skeleton/ directory is what fetch:template actually copies; every
        file inside it can use {{ values.X }} placeholders replaced with the
        user's form input.
     🔑 spec.output.links/text is what renders on the scaffolder Task
        completion page — skip it and the user sees only raw step logs, no
        clickable "Open in Catalog" link.
     🔑 A custom action is built with createTemplateAction, declares an input
        schema, and calls ctx.output(...) to hand results to later steps —
        the same contract as any built-in action.
     🔑 Rule of thumb: reach for a custom action only when the built-in
        catalog has no action for the job — most templates need zero custom
        code.
     🔑 The 3 most common template failures: a missing required parameter, a
        wrong/unregistered templateRef, or a typo'd {{ values.X }}
        placeholder left literally in the output.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
     ⚠️ Confusing catalog:write with catalog:register — catalog:write only
        writes a YAML file to disk, it does NOT register anything in the
        Catalog.
     ⚠️ Assuming a custom action needs to reinvent file-copying — most custom
        actions should call an external system (Slack, ticketing, a check)
        and let the built-ins handle files/repos/catalog.
     ⚠️ Not marking a custom action's input properties as `required` — an
        unvalidated ctx.input.X can be undefined at runtime and crash the
        handler.
     ⚠️ Missing rules: [{allow: [Template]}] on the catalog.locations entry
        that points at a template — a template not marked as such won't run.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
     ⚡ Validate template.yaml syntax before running it (see cheat-card)
        instead of debugging blind inside the Create wizard.
     ⚡ Run with BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug when a step's output doesn't seem
        to flow into the next step — the debug log shows the resolved
        {{ steps.* }} values.
     ⚡ Check the "IF YOU NEED TO... REACH FOR..." decision table (12.3)
        before writing any custom action.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 12 — COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER                                    │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Validate template.yaml syntax before running it                           │
         │  $ yarn backstage-cli repo schema openapi validate ./template.yaml           │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Run locally with debug logging (see resolved steps.*.output values)       │
         │  $ BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug yarn dev                                        │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Reading step output in a LATER step:                                      │
         │    ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }}                                          │
         │                                                                              │
         │  Common errors -> fix:                                                       │
         │    'Input required property owner'  -> required: [owner] not filled in form  │
         │    'Template not found'              -> not registered in catalog.locations  │
         │    literal {{ }} left in output      -> typo in the {{ values.X }} name      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         .vs-prose — In words: 12.1 gave the complete built-in action catalog —
         the tools that cover most templates. 12.2 showed the skeleton/
         directory those fetch actions actually copy, and how {{ values.X }}
         placeholders inside it get replaced. When neither is enough, 12.3
         showed how to write a custom action with the exact same ctx.input →
         handler → ctx.output(...) contract as any built-in. Finally 12.4
         closed the loop: validate the template's schema and run with debug
         logging to catch the 3 most common failures before they reach a user.


Chapter 13: Building Templates for easypj 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~40min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
    Chapters 11-12 taught the theory of Software Templates in the abstract.
    Chapter 13 makes it concrete: the exact 4 templates the easypj platform
    team maintains today — a Django microservice generator, a React frontend
    generator, a DB migration job generator, and a Terraform infrastructure
    generator — each a complete, copy-ready template.yaml you can register
    for easypj as-is.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ name all 4 easypj templates and what each one scaffolds (service,
       website, service, resource);
    ✅ read a complete production template.yaml end to end (metadata,
       parameters, steps) for each of the 4 templates;
    ✅ recognize the shared 3-step shape (fetch → publish:github →
       catalog:register) common to all 4 templates;
    ✅ identify what differs between templates (skeleton contents, type,
       owner) versus what stays identical (the step wiring);
    ✅ register all 4 templates into the Catalog via catalog.locations
       entries in app-config.yaml;
    ✅ explain how tags and categories surface recommended vs optional
       templates in the Create UI.

    CBA RELEVANCE: Chapter 13 is where the Customizing domain (🎯 32%) gets
    tested end-to-end — expect exam scenarios that hand you a broken or
    partial template.yaml matching one of these 4 patterns and ask you to
    complete or fix it.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    13.1 Template 1: Django Microservice → 13.2 Template 2: React Frontend →
    13.3 Template 3: Database Migration Job → 13.4 Template 4: Infrastructure
    as Code → 13.5 Registering Templates in the Catalog → 13.6 CBA Exam:
    Chapter 13 Practice Questions.

         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  THE 4 easypj TEMPLATES — AT A GLANCE                                                   │
         │                                                                                         │
         │  TEMPLATE                  TYPE      OWNER          SCAFFOLDS                           │
         │  -------------------------  --------  -------------  --------------------------------   │
         │  django-service (13.1)       service   team-platform   New Django backend microservice  │
         │  react-frontend (13.2)        website   team-fe          New React frontend app         │
         │  db-migration-job (13.3)       service   team-platform     K8s Job for a one-off DB     │
         │                                                             migration                   │
         │  infra-as-code (13.4)           resource  team-platform      Terraform for new cloud    │
         │                                                               infrastructure            │
         │                                                                                         │
         │  All 4 share the same 3-step shape: fetch -> publish:github -> catalog:register         │
         │  (see the 12.1 execution-flow diagram) - only the skeleton/ contents differ.            │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.1 Template 1: New Django Microservice
         - Purpose: Scaffold a new Django app for the easypj ecosystem
         - template.yaml: Complete YAML
         - Skeleton: Django boilerplate with best practices
         - Parameters: Service name, owner, description, Python version
         - Output: GitHub repo + registered in Catalog
         [TEMPLATE 1 — Django Microservice — Complete template.yaml]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3                                     │
         │  kind: Template                                                                  │
         │  metadata:                                                                       │
         │    name: django-service                                                          │
         │    title: New Django Microservice                                                │
         │    tags: [django, python, recommended]                                           │
         │  spec:                                                                           │
         │    owner: team-platform                                                          │
         │    type: service                                                                 │
         │    parameters:                                                                   │
         │      - title: Service Details                                                    │
         │        required: [name, owner]                                                   │
         │        properties:                                                               │
         │          name: { type: string, pattern: "^[a-z0-9-]+$" }                         │
         │          owner: { type: string, ui:field: OwnerPicker }                          │
         │          pythonVersion: { type: string, enum: ['3.11','3.12'] }                  │
         │    steps:                                                                        │
         │      - id: fetch                                                                 │
         │        action: fetch:template                                                    │
         │        input: { url: ./skeleton, values: { name: ${{ parameters.name }} } }      │
         │      - id: publish                                                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                                    │
         │        input: { repoUrl: "github.com?owner=easypj&repo=${{ parameters.name }}" } │
         │      - id: register                                                              │
         │        action: catalog:register                                                  │
         │        input: { repoContentsUrl: "${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}" } │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.5 Registering Templates in the Catalog
         - How templates appear in Backstage UI
         - Template categories and tags
         - Recommended vs optional templates
         - Template lifecycle management
         [REGISTERING ALL 4 TEMPLATES — app-config.yaml]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  catalog:                                                  │
         │    locations:                                              │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/django-service/template.yaml    │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/react-frontend/template.yaml    │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/db-migration-job/template.yaml  │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/infra-as-code/template.yaml     │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
    All 4 easypj templates are registered the same way every other Template
    entity is: a type: file catalog.locations entry pointing at each
    template.yaml, gated by rules: [{allow: [Template]}] (13.5). Under the
    hood this means the Catalog's ingestion pipeline (Part 3, 7.2) treats a
    Template exactly like it treats a Component or a System — an entity
    provider reads the location, a processor validates and stores it, and the
    template becomes queryable/searchable like any other catalog entity. That
    is also why a broken template.yaml (bad YAML, missing required fields)
    fails the same way a broken catalog-info.yaml would: it simply won't
    appear in the Catalog until the syntax is fixed.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
     🔑 easypj maintains exactly 4 templates today: django-service (service),
        react-frontend (website), db-migration-job (service), infra-as-code
        (resource).
     🔑 All 4 share the identical 3-step shape: fetch → publish:github →
        catalog:register — only the skeleton/ contents and parameters differ.
     🔑 django-service and db-migration-job are both owned by team-platform
        and typed service, despite scaffolding very different things (a
        Django app versus a one-off K8s Job).
     🔑 react-frontend is the only one of the 4 typed website and owned by
        team-fe.
     🔑 infra-as-code is the only one typed resource — it scaffolds Terraform
        (main.tf/variables.tf/outputs.tf/backend.tf), not application code.
     🔑 All 4 templates are registered as type: file catalog.locations
        entries in app-config.yaml, each with rules: [{allow: [Template]}].
     🔑 Templates, like every catalog entity, become visible in the
        Backstage Create wizard as soon as their location is registered — no
        extra deploy step beyond a config reload.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
     ⚠️ Assuming every template must be type: service — infra-as-code is
        type: resource and react-frontend is type: website; spec.type is a
        free classification, not fixed to the action steps.
     ⚠️ Copy-pasting one template's owner (e.g. team-platform) into another
        without checking who actually owns it (react-frontend is team-fe).
     ⚠️ Forgetting the rules: [{allow: [Template]}] restriction on each
        catalog.locations entry — without it Backstage may misinterpret the
        file as a different kind.
     ⚠️ Missing a required parameter (e.g. targetService on db-migration-job,
        cloudProvider on infra-as-code) so the generated skeleton has no way
        to know what it's targeting.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
     ⚡ Start any new easypj template from whichever of the 4 existing ones
        is structurally closest (same type/owner) and only change the
        skeleton/ and parameters.
     ⚡ Keep the fetch → publish:github → catalog:register step IDs and shape
        identical across templates — consistency here makes slotting in a
        custom action (12.3) easy without re-learning the wiring.
     ⚡ Register a new template with a single extra catalog.locations entry —
        no code change to Backstage itself is required.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 13 — COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER                                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  THE 4 easypj TEMPLATES (name / type / owner):                           │
         │    django-service     service   team-platform                            │

         │    db-migration-job   service   team-platform                            │

         │                                                                          │
         │  # Generic registration pattern (app-config.yaml), repeat per template:  │
         │  catalog:                                                                │
         │    locations:                                                            │
         │      - type: file                                                        │
         │        target: ./templates/<name>/template.yaml                          │

        templates/notification-service/template.yaml.

        unique to it (not shared by the others).

         template.yaml files — a Django microservice, a React frontend, a
         database migration job, and a Terraform infrastructure module —
         each with its own type, owner, and parameters. Despite scaffolding
         very different things, all 4 run the identical fetch → publish:github
         → catalog:register step sequence from Chapters 11-12. 13.5 then
         showed how all 4 get switched on at once: one catalog.locations
         entry per template, each gated by rules: [{allow: [Template]}], in
         the same app-config.yaml that registers every other easypj entity.


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
  🔑 PART 4 — MUST-REMEMBER
   • A Software Template = template.yaml (metadata + spec) + a skeleton/
     directory of files with {{ values.X }} placeholders + a sequence of steps.
   • The near-universal 3-step shape is fetch → publish → register: copy the
     skeleton, push it to GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket/Azure, register the result
     in the Catalog — only the skeleton/ contents differ between templates.
   • Built-in actions (fetch:*, publish:*, catalog:*, fs:delete,
     roadiehq:utils:fs:replace) cover the vast majority of templates; write a
     custom action with createTemplateAction only when nothing built-in fits.
   • Every step's OUTPUT becomes available to every LATER step as
     ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — that is how register can reach back to
     what publish produced without either action knowing the other exists.
   • spec.output.links/text controls what the user actually sees on the
     scaffolder "Task" completion page — skip it and they see only raw logs.
   • easypj ships exactly 4 templates today — django-service (service),
     react-frontend (website), db-migration-job (service), infra-as-code
     (resource) — each registered as a type: file catalog.locations entry
     with rules: [{allow: [Template]}] in app-config.yaml.
   • Templates are registered in the Catalog exactly like any other entity —
     which is why Part 3 (the Catalog) had to come before Part 4 (Templates).

  [PART 4 VISUAL SUMMARY — From blank form to 4 live easypj templates]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PART 4 VISUAL SUMMARY — From blank form to 4 live easypj templates  │
  │                                                                      │
  │    👤 Dev clicks CREATE ──▶ picks one of 4 templates ──▶ fills form  │
  │             (Ch11: template.yaml + parameters define this form)      │
  │                                                                      │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼                                       │
  │   ⚙️  STEPS RUN IN ORDER (Ch12: built-in + custom actions)           │
  │     fetch:template ──▶ [optional custom action] ──▶ publish:github   │
  │     ──▶ catalog:register                                             │
  │                                                                      │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼                                       │
  │   📦 NEW ENTITY LIVE IN CATALOG (Ch13: 1 of the 4 real templates)    │
  │     django-service · react-frontend · db-migration-job ·             │
  │     infra-as-code                                                    │
  │                                                                      │
  │   ⏱️  ~2 minutes, end to end — versus ~2 hours scaffolding by hand   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  .vs-prose — In words: a developer clicks Create, picks one of easypj's 4
  templates, and fills in a form generated straight from that template's
  parameters (Chapter 11). Backstage then runs the template's steps in order —
  almost always fetch:template to copy the skeleton, optionally a custom
  action for anything org-specific, then publish:github and catalog:register
  (Chapter 12). Seconds later the new service — a Django microservice, a React
  frontend, a DB migration job, or a Terraform module (Chapter 13) — is a live,
  searchable entity in the same Software Catalog Part 3 introduced. What took
  a developer roughly 2 hours by hand now takes about 2 minutes, end to end.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────


PART 5: TECHDOCS — DOCUMENTATION-AS-CODE                                  🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 5 INTRO]
  Parts 1-4 gave jobpost a live Catalog entity and four working Software
  Templates that scaffold brand-new services end to end — but neither one
  tells a new developer HOW jobpost actually works, how to call its API, or
  how to deploy it. That is what documentation is for, and in most orgs that
  documentation lives in a separate wiki that drifts out of date the moment
  the code changes. Part 5 fixes that by keeping the docs in the SAME repo,
  the SAME pull request, and the SAME Markdown files as the code: TechDocs,
  Backstage's documentation-as-code system. Ch14 explains the pipeline that
  turns a docs/ folder into a rendered site on jobpost's entity page; Ch15
  uses that pipeline to actually plan, write, and publish jobpost's real
  six-file documentation set.

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of TechDocs like assembly instructions printed inside a
  product's own box, instead of a separate manual you have to look up online
  that goes stale the moment the product changes. Ch14 is the factory line
  that folds those instructions into the box — Markdown in the repo, run
  through MkDocs, published as a static site, rendered by Backstage's
  TechDocs reader (Generator -> Publisher -> Reader). Ch15 is you actually
  writing the instructions for jobpost's box: architecture, API, deployment,
  contributing — so the docs ship, and update, with the product itself.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 5 map]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ch14 CONCEPTS ────────────────────▶ Ch15 BUILD IT                   │
  │   the TechDocs pipeline (Generator /    plan + write jobpost's 6     │
  │   Publisher / Reader), mkdocs.yml,        real docs, publish them,   │
  │   app-config.yaml storage publishers        verify on entity page    │
  │          │                                          │                │
  │          └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘                │
  │                                ▼                                     │
  │          jobpost entity page Docs tab, rebuilding on every git push  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Read the map as a sentence: first learn what TechDocs IS and how the
  Generator/Publisher/Reader pipeline turns Markdown into a rendered site
  (Ch14), then use that exact pipeline to plan and write jobpost's own six
  real docs and publish them behind one catalog-info.yaml annotation (Ch15).
  Ch14 is the machinery; Ch15 is you operating it on a real project.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🧭 DECISION GUIDE — where do I start in Part 5?                       │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────           │
  │  If you...                             Then...                         │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────           │
  │  have never used TechDocs              start at Ch14, read 14.1-14.3   │

  │                                           Takeaways + Commands card    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 5 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part jobpost will have a complete Documentation-as-Code
  setup: an mkdocs.yml at the repo root, six real docs (index, architecture,
  api-reference, getting-started, deployment, contributing) under /docs, a
  backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. annotation wired into catalog-info.yaml,
  and a working local preview via `npx @techdocs/cli serve` — with the
  published site rebuilding automatically on every push to Git.

Chapter 14: TechDocs — Documentation That Lives With Your Code 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~40min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         jobpost's entity page can show its README, its architecture, its
         API — but only if that documentation exists somewhere Backstage
         can render it. Wikis rot; a docs/ folder that ships in the same
         repo, and the same pull request, as the code it describes does
         not. Before Ch15 has you write jobpost's real docs, you need to
         know what TechDocs actually is and how Markdown becomes a
         rendered page on the entity screen.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ define documentation-as-code and explain why TechDocs docs live
            in the same repo as the code they describe
         ✅ name the four TechDocs architecture pieces (Generator,
            Publisher, techdocs-backend, Reader) and what each one does
         ✅ distinguish the 'Basic' (local, on-server) deployment model from
            the 'Recommended' (CI/CD-built, cloud-stored) model
         ✅ configure techdocs.builder/generator/publisher in
            app-config.yaml for both Dev and Production
         ✅ choose a Publisher storage backend (local, googleGcs, awsS3,
            azureBlobStorage, openStackSwift) and justify the choice
         ✅ apply TechDocs writing best practices (admonitions, Mermaid,
            clear nav) instead of the anti-patterns they replace

    CBA RELEVANCE: TechDocs configuration is graded under Customizing
         (32%), the single largest CBA domain — questions on the pipeline,
         the app-config.yaml keys, and storage choice all assume this
         chapter's model.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         14.1 What are TechDocs? -> 14.2 Setting Up TechDocs ->
         14.3 TechDocs Configuration in app-config.yaml ->
         14.4 Writing Effective TechDocs -> 14.5 CBA Exam Practice.

    14.1 What are TechDocs?
         - Documentation-as-code: Docs live in your repo, next to code
         - Written in Markdown (.md) or MDX (.mdx)
         - Auto-published when you push to Git
         - Rendered beautifully in Backstage
         - Powered by MkDocs under the hood
         [DIAGRAM: Code + Docs together in Git → TechDocs → Backstage]

         [TECHDOCS PIPELINE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  📖 TECHDOCS — Documentation-as-Code Pipeline                │
         │                                                              │
         │  📝 Developer writes docs in Markdown                         │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  📦 Docs live in same repo as code                           │
         │     /docs/                                                   │
         │       index.md                                               │
         │       architecture.md                                        │
         │       api-reference.md                                       │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  🚀 Git push triggers TechDocs build                         │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  ⚙️ TechDocs Generator (MkDocs)                              │
         │     • Converts Markdown → Static HTML                        │
         │     • Applies MkDocs theme                                   │
         │     • Generates navigation                                   │
         │     • Creates search index                                   │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  📤 TechDocs Publisher                                       │
         │     • Publishes to storage (local, GCS, S3, Azure)           │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  🖥️ Backstage TechDocs Reader                                 │
         │     • Displays docs on Entity page                           │
         │     • Full-text search                                       │
         │     • Navigation between docs                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TECHDOCS ARCHITECTURE — Official Component Names]
         - Generator: runs MkDocs to convert Markdown into static HTML/CSS/JS, stripping the JS afterward for security before handing the output to the Publisher
         - Publisher: stores the generated static files — local filesystem in a Basic setup, or cloud storage (AWS S3, GCS, Azure Blob Storage) in a Recommended/production setup
         - techdocs-backend plugin: the single backend intermediary that retrieves generated docs from storage and serves them, centralizing security handling across every storage type
         - Reader: the frontend piece that requests docs from techdocs-backend using the entity ID and renders them on the entity page
         - Backstage names these two deployment models 'Basic' (docs generated on-demand on the Backstage server itself, stored locally) and 'Recommended' (docs generated in CI/CD, stored in cloud storage, served on-demand by techdocs-backend)

    14.2 Setting Up TechDocs
         - mkdocs.yml configuration
         - docs/ directory structure
         - Markdown best practices for TechDocs
         - Adding diagrams (Mermaid, PlantUML)
         - Adding code snippets with syntax highlighting
         [CODE BLOCK: Complete mkdocs.yml example]

         [mkdocs.yml — TechDocs Configuration]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # mkdocs.yml                                                │
         │  site_name: 'jobpost Documentation'                          │
         │  site_description: 'Job posting application docs'            │
         │                                                              │
         │  nav:                                                        │
         │    - Home: index.md                                          │
         │    - Architecture: architecture.md                           │
         │    - 'API Reference': api-reference.md                       │
         │    - 'Getting Started': getting-started.md                   │
         │    - Deployment: deployment.md                               │
         │    - Contributing: contributing.md                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  plugins:                                                    │
         │    - techdocs-core                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  markdown_extensions:                                        │
         │    - admonition                                              │
         │    - codehilite                                              │
         │    - pymdownx.superfences:                                   │
         │        custom_fences:                                        │
         │          - name: mermaid                                     │
         │            class: mermaid                                    │
         │            format: !!python/name:pymdownx.superfences.       │
         │                    fence_div_format                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  docs/ DIRECTORY ANATOMY — Generic MkDocs Project Layout                     │
         │                                                                              │
         │  jobpost/                     (repo root)                                    │
         │    mkdocs.yml                  <- nav + plugins config (shown above)         │
         │    docs/                                                                     │
         │      index.md                   <- required, the home page                   │
         │      architecture.md                                                         │
         │      api-reference.md                                                        │
         │      assets/                      <- images referenced by ![](assets/x.png)  │
         │      overrides/                     <- optional, custom theme overrides      │
         │                                                                              │
         │  Only docs/ + mkdocs.yml are required - assets/ and overrides/ are opt-in.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.3 TechDocs Configuration in app-config.yaml
         - techdocs.builder: local | external
         - techdocs.generator: runIn (local | docker)

         - techdocs.generator.dockerImage: overrides the default MkDocs Docker image used when runIn: docker
         - techdocs.generator.pullImage: boolean controlling whether a fresh copy of the Docker image is pulled before each build
         - techdocs.generator.mkdocs.omitTechdocsCorePlugin: boolean (default false) — skips auto-injecting the techdocs-core plugin into mkdocs.yml
         - techdocs.generator.mkdocs.legacyCopyReadmeMdToIndexMd: boolean fallback that copies README.md to index.md when docs/index.md is missing
         - techdocs.generator.mkdocs.disableExternalFonts: boolean for air-gapped networks that can't reach external font CDNs
         - techdocs.generator.mkdocs.defaultPlugins: array of MkDocs plugin names injected by default
         - techdocs.publisher: local | googleGcs | awsS3 | azureBlobStorage | openStackSwift
         - Storage configuration for production
         [TECHDOCS SECTION — app-config.yaml, Dev vs Production]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Development                                                  │
         │  techdocs:                                                      │
         │    builder: local                                               │
         │    generator: { runIn: local }                                  │
         │    publisher: { type: local }                                   │
         │                                                                 │
         │  # Production                                                   │
         │  techdocs:                                                      │
         │    builder: external          # CI/CD pre-builds docs           │
         │    generator: { runIn: docker } # isolated MkDocs container     │
         │    publisher:                                                   │
         │      type: awsS3                                                │
         │      awsS3: { bucketName: easypj-techdocs, region: us-east-1 }  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - googleGcs needs publisher.googleGcs.bucketName (required); optional: bucketRootPath, projectId, credentials
         - azureBlobStorage needs publisher.azureBlobStorage.containerName (required — note the field is containerName, not bucketName); optional: connectionString, credentials
         - awsS3 also accepts optional bucketRootPath, endpoint, s3ForcePathStyle, and sse ('aws:kms' or 'AES256') for server-side encryption

    14.4 Writing Effective TechDocs
         - Structure: index.md as entry point
         - Navigation: mkdocs.yml nav section
         - Admonitions: Notes, warnings, tips
         - Code blocks with line highlighting
         - Mermaid diagrams for architecture
         - Cross-references between docs
         [CODE BLOCK: Example TechDocs MD file with best practices]

         [TECHDOCS STORAGE STRATEGIES — Dev vs Production]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  💾 TECHDOCS PUBLISHER — Storage Options                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ PUBLISHER TYPE     DEV          PRODUCTION              ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ local              ✅ Default   ❌ Not scalable          ││
         │  │                    Stores in    Multiple pods can't     ││
         │  │                    ./techdocs-  share local storage     ││
         │  │                    output/                               ││
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ googleGcs          🟡 Config    ✅ Recommended           ││
         │  │ (Google Cloud      needed       Auto-scales, cheap,     ││
         │  │  Storage)                       CDN-ready               ││
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ awsS3              🟡 Config    ✅ Recommended           ││
         │  │ (AWS S3)           needed       Auto-scales, CDN via    ││
         │  │                                 CloudFront              ││
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ azureBlobStorage   🟡 Config    ✅ Recommended           ││
         │  │ (Azure Blob)       needed       Auto-scales, CDN-ready  ││
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ openStackSwift     🟡 Config    ✅ For OpenStack shops   ││
         │  │                    needed                                ││
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 For production: ALWAYS use cloud storage (GCS/S3/Azure) │
         │     for multi-pod Backstage deployments!                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TECHDOCS WRITING BEST PRACTICES — Do's and Don'ts]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ✍️ TECHDOCS WRITING GUIDE                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ DO:                          ❌ DON'T:                    │
         │  ─────────────────────────────   ──────────────────────────  │
         │  ✅ Keep docs next to code       ❌ Put docs in a separate   │
         │     (/docs/ in each repo)           wiki/Confluence          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Use Mermaid for diagrams     ❌ Use screenshots that     │
         │     (text-based, versionable)       go stale quickly         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Structure with clear nav     ❌ One giant README with    │
         │     (mkdocs.yml nav section)        everything               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Use admonitions for          ❌ Bury important warnings  │
         │     notes/warnings/tips             in paragraphs            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Include code snippets        ❌ Describe code without    │
         │     with syntax highlighting        showing it               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Write for NEW developers     ❌ Assume everyone knows    │
         │     (explain acronyms, context)     the context              │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Update docs WITH code        ❌ "I'll update docs later" │
         │     (same PR as code change)        (never happens)          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The [TECHDOCS ARCHITECTURE] list in 14.1 names four pieces that
         do the real work: the Generator runs MkDocs to turn Markdown into
         static HTML/CSS/JS, then strips the JS afterward for security
         before handing off to the Publisher, which stores those static
         files (locally in Basic, or in cloud storage in Recommended). The
         techdocs-backend plugin is the single backend intermediary that
         retrieves docs from whichever storage type was configured and
         serves them, so the Reader (14.1's frontend piece) never needs to
         know or care where the files actually live — it just asks
         techdocs-backend for an entity's docs by id.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         ✅ TechDocs = documentation-as-code: Markdown/MDX docs versioned in
            the same repo, same PR, as the code they describe.
         ✅ The pipeline is Generator (MkDocs build) -> Publisher (storage)
            -> techdocs-backend (retrieval) -> Reader (render on entity
            page).
         ✅ 'Basic' = docs built on the Backstage server itself, stored
            locally; 'Recommended' = docs built in CI/CD, stored in cloud
            storage, served on demand.
         ✅ mkdocs.yml needs a nav: list and plugins: [techdocs-core]; the
            docs/ directory needs at minimum an index.md.
         ✅ techdocs.publisher.type chooses the storage backend: local
            (Dev only), googleGcs, awsS3, azureBlobStorage, or
            openStackSwift (Production).
         ✅ Good TechDocs practice: docs next to code, Mermaid over stale
            screenshots, a clear mkdocs.yml nav, admonitions for
            warnings/tips, and updating docs in the SAME PR as the code.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Assuming techdocs.publisher: local scales to Production — it
            does not; multiple pods can't share local storage.
         ⚠️ Confusing the Generator (builds the static site) with the
            Publisher (stores it) — they are two distinct pipeline stages.
         ⚠️ Forgetting azureBlobStorage's field is containerName, not
            bucketName like googleGcs/awsS3.
         ⚠️ Assuming the Reader talks to storage directly — it always goes
            through techdocs-backend, regardless of storage type.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Default to techdocs.generator.runIn: docker in Production —
            it's the isolated, CI/CD-friendly path away from local.
         ⚡ Reach for legacyCopyReadmeMdToIndexMd only as a fallback; a real
            docs/index.md written for TechDocs beats an auto-copied README.
         ⚡ Use Mermaid diagrams over screenshots from the start — they're
            versionable text and never go stale.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CH14 CHEAT-CARD — TechDocs Configuration Keys                        │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────       │
         │  mkdocs.yml                        nav: / plugins: [techdocs-core]    │
         │  techdocs.builder                  local | external                   │
         │  techdocs.generator.runIn          local | docker                     │
         │  techdocs.generator.dockerImage    override default MkDocs image      │
         │  techdocs.generator.pullImage      pull fresh image before build      │
         │  techdocs.generator.mkdocs.        skip auto-injecting techdocs-core  │
         │    omitTechdocsCorePlugin                                             │
         │  techdocs.generator.mkdocs.        copy README.md -> index.md if      │
         │    legacyCopyReadmeMdToIndexMd       docs/index.md is missing         │
         │  techdocs.publisher.type           local | googleGcs | awsS3 |        │
         │                                       azureBlobStorage |              │
         │                                       openStackSwift                  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (10 min, do this now)

            (fill in Generator, Publisher, techdocs-backend, Reader).

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Ch14 taught the TechDocs pipeline in the abstract; this chapter
         is you running it end to end on jobpost. By the end you will have
         planned, written, and published jobpost's actual six-file doc
         set, and verified it renders on the real entity page — the same
         workflow you'll repeat for every other easypj service.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ plan a doc set (index, architecture, api-reference,
            getting-started, deployment, contributing) and order it in
            mkdocs.yml's nav: list
         ✅ write an architecture.md with a Mermaid graph TD diagram of
            jobpost's real components (Nginx, Django, PostgreSQL, Redis)
         ✅ write api-reference.md documenting a real endpoint's query
            params, response body, and auth requirement
         ✅ write deployment.md covering environment variables, kubectl
            apply, and kubectl rollout undo for rollback
         ✅ register jobpost's docs with the backstage.io/techdocs-ref
            annotation and verify them on the entity page's Docs tab
         ✅ preview docs locally with `npx @techdocs/cli serve` before
            pushing

    CBA RELEVANCE: applying TechDocs to a real service is graded under
         Customizing (32%) — the exam expects you to recognize a complete,
         correctly-published doc set, not just recite the pipeline.

    15.5 Publishing and Viewing TechDocs
         - Registering easypj in the Catalog with TechDocs annotation
         - Verifying TechDocs appear on the entity page
         - Updating docs: Push to Git → auto-rebuild
         [PUBLISHING TECHDOCS — Complete Workflow]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. Add annotation to catalog-info.yaml:                            │
         │     backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.                                │
         │  2. Create mkdocs.yml at repo root (nav + techdocs-core plugin)     │
         │  3. Write the 6 docs from 15.1 into /docs/                          │
         │  4. git push — TechDocs builds automatically                        │
         │  5. Visit the jobpost entity page → click the Docs tab              │
         │  6. Future doc updates: same PR as the code change → auto-rebuild   │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Local preview without a running Backstage instance: `npx @techdocs/cli serve` builds mkdocs.yml with MkDocs and serves the rendered site with live reload, so docs can be checked before pushing
         - `techdocs-cli` is the same official CLI package used by CI/CD pipelines to generate and publish docs straight to cloud storage under the 'external' builder model

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         15.1's [SITE MAP] shows the real dependency: mkdocs.yml's nav:
         list from Ch14.2 is not just navigation decoration, it is the
         ORDER Backstage renders the sidebar in, top to bottom — index.md
         first because it loads first, then architecture, api-reference,
         getting-started, deployment, and contributing, each cross-linked
         from the page that needs it. 15.5's publishing workflow closes
         the loop from Ch14: the backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
         annotation tells techdocs-backend where the docs source root is,
         a git push triggers the Generator to rebuild, and the same
         techdocs-cli package used for local preview is what CI/CD runs
         under the 'external' builder model.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         ✅ jobpost's real doc set is exactly 6 files: index, architecture,
            api-reference, getting-started, deployment, contributing.
         ✅ architecture.md uses a Mermaid graph TD diagram — versionable
            text, not a screenshot that goes stale.
         ✅ api-reference.md documents real endpoints: query params,
            example JSON response, and the auth requirement.
         ✅ deployment.md covers env vars (DATABASE_URL, DJANGO_SECRET_KEY),
            kubectl apply for deploy, and kubectl rollout undo for
            rollback.
         ✅ Publishing is ONE annotation — backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
            in catalog-info.yaml — plus a git push; no separate manual
            build step.
         ✅ `npx @techdocs/cli serve` previews the rendered site locally,
            with live reload, before anything is pushed.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Forgetting the mkdocs.yml nav: order IS the sidebar order —
            reordering nav: changes what users see first.
         ⚠️ Treating techdocs-ref as optional — without it, techdocs-backend
            has no dir: source root and the Docs tab stays empty.
         ⚠️ Assuming docs need a separate deploy step — they rebuild
            automatically from the same git push as the code change.
         ⚠️ Skipping the auth line in api-reference.md — the CBA expects
            API docs to state the auth requirement explicitly, not imply
            it.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Preview with `npx @techdocs/cli serve` before every push — it
            catches broken Mermaid syntax and dead nav: links for free.
         ⚡ Write deployment.md's env var table first — it doubles as the
            checklist for a new environment's setup.
         ⚡ Update docs in the SAME PR as the code change they describe —
            never "I'll update docs later."

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CH15 CHEAT-CARD — Publishing easypj's Docs                        │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
         │  backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.   catalog-info.yaml annotation   │
         │  index / architecture / api-        the 6 real docs, in nav order  │
         │    reference / getting-started /      from mkdocs.yml (Ch14.2)     │
         │    deployment / contributing                                       │
         │  npx @techdocs/cli serve            local preview, no Backstage    │
         │                                        instance needed             │
         │  techdocs-cli                       official CLI, also used by     │
         │                                        CI/CD to build + publish    │
         │  git push                           triggers an automatic rebuild  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (10 min, do this now)
         1. Write the one catalog-info.yaml line that registers jobpost's
            docs source root.
         2. From memory, list jobpost's 6 doc files in the order mkdocs.yml
            renders them in the sidebar.
         3. Name the 2 env vars deployment.md documents and what each is
            for.
         4. Run (or describe running) `npx @techdocs/cli serve` and say
            what it lets you check before pushing.


    15.6 CBA Exam: Chapter 15 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Complete TechDocs setup, writing, publishing

         │          │                                                          │
         │          ▼                                                          │
         │  15.5 PUBLISH: techdocs-ref annotation -> git push -> auto-rebuild  │
         │          │                                                          │
         │          ▼                                                          │
         │  jobpost entity page Docs tab, live and searchable                  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         Ch15 in one paragraph: 15.1 plans jobpost's 6 real doc files and
         the mkdocs.yml nav: order they render in; 15.2-15.4 write them —
         a Mermaid architecture diagram, a real REST endpoint with auth,
         and a deployment guide with env vars and kubectl commands; 15.5
         switches the whole set on with one backstage.io/techdocs-ref
         annotation, so every future update is just another git push into
         the same PR as the code change that made the old docs stale.


  🔑 PART 5 — MUST-REMEMBER
   • TechDocs is documentation-as-code: docs live as Markdown/MDX in the SAME
     repo as the code, versioned and reviewed in the SAME pull requests.
   • The pipeline has three named pieces: the Generator (runs MkDocs, turns
     Markdown into static HTML/CSS/JS, then strips the JS for security), the
     Publisher (stores the generated files), and techdocs-backend + the
     Reader (serve and render them on the entity page) (Ch14.1).
   • Backstage names two deployment models: 'Basic' (docs generated on the
     Backstage server itself, stored locally) and 'Recommended' (docs built
     in CI/CD, stored in cloud storage, served on-demand) (Ch14.1, Ch14.3).
   • Every project needs an mkdocs.yml (nav + plugins: [techdocs-core]) and a
     docs/ directory with at least index.md (Ch14.2).
   • techdocs.builder/generator/publisher in app-config.yaml controls WHERE
     docs build and WHERE they're stored; for production, ALWAYS use cloud
     storage (googleGcs/awsS3/azureBlobStorage) — local doesn't scale across
     multiple pods (Ch14.3, Ch14.4).
   • jobpost's real doc set is exactly 6 files — index, architecture,
     api-reference, getting-started, deployment, contributing — linked
     together in the order mkdocs.yml's nav: list defines (Ch15.1).
   • Publishing is one annotation: backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. in
     catalog-info.yaml, then a git push triggers an automatic rebuild;
     `npx @techdocs/cli serve` previews locally without Backstage running
     at all (Ch15.5).

  [PART 5 VISUAL SUMMARY]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PART 5 VISUAL SUMMARY — from Markdown in the repo to a live doc site  │
  │                                                                        │
  │  Ch14  TECHDOCS PIPELINE                                               │
  │    Markdown/MDX docs -> MkDocs Generator -> Publisher -> Reader        │
  │          │                                                             │
  │          ▼                                                             │
  │  Ch15  jobpost's REAL DOCS, WRITTEN AND PUBLISHED                      │
  │    index · architecture · api-reference · getting-started ·            │
  │    deployment · contributing   (6 files under /docs)                   │
  │          │                                                             │
  │          ▼                                                             │
  │  jobpost entity page Docs tab: rendered, searchable, and               │
  │  auto-rebuilding on every git push — no separate wiki to keep in sync  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  .vs-prose — In words: Ch14 explains the machinery — Markdown/MDX docs run
  through the MkDocs Generator, handed to a Publisher (local for Dev, cloud
  storage for Production), and served back onto the entity page by
  techdocs-backend and the Reader. Ch15 spends that machinery on jobpost for
  real: six docs (index, architecture, api-reference, getting-started,
  deployment, contributing) planned, written with Mermaid diagrams and real
  endpoint/deployment examples, then switched on with a single
  backstage.io/techdocs-ref annotation. From then on, updating docs is just
  another git push — the same PR as the code change that made them stale.

PART 6: PLUGINS — EXTENDING BACKSTAGE                                    🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 6 INTRO]
  Backstage core ships four foundational systems — the Catalog, Software
  Templates, TechDocs, and Search — but almost nothing else you see in a real
  developer portal is built into the core app. Every extra card, tab, sidebar
  item and integration on jobpost's entity page is a PLUGIN: an installable
  package with its own id, its own routes, and (often) its own backend. Part 6
  is where the "everything is a plugin" idea stops being trivia and starts
  being something you can install, configure and — when nothing on the shelf
  fits — build yourself.

  This part exists because Parts 1-5 gave you a fully catalogued, templated,
  documented easypj — but a bare entity page. Real platform teams do not stop
  there: they want jobpost's page to show its GitHub README, its CI/CD status,
  its Grafana dashboard, its on-call rotation, and eventually a metrics
  dashboard nobody else has built. That is exactly the arc of this part:
  understand the machinery (Ch16), turn on the plugins that already exist
  (Ch17), then author your own when they don't (Ch18).

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of Backstage as a smartphone. The phone ships with a few
  built-in apps (Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search) — that's the OS. Every
  other capability — a GitHub client, a Grafana viewer, a PagerDuty dashboard
  — is an APP you install from an app store. Ch16 teaches you how the phone's
  app framework works under the hood; Ch17 is you browsing the app store and
  installing six real apps onto jobpost's page; Ch18 is you writing your own
  app because the store doesn't have exactly what your team needs.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 6 map]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch16 ARCHITECTURE ──────▶ Ch17 CONSUME ──────▶ Ch18 BUILD            │
  │  what a plugin IS          install/configure       author your own   │
  │  (frontend/backend/         6 real plugins on       plugin when none │
  │   full-stack, ApiRefs)      jobposts entity page    of the above fit │
  │         │                          │                        │        │
  │         └──────────────┬───────────┴────────┬───────────────┘        │
  │                         ▼                    ▼                       │
  │             jobposts entity page, fully wired with plugins           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Read the map as a sentence: first learn what a plugin actually IS and how it
  is wired into the app (Ch16), then CONSUME six real, already-built plugins by
  wiring one annotation each onto jobpost (Ch17), then, only when nothing on
  the shelf fits, BUILD your own end-to-end plugin (Ch18). Each chapter is a
  prerequisite for the next.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
