  [PART 6 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part you will have wired 6 real plugins onto jobpost's
  entity page (GitHub, CI/CD, Grafana, PagerDuty, Sentry, Slack) via
  catalog-info.yaml annotations, AND built and tested a custom easypj
  Dashboard plugin from scratch — a backend endpoint, a frontend component
  that calls it through discoveryApiRef, and a passing Jest test.

Chapter 16: Plugin Architecture 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~50min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         jobpost's entity page currently shows only what core Backstage
         ships. Every card you'll ever add to it — a GitHub README, a
         Grafana graph, a custom metrics widget — is a plugin under the
         hood. Before you can install, configure or build one, you need to
         know what a plugin actually IS and how it plugs into the app.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ define what a Backstage plugin is and name its three shapes
            (Frontend-only, Backend-only, Full-Stack)
         ✅ explain how createPlugin()/createRoutableExtension() wire a
            frontend plugin into a route, and name the newer
            createFrontendPlugin() surface
         ✅ explain how createRouter() exposes backend plugin endpoints
         ✅ distinguish the New Backend System (createBackendPlugin()) from
            the legacy createRouter()/backend-common pattern
         ✅ navigate the 200+ plugin marketplace and categorize plugins by
            type (core, CI/CD, monitoring, incident mgmt, PM/docs)
         ✅ decide whether a feature needs to be Frontend-only, Backend-only,
            or Full-Stack

    CBA RELEVANCE: plugin architecture is graded under Customizing (32%),
         the single largest CBA domain — every plugin-related question on
         the exam assumes this chapter's model.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         16.1 What are Backstage Plugins? -> 16.2 Frontend Plugins ->
         16.3 Backend Plugins -> 16.4 The Plugin Marketplace ->
         16.5 CBA Exam Practice.

    16.1 What are Backstage Plugins?
         - Plugins are the building blocks of Backstage
         - Every feature is a plugin (Catalog, TechDocs, Scaffolder, Search)
         - Plugins can be: Frontend-only, Backend-only, or Full-stack
         - Plugin ecosystem: 200+ community plugins
         - You can build custom plugins for YOUR organization

         - ℹ️ Official docs now label createPlugin/createRoutableExtension
           (shown in 16.2) as the LEGACY frontend system. Newer Backstage
           apps build frontend plugins with createFrontendPlugin() from
           @backstage/frontend-plugin-api instead — same underlying
           concepts (plugin id, routable extensions, ApiRefs) but a
           different API surface
         [DIAGRAM: Plugin architecture overview]

         [PLUGIN ARCHITECTURE — How Plugins Work]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │              BACKSTAGE APP                           │    │
         │  │                                                     │    │
         │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │    │
         │  │  │ Catalog  │  │Scaffolder│  │ TechDocs │  ...     │    │
         │  │  │ Plugin   │  │ Plugin   │  │ Plugin   │          │    │
         │  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘          │    │
         │  │       │             │              │                │    │
         │  │  ┌────┴─────────────┴──────────────┴────┐          │    │
         │  │  │         PLUGIN FRAMEWORK              │          │    │
         │  │  │  • Plugin Registry                    │          │    │
         │  │  │  • Route Management                   │          │    │
         │  │  │  • API Client Proxy                   │          │    │
         │  │  │  • Component Mounting                 │          │    │
         │  │  │  • Event Bus                          │          │    │
         │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘          │    │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.2 Frontend Plugins
         - createPlugin() API
         - createRoutableExtension() for routing
         - createComponentExtension() for cards
         - Plugin API: useApi(), createApiRef()

         - createApiFactory() ties a createApiRef() definition to a concrete
           implementation (registered in packages/app/src/apis.ts) — the
           ApiRef alone is just an id/type, the factory supplies the object
           useApi() actually returns
         - Accessing backend data from frontend
         [FRONTEND PLUGIN SKELETON — Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard/src/plugin.ts                          │
         │  import { createPlugin, createRoutableExtension } from              │
         │    '@backstage/core-plugin-api';                                    │
         │  import { rootRouteRef } from './routes';                           │
         │                                                                     │
         │  export const easypjDashboardPlugin = createPlugin({                │
         │    id: 'easypj-dashboard',                                          │
         │    routes: { root: rootRouteRef },                                  │
         │  });                                                                │
         │                                                                     │
         │  export const EasypjDashboardPage = easypjDashboardPlugin.provide(  │
         │    createRoutableExtension({                                        │
         │      name: 'EasypjDashboardPage',                                   │
         │      component: () => import('./components/DashboardPage')          │
         │        .then(m => m.DashboardPage),                                 │
         │      mountPoint: rootRouteRef,                                      │
         │    }),                                                              │
         │  );                                                                 │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.3 Backend Plugins
         - createRouter() for HTTP endpoints
         - Database access via PluginDatabaseManager
         - URL reading via UrlReader
         - Event system for cross-plugin communication
         - Scheduling tasks (cron-like)

         - ⚠️ LEGACY NOTE: The createRouter()/@backstage/backend-common
           pattern shown below is the OLD backend system. The official docs
           confirm @backstage/backend-common is now deprecated — new backend
           plugins should use createBackendPlugin() from
           @backstage/backend-plugin-api instead (the New Backend System,
           built on 5 concepts: Backend, Plugins, Services, Extension
           Points, and Modules). See 18.3 for the modern pattern.
         [BACKEND PLUGIN SKELETON — Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard-backend/src/service/router.ts              │
         │  import { createRouter } from '@backstage/backend-common';              │
         │  import express from 'express';                                         │
         │                                                                         │
         │  export async function createEasypjRouter(): Promise<express.Router> {  │
         │    const router = express.Router();                                     │
         │    router.use(express.json());                                          │
         │                                                                         │
         │    router.get('/job-stats', async (_req, res) => {                      │
         │      res.json({ activePostings: 42, newToday: 3 });                     │
         │    });                                                                  │
         │                                                                         │
         │    return router;                                                       │
         │  }                                                                      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - @backstage/plugin-catalog
         - @backstage/plugin-scaffolder
         - @backstage/plugin-techdocs
         - @backstage/plugin-search
         - @backstage/plugin-kubernetes
         - Community plugins: PagerDuty, Sentry, Jira, Grafana, etc.

         [FRONTEND vs BACKEND vs FULL-STACK PLUGINS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔌 PLUGIN TYPES — When to Use Each                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ TYPE          DESCRIPTION        EXAMPLE                ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────││
         │  │ 🖥️ Frontend   Only renders UI.    • GitHub README card  ││
         │  │    Only       Calls external APIs  • Grafana iframe      ││
         │  │               directly from       • User avatar menu    ││
         │  │               browser.                                  ││
         │  │                                                         ││
         │  │ ⚙️ Backend    Only runs on        • Catalog processors   ││
         │  │    Only       server. Processes   • Search indexing      ││
         │  │               data, no UI.        • Auth providers       ││
         │  │                                                         ││
         │  │ 🔄 Full-Stack Has BOTH frontend   • Kubernetes plugin    ││
         │  │               AND backend.        • Scaffolder           ││
         │  │               Most common type.   • PagerDuty            ││
         │  │                                    • Sentry               ││
         │  │                                    • TechDocs             ││
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Most plugins are Full-Stack: UI in packages/app,         │
         │     backend logic in packages/backend                        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The [PLUGIN FRAMEWORK] box in 16.1 lists five internal services
         every plugin rides on: a Plugin Registry (who's installed), Route
         Management (which URL renders which page), an API Client Proxy
         (how a component reaches its backend without hardcoding a host), a
         Component Mounting layer (where a card actually renders), and an
         Event Bus (cross-plugin signals). The Frontend/Backend split in
         16.2/16.3 is really just "which side of that framework does my
         code plug into" — and createApiRef() + createApiFactory() (16.2)
         is the glue: the ApiRef is just an id/type, the factory
         (registered in packages/app/src/apis.ts) is what actually
         satisfies useApi() at runtime.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         ✅ Every Backstage feature (Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search)
            is itself a plugin — there's no separate "core app" logic
            outside the plugin framework.
         ✅ Plugins ship as Frontend-only, Backend-only, or Full-Stack; most
            real plugins are Full-Stack.
         ✅ createPlugin()/createRoutableExtension() is the LEGACY frontend
            API; createFrontendPlugin() from @backstage/frontend-plugin-api
            is the newer surface — same concepts, different API.
         ✅ createRouter()/@backstage/backend-common is the LEGACY backend
            pattern; createBackendPlugin() from @backstage/backend-plugin-api
            (the New Backend System) is current.
         ✅ useApi()+createApiRef() let a component call a backend without
            hardcoding a URL; createApiFactory() supplies the concrete
            implementation.
         ✅ 200+ community plugins exist at backstage.io/plugins across
            categories like CI/CD, monitoring, incident management, and
            PM/docs.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Assuming createPlugin()/createRoutableExtension() is the ONLY
            frontend API — official docs now point to createFrontendPlugin()
            as well.
         ⚠️ Confusing @backstage/backend-common's createRouter() (deprecated
            legacy) with the New Backend System's createBackendPlugin().
         ⚠️ Forgetting that an ApiRef alone (createApiRef()) does nothing —
            it needs a createApiFactory() registered in apis.ts before
            useApi() returns something real.
         ⚠️ Assuming every plugin must be Full-Stack — many (a GitHub README
            card, for instance) are Frontend-only.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Check backstage.io/plugins BEFORE writing a custom plugin — the
            marketplace already has 200+ entries.
         ⚡ When reading unfamiliar plugin code, find its plugin.ts first —
            that's where the id, routes and APIs are declared.
         ⚡ A plugins/<name>-backend/ sibling package is your fastest signal
            that a plugin is Full-Stack.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CH16 CHEAT-CARD — Plugin API Building Blocks                            │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────       │
         │ createPlugin({ id, routes })            declare a frontend plugin       │
         │ createRoutableExtension({...})          mount a page onto a route       │
         │ createApiRef<T>()                       declare an API contract id      │
         │ createApiFactory({ api, deps, factory }) supply the real implementation │
         │ useApi(someApiRef)                      consume an API in a component   │
         │ createRouter()                          legacy backend HTTP router      │
         │ createBackendPlugin({ pluginId,         New Backend System entry        │
         │   register(env) {...} })                                                │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (10 min, do this now)
         1. Open packages/app/src/apis.ts and find one createApiFactory()
            call — identify which ApiRef it implements.
         2. Using the 16.4 [FRONTEND vs BACKEND vs FULL-STACK] table, sort
            three real jobpost plugins by type.
         3. Pick one plugin from the 16.4 marketplace map you'd install for
            jobpost's monitoring tab.
         4. From memory, name the New Backend System's 5 concepts (Backend,
            Plugins, Services, Extension Points, Modules).

    16.5 CBA Exam: Chapter 16 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Plugin concepts, architecture, types, framework

    [CH16 VISUAL SUMMARY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CH16 VISUAL SUMMARY — Anatomy of a Plugin                            │
         │                                                                      │
         │   16.1 EVERY feature is a plugin (Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs...)  │
         │         │                                                            │
         │         ▼                                                            │
         │   16.2 FRONTEND        16.3 BACKEND                                  │
         │    createPlugin()        createRouter() (legacy) /                   │
         │    + Routable/Component   createBackendPlugin() (New Backend System) │
         │    Extensions, useApi()                                              │
         │         │                     │                                      │
         │         └──────────┬──────────┘                                      │
         │                     ▼                                                │
         │   16.4 MARKETPLACE: 200+ plugins, Frontend-only / Backend-only /     │
         │                      Full-Stack (most common)                        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         Ch16 in one paragraph: 16.1 establishes that literally every
         Backstage feature is a plugin; 16.2 and 16.3 show the two API
         surfaces a plugin can plug into (frontend routes/components,
         backend routers/services) — one legacy, one current, for each
         side; 16.4 zooms out to the 200+ plugin marketplace and the
         Frontend/Backend/Full-Stack decision you'll make again and again
         in Ch17 and Ch18.


Chapter 17: Essential Plugins for Daily Use 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~45min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         The jobpost team keeps asking why their entity page can't just
         show the Grafana dashboard, the on-call rotation, or the latest CI
         run. It already can — every card in this chapter is one annotation
         away. This chapter turns on six real plugins so jobpost's entity
         page stops being a static record and starts being a live
         dashboard.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ install and configure the GitHub Integration plugin and read
            its README/PR/Actions cards
         ✅ wire CI/CD plugins (Jenkins, GitHub Actions, ArgoCD) via
            catalog-info.yaml annotations
         ✅ embed Grafana/Prometheus/Datadog monitoring cards on an entity
            page
         ✅ connect PagerDuty so on-call status appears on jobpost's page
         ✅ surface Sentry error tracking on an entity page
         ✅ route Scaffolder/deploy notifications to Slack
         ✅ trace any plugin card back to the single annotation that turns
            it on

    CBA RELEVANCE: installing, configuring and using plugins is graded
         under Customizing (32%) — the same domain as Ch16 and Ch18.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         17.1 GitHub Integration -> 17.2 CI/CD Plugins -> 17.3 Monitoring
         Plugins -> 17.4 Incident Management -> 17.5 Error Tracking ->
         17.6 Communication -> 17.7 CBA Exam Practice.

         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WHERE DO ALL THESE PLUGINS SHOW UP? — jobpost Entity Page Map                │
         │                                                                               │
         │  jobpost ENTITY PAGE                                                          │
         │    |-- Overview tab                                                           │
         │    |     |-- GitHub card (README, contributors, latest PRs)        17.1       │
         │    |     |-- PagerDuty card (on-call, open incidents)              17.4       │
         │    |-- CI/CD tab                                                              │
         │    |     |-- GitHub Actions status                                17.2        │
         │    |     |-- Jenkins build status                                 17.2        │
         │    |     |-- ArgoCD sync/health status                            17.2, 21.4  │
         │    |-- Monitoring tab                                                         │
         │    |     |-- Grafana embedded dashboard                           17.3        │
         │    |     |-- Sentry recent errors card                            17.5        │
         │    |-- (no tab — background notifications)                                    │
         │          |-- Slack: scaffolder/deploy alerts to #easypj-deploys   17.6        │
         │                                                                               │
         │  Every card on this map comes from ONE annotation on catalog-info.yaml -      │
         │  the plugin reads the annotation, the entity page renders the card.           │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.1 GitHub Integration Plugin
         - Installing and configuring
         - Viewing repo details, README, contributors
         - GitHub Actions CI/CD status
         - Pull request overview

         - Feature discovery: if app.packages is set to 'all' in
           app-config.yaml (the default for new Backstage apps), plugins
           added as app dependencies — including these integration plugins —
           are auto-detected and installed with no App.tsx code changes;
           otherwise they're wired manually via createApp's features array
         [GITHUB PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                          │
         │  integrations:                              │
         │    github:                                  │
         │      - host: github.com                     │
         │        token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}               │
         │                                             │
         │  # Annotation needed on the entity          │
         │  # github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.2 CI/CD Plugins (Jenkins, GitHub Actions, ArgoCD)
         - Jenkins plugin: View builds, trigger jobs
         - GitHub Actions: Workflow status
         - ArgoCD plugin: GitOps deployment status
         [CI/CD PLUGIN CONFIGURATION — GitHub Actions + ArgoCD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Annotations enable each CI/CD plugin's card:                          │
         │  metadata:                                                               │
         │    annotations:                                                          │
         │      github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost      # GitHub Actions card  │
         │      jenkins.io/job-full-name: easypj/jobpost-build # Jenkins card       │
         │      argocd/app-name: jobpost                        # ArgoCD card       │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # app-config.yaml — ArgoCD backend needs its API endpoint               │
         │  argocd:                                                                 │
         │    baseUrl: https://argocd.easypj.com                                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.3 Monitoring Plugins (Grafana, Prometheus, Datadog)
         - Grafana: Embed dashboards
         - Prometheus: Alert status
         - Datadog: Service health
         [MONITORING PLUGIN CONFIGURATION — Grafana]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                   │
         │  proxy:                                              │
         │    endpoints:                                        │
         │      '/grafana/api':                                 │
         │        target: https://grafana.easypj.com            │
         │        headers: { Authorization: ${GRAFANA_TOKEN} }  │
         │                                                      │
         │  # Annotation on the entity                          │
         │  # grafana/dashboard-selector: 'tag:jobpost'         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.4 Incident Management (PagerDuty, OpsGenie)
         - PagerDuty: On-call schedule, incident status
         - Linking services to incident management
         [PAGERDUTY PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                     │
         │  pagerDuty:                            │
         │    apiToken: ${PAGERDUTY_TOKEN}        │
         │                                        │
         │  # Annotation on the entity            │
         │  metadata:                             │
         │    annotations:                        │
         │      pagerduty.com/service-id: P12345  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.5 Error Tracking (Sentry, Rollbar)
         - Sentry: Error overview, recent issues
         - Linking projects to Sentry
         [SENTRY PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                           │
         │  sentry:                                     │
         │    organization: easypj                      │
         │                                              │
         │  # Annotation on the entity                  │
         │  metadata:                                   │
         │    annotations:                              │
         │      sentry.io/project-slug: easypj/jobpost  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.6 Communication (Slack, Microsoft Teams)
         - Slack plugin: Channel integration
         - Teams plugin: Notifications
         [COMMUNICATION PLUGIN CONFIGURATION — Slack]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                                 │
         │  slack:                                                            │
         │    webhookUrl: ${SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL}                                │
         │                                                                    │
         │  # Used by Scaffolder notifications and custom actions (see 12.3)  │
         │  # to post to a #easypj-deploys channel on template completion     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         Every plugin in this chapter follows the exact same wiring
         pattern: a secret or endpoint lives in app-config.yaml
         (integrations.github, proxy.endpoints, pagerDuty.apiToken,
         sentry.organization, slack.webhookUrl), and an entity-specific
         identifier lives in catalog-info.yaml's metadata.annotations (the
         [WHERE DO ALL THESE PLUGINS SHOW UP?] box maps each card to its
         chapter section). The card only renders when BOTH pieces are
         present — the plugin reads the annotation to know WHICH external
         resource to query, and app-config.yaml to know HOW to
         authenticate. Grafana (17.3) additionally never talks to the
         browser directly — it's routed through app-config.yaml's
         proxy.endpoints so the Grafana token never reaches the client.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         ✅ All 6 integration plugins share one pattern: app-config.yaml
            holds the secret/endpoint, catalog-info.yaml's
            metadata.annotations turns the card ON for a specific entity.
         ✅ GitHub Integration needs integrations.github (app-config.yaml)
            + github.com/project-slug (annotation).
         ✅ CI/CD cards (GitHub Actions, Jenkins, ArgoCD) each have their
            own annotation; ArgoCD additionally needs argocd.baseUrl.
         ✅ Monitoring plugins route browser calls through a backend
            proxy: entry, not directly to the external service.
         ✅ PagerDuty/Sentry need BOTH an app-config.yaml token/org AND an
            entity annotation — the token alone shows nothing.
         ✅ Slack (17.6) has no entity-page card — it's used by Scaffolder/
            custom actions (12.3) to post deploy alerts to a channel.
         ✅ If no plugin exists for a tool you need, that's the "build a
            custom plugin" case covered in 18.1.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Forgetting the entity-level annotation — the app-config.yaml
            token alone will NOT make a card appear.
         ⚠️ Mixing up WHERE config belongs: secrets/endpoints in
            app-config.yaml, entity identifiers in catalog-info.yaml
            annotations.
         ⚠️ Assuming Grafana talks directly to the browser — it's proxied
            through app-config.yaml's proxy.endpoints.
         ⚠️ Confusing jenkins.io/job-full-name (Jenkins) with
            github.com/project-slug (GitHub Actions) — separate
            annotations for separate cards.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Copy the annotation block from 17.2 as a template — swap only
            the value per entity.
         ⚡ Keep every plugin token as ${ENV_VAR} in app-config.yaml, never
            hardcoded.
         ⚡ When a card doesn't render, check the annotation FIRST, then
            the app-config.yaml section — in that order.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CH17 CHEAT-CARD — Annotations That Turn On Each Card                   │
         │ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
         │ github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost      GitHub + Actions cards    │
         │ jenkins.io/job-full-name: easypj/jobpost-...  Jenkins card             │
         │ argocd/app-name: jobpost                       ArgoCD sync/health card │
         │ grafana/dashboard-selector: tag:jobpost       Grafana dashboard card   │
         │ pagerduty.com/service-id: P12345               PagerDuty on-call card  │
         │ sentry.io/project-slug: easypj/jobpost        Sentry errors card       │
         │                                                                        │
         │ app-config.yaml keys needed alongside them:                            │
         │ integrations.github / argocd.baseUrl / proxy.endpoints[/grafana/api]   │
         │ pagerDuty.apiToken / sentry.organization / slack.webhookUrl            │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (10 min, do this now)
         1. Add the github.com/project-slug annotation to jobpost's
            catalog-info.yaml and confirm the GitHub card would appear.
         2. Wire one CI/CD annotation (Jenkins or ArgoCD, per 17.2) onto
            the same entity.
         3. Locate the proxy: entry (17.3) that would serve a Grafana
            dashboard.
         4. Name which channel Slack notifications land in, per 17.6.

    17.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 17 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Plugin installation, configuration, usage

    [CH17 VISUAL SUMMARY]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CH17 VISUAL SUMMARY — One Annotation, One Card                         │
         │                                                                        │
         │   catalog-info.yaml            app-config.yaml                         │
         │   metadata.annotations:   +    integrations / proxy / tokens           │
         │    github.com/project-slug          (17.1, 17.2)                       │
         │    jenkins.io/job-full-name         (17.2)                             │

         Ch17 in one paragraph: six plugins, one pattern — a secret/
         endpoint in app-config.yaml (17.1-17.6) plus one annotation on
         catalog-info.yaml, and jobpost's entity page grows a real, live
         card. Slack is the one exception: it has no card of its own,
         instead pushing Scaffolder/deploy alerts (12.3) to a channel. By
         the end of this chapter jobpost's entity page is no longer
         static — it's the single place developers check for code, CI
         status, monitoring, on-call and errors.


Chapter 18: Building Custom Plugins
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~65min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Sometimes no existing plugin fits — jobpost needs a metrics
         dashboard nobody's built yet. This chapter takes you from
         "consumer of plugins" (Ch17) to "author of one": you'll scaffold,
         wire, test and style a real custom plugin, the easypj Dashboard,
         end to end.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ decide, using the build-vs-use decision tree, whether a need
            justifies a custom plugin
         ✅ scaffold a new frontend plugin package with yarn new /
            backstage-cli new
         ✅ scaffold a new backend plugin using the New Backend System's
            createBackendPlugin()
         ✅ build the easypj Dashboard plugin end-to-end: backend endpoint
            -> frontend fetch via discoveryApiRef -> rendered card
         ✅ write and run a Jest test for a plugin component with
            renderInTestApp()
         ✅ distinguish editing App.tsx/Root.tsx (org-specific wiring) from
            building a reusable plugin package
         ✅ style a plugin UI with Material UI components and Backstage's
            own core-components

    CBA RELEVANCE: building custom plugins is graded under Customizing
         (32%) — the same domain as Ch16 and Ch17, and the one the CBA
         exam weights heaviest.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         18.1 When to Build a Custom Plugin -> 18.2 Creating a Frontend
         Plugin -> 18.3 Creating a Backend Plugin -> 18.4 Building the
         easypj Dashboard Plugin -> 18.5 Testing Custom Plugins ->
         18.6 Making Changes to React Code in the App -> 18.7 Using
         Material UI Components -> 18.8 CBA Exam Practice.

    18.1 When to Build a Custom Plugin
         - No existing plugin for your tool
         - Custom internal workflows
         - Organization-specific dashboards
         - Data aggregation from multiple sources
         [DECISION TREE — Build a Custom Plugin, or Not?]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Does an official/community plugin already do this?                     │
         │      |                                                                  │
         │      |-- YES -> Use it. Check backstage.io/plugins first, always.       │
         │      |                                                                  │
         │      |-- NO -> Is this workflow SPECIFIC to your org (not reusable      │
         │              by other Backstage users)?                                 │
         │              |                                                          │
         │              |-- YES -> Build a custom plugin (18.2-18.4)               │
         │              |                                                          │
         │              |-- NO -> Consider open-sourcing it as a community plugin  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.2 Creating a Frontend Plugin — Step by Step
         - yarn new (interactive), or backstage-cli new --select frontend-plugin
         - Plugin structure: index.ts, PluginPage.tsx, api.ts
         - Registering routes and navigation
         - Adding plugin to the sidebar
         - Styling with Material UI
         [COMPLETE WALKTHROUGH with code]

         [CUSTOM PLUGIN — Directory Structure]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  plugins/easypj-dashboard/                                   │
         │  ├── src/                                                    │
         │  │   ├── index.ts              ← Plugin entry, exports       │
         │  │   ├── plugin.ts             ← createPlugin() definition   │
         │  │   ├── api.ts                ← API client (if needed)      │
         │  │   ├── routes.ts             ← Route definitions           │
         │  │   └── components/                                         │
         │  │       ├── DashboardPage.tsx  ← Main plugin page           │
         │  │       ├── JobStatsCard.tsx   ← Dashboard card             │
         │  │       └── ServiceHealth.tsx  ← Health status component    │
         │  ├── package.json                                            │
         │  └── README.md                                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.3 Creating a Backend Plugin
         - yarn new (interactive), or backstage-cli new --select backend-plugin
         - Creating API endpoints with Express Router
         - Database access for plugin data
         - Authentication and authorization in plugins
         [BACKEND PLUGIN WALKTHROUGH — Wiring the Router]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard-backend/src/plugin.ts                     │
         │  import { createBackendPlugin, coreServices } from                     │
         │    '@backstage/backend-plugin-api';                                    │
         │  import { createEasypjRouter } from './service/router';                │
         │                                                                        │
         │  export const easypjDashboardBackendPlugin = createBackendPlugin({     │
         │    pluginId: 'easypj-dashboard',                                       │
         │    register(env) {                                                     │
         │      env.registerInit({                                                │
         │        deps: { httpRouter: coreServices.httpRouter },                  │
         │        async init({ httpRouter }) {                                    │
         │          httpRouter.use(await createEasypjRouter());                   │
         │        },                                                              │
         │      });                                                               │
         │    },                                                                  │
         │  });                                                                   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.4 Building the easypj Dashboard Plugin
         - Purpose: Custom dashboard showing jobpost metrics
         - Features: Job stats, deployment status, recent errors
         - Frontend: React components with MUI
         - Backend: API endpoints for metrics
         [easypj DASHBOARD PLUGIN — Frontend Calling the Backend]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard/src/components/DashboardPage.tsx                    │
         │  import { useApi, discoveryApiRef } from '@backstage/core-plugin-api';           │
         │  import { InfoCard, Progress } from '@backstage/core-components';                │
         │  import useAsync from 'react-use/lib/useAsync';                                  │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  export const DashboardPage = () => {                                            │
         │    const discoveryApi = useApi(discoveryApiRef);                                 │
         │    const { value, loading } = useAsync(async () => {                             │
         │      const baseUrl = await discoveryApi.getBaseUrl('easypj-dashboard');          │
         │      const res = await fetch(baseUrl + '/job-stats');                            │
         │      return res.json();                                                          │
         │    });                                                                           │
         │    if (loading) return <Progress />;                                             │
         │    return <InfoCard title='Job Stats'>{value.activePostings} active</InfoCard>;  │
         │  };                                                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.5 Testing Custom Plugins
         - Unit tests with Jest
         - Integration tests
         - Manual testing with yarn dev
         [TESTING CUSTOM PLUGINS — Real Test Code]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard/src/components/DashboardPage.test.tsx    │
         │  import { renderInTestApp } from '@backstage/test-utils';             │
         │  import { DashboardPage } from './DashboardPage';                     │
         │                                                                       │
         │  describe('DashboardPage', () => {                                    │
         │    it('renders job stats', async () => {                              │
         │      const { getByText } = await renderInTestApp(<DashboardPage />);  │
         │      expect(getByText(/active/i)).toBeInTheDocument();                │
         │    });                                                                │
         │  });                                                                  │
         │                                                                       │
         │  # Run: yarn test --testPathPattern=easypj-dashboard                  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.6 Making Changes to React Code in the Backstage App
         - This is DIFFERENT from building a plugin (18.1-18.4): here you edit
           the App SHELL itself — packages/app/src/App.tsx and Root.tsx
         - Common App-shell changes: add a route, add/remove a sidebar item,
           change the homepage, wire up a custom theme (see Ch4.5)
         - App.tsx wires plugins + routes together; Root.tsx defines the
           persistent shell (sidebar, header) that wraps every page

         [APP SHELL FILES — What Lives Where]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FILE                          WHAT YOU EDIT IT FOR                       │
         │  ────────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  packages/app/src/App.tsx       Register plugins/APIs, define <Routes>,   │
         │                                 set the homepage component                │
         │  packages/app/src/components/    Sidebar items, header, persistent        │
         │    Root/Root.tsx                 layout                                   │
         │  packages/app/src/apis.ts       Wire up ApiRef implementations            │
         │  packages/app/src/components/    Your own shared React components used    │
         │                                 across multiple pages                     │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ADDING A NEW ROUTE — Real Diff on App.tsx]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/app/src/App.tsx                                               │
         │  import { EasypjDashboardPage } from '@internal/plugin-easypj-dashboard';  │
         │                                                                            │
         │  const routes = (                                                          │
         │    <FlatRoutes>                                                            │
         │      <Route path='/catalog' element={<CatalogIndexPage />} />              │
         │      <Route path='/create' element={<ScaffolderPage />} />                 │
         │  +   <Route path='/easypj-dashboard' element={<EasypjDashboardPage />} />  │
         │    </FlatRoutes>                                                           │
         │  );                                                                        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ADDING A SIDEBAR ITEM — Real Diff on Root.tsx]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/app/src/components/Root/Root.tsx                                │
         │  import DashboardIcon from '@material-ui/icons/Dashboard';                   │
         │                                                                              │
         │  export const Root = ({ children }: PropsWithChildren<{}>) => (              │
         │    <SidebarPage>                                                             │
         │      <Sidebar>                                                               │
         │        <SidebarItem icon={HomeIcon} to='catalog' text='Home' />              │
         │        <SidebarItem icon={CreateComponentIcon} to='create' text='Create' />  │
         │  +     <SidebarItem icon={DashboardIcon} to='easypj-dashboard'               │
         │  +                  text='easypj Dashboard' />                               │
         │      </Sidebar>                                                              │
         │      {children}                                                              │
         │    </SidebarPage>                                                            │
         │  );                                                                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CHANGING THE HOMEPAGE]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/app/src/App.tsx                                              │
         │  const routes = (                                                         │
         │    <FlatRoutes>                                                           │
         │      <Navigate key='/' to='catalog' />        // default: Catalog page    │
         │  +   <Navigate key='/' to='easypj-dashboard' /> // custom: your own page  │
         │    </FlatRoutes>                                                          │
         │  );                                                                       │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] 🔑 CBA distinction: building a PLUGIN (18.1-18.4) creates
         reusable, installable code in its own package. Editing App.tsx/Root.tsx
         directly is ORG-SPECIFIC wiring — it can't be reused by another
         Backstage instance the way a real plugin package can.

    18.7 Using Material UI Components
         - Backstage's entire UI is built on Material UI (MUI v4/v5 — check
           your version, Backstage is migrating between the two)
         - You do NOT need a new CSS framework to build plugin UI — MUI
           components + Backstage's own Core Components cover almost
           everything you'll need

         [COMMON MUI COMPONENTS IN BACKSTAGE PLUGINS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  COMPONENT        IMPORT FROM                    USE IT FOR                      │
         │  ───────────────  ──────────────────────────     ────────────────────────        │
         │  Typography        @material-ui/core               All text — h1-h6, body1       │
         │  Button             @material-ui/core               Actions, form submits        │
         │  Card / CardContent @material-ui/core               Dashboard panels             │
         │  Grid                @material-ui/core               Responsive layout           │
         │  Table / TableRow     @material-ui/core               Tabular data (job list)    │
         │  TextField             @material-ui/core               Form inputs               │
         │  Chip                    @material-ui/core               Tags, status badges     │
         │  CircularProgress         @material-ui/core               Loading states         │
         │  InfoCard                  @backstage/core-components     Backstage-styled card  │
         │  Table (Backstage's)        @backstage/core-components     Sortable/filterable   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [REAL CODE — A JobStatsCard Using MUI + Backstage Components]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/easypj-dashboard/src/components/JobStatsCard.tsx       │
         │  import { InfoCard } from '@backstage/core-components';            │
         │  import { Grid, Typography, Chip } from '@material-ui/core';       │
         │                                                                    │
         │  export const JobStatsCard = () => (                               │
         │    <InfoCard title='Job Postings'>                                 │
         │      <Grid container spacing={2}>                                  │
         │        <Grid item xs={6}>                                          │
         │          <Typography variant='h4'>42</Typography>                  │
         │          <Typography variant='body2'>Active postings</Typography>  │
         │        </Grid>                                                     │
         │        <Grid item xs={6}>                                          │
         │          <Chip label='3 new today' color='primary' />              │
         │        </Grid>                                                     │
         │      </Grid>                                                       │
         │    </InfoCard>                                                     │
         │  );                                                                │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [STYLING — sx PROP vs makeStyles]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  MUI v5 (sx prop — preferred, newer)     MUI v4 (makeStyles — legacy)      │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────      ──────────────────────────────    │
         │  <Box sx={{ p: 2, bgcolor: 'primary.',    const useStyles = makeStyles({   │
         │                   main' }}>                   root: { padding: 16 },       │
         │    Content                                 });                             │
         │  </Box>                                    const classes = useStyles();    │
         │                                            <div className={classes.root}>  │
         │  No separate hook needed — styles          Requires a hook + className —   │
         │  live INLINE on the component               more boilerplate, but still    │
         │                                             widely used in Backstage core  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] 🎯 CBA Tip — Theming: Backstage themes (Ch4.5) are built
         with MUI's createTheme(). Any component using MUI's theme-aware
         props (palette.primary.main, spacing()) automatically adopts a
         custom theme — you rarely need to hardcode colors in a plugin.

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         18.3's createBackendPlugin() is the New Backend System pattern
         referenced back in 16.3: register(env) declares the plugin's
         dependencies explicitly (deps: { httpRouter: coreServices.
         httpRouter }), and env.registerInit's callback only receives what
         it declared — a dependency-injection model that replaces the
         legacy createRouter()/backend-common approach. On the frontend
         side, 18.4's DashboardPage never hardcodes a backend URL: it asks
         discoveryApiRef.getBaseUrl('easypj-dashboard') for the address,
         which is how Backstage lets a plugin's frontend and backend
         halves find each other regardless of where they're actually
         deployed.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         ✅ The decision tree in 18.1 is the gate: check backstage.io/
            plugins first, build custom only for org-specific, non-reusable
            workflows.
         ✅ A frontend plugin package lives under plugins/<name>/src with
            index.ts, plugin.ts, api.ts, routes.ts, components/.
         ✅ A backend plugin registers with createBackendPlugin({
            pluginId, register(env) {...} }) and must explicitly declare
            its dependencies.
         ✅ The easypj Dashboard plugin's frontend calls its own backend
            through discoveryApiRef.getBaseUrl(pluginId) — never a
            hardcoded URL.
         ✅ renderInTestApp() from @backstage/test-utils is the standard
            way to unit-test a plugin component.
         ✅ Editing App.tsx/Root.tsx directly (18.6) is ORG-SPECIFIC
            wiring, not a reusable plugin — that's the CBA distinction
            called out in 18.6's info box.
         ✅ MUI (v4 makeStyles legacy vs v5 sx prop) plus Backstage's own
            core-components (InfoCard, Table) cover almost all plugin UI
            needs.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Confusing "add a route in App.tsx" with "build a plugin" —
            only the latter produces reusable, installable code.
         ⚠️ Forgetting to declare a backend dependency (deps: { httpRouter:
            coreServices.httpRouter }) — registerInit's callback won't
            receive it.
         ⚠️ Hardcoding a backend URL in a frontend component instead of
            using discoveryApiRef.getBaseUrl().
         ⚠️ Mixing MUI v4 makeStyles patterns into a v5 (sx prop) codebase,
            or vice versa, inconsistently.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Scaffold new plugin packages with yarn new / backstage-cli new
            instead of hand-copying an existing plugin's files.
         ⚡ Reach for @backstage/core-components (InfoCard, Table) before
            raw MUI — it's already theme-aware.
         ⚡ Run yarn test --testPathPattern=<plugin-name> to scope Jest to
            just your new plugin during development.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CH18 CHEAT-CARD — Building & Testing a Custom Plugin                      │
         │ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────       │
         │ yarn new                                interactive plugin generator      │
         │ backstage-cli new --select frontend-plugin   scaffold a frontend pkg      │
         │ backstage-cli new --select backend-plugin    scaffold a backend pkg       │
         │ createBackendPlugin({ pluginId,          New Backend System entry         │
         │   register(env) {...} })                                                  │
         │ discoveryApi.getBaseUrl(pluginId)       frontend finds its own backend    │
         │ yarn dev                                 manual test in the running app   │
         │ yarn test --testPathPattern=<plugin>     run just this plugins Jest tests │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (15 min, do this now)
         1. Run the build-vs-buy decision tree (18.1) against one real
            jobpost need.
         2. Sketch the frontend plugin file layout (18.2) you'd generate
            with yarn new for that need.
         3. Write one Jest test outline for a plugin component using
            renderInTestApp() (18.5).
         4. Decide: does your change belong in App.tsx/Root.tsx (18.6) or
            in a new plugin package?

    18.8 CBA Exam: Chapter 18 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Custom plugin creation, structure, testing, App.tsx/Root.tsx
           editing, and Material UI component usage

         │    index.ts/plugin.ts/api.ts    createBackendPlugin + httpRouter          │
         │         │                            │                                    │
         │         └─────────────┬──────────────┘                                    │
         │                        ▼                                                  │
         │   18.4 easypj DASHBOARD PLUGIN: discoveryApiRef -> fetch -> InfoCard      │
         │         │                                                                 │
         │         ▼                                                                 │

         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         Ch18 in one paragraph: 18.1's decision tree is the gate every
         custom plugin must pass through; 18.2-18.3 scaffold the frontend
         and backend halves; 18.4 wires them together as the easypj
         Dashboard plugin; 18.5 proves it works with a real Jest test;
         18.6-18.7 finish the job by wiring the plugin into the app shell
         and styling it with MUI — while calling out, one more time, that
         App.tsx/Root.tsx edits are org-specific wiring, not the reusable
         plugin package itself.


  🔑 PART 6 — MUST-REMEMBER
   • Every Backstage feature — Catalog, Scaffolder, TechDocs, Search — is
     itself a plugin; there is no separate "core app" logic outside the
     plugin framework (Ch16).
   • Plugins ship as Frontend-only, Backend-only, or Full-Stack; most real
     plugins are Full-Stack (Ch16.4).
   • createPlugin()/createRoutableExtension() is the legacy frontend API;
     createFrontendPlugin() from @backstage/frontend-plugin-api is the newer
     one — same concepts, different surface (Ch16.2).
   • createRouter()/@backstage/backend-common is the legacy backend pattern;
     createBackendPlugin() from @backstage/backend-plugin-api (the New
     Backend System) is current (Ch16.3, Ch18.3).
   • Every integration card on jobpost's entity page is turned on by ONE
     annotation in catalog-info.yaml, paired with config in app-config.yaml
     (Ch17).
   • A custom plugin (Ch18) is scaffolded with yarn new / backstage-cli new,
     wires frontend-to-backend via discoveryApiRef, and is tested with
     renderInTestApp().
   • Editing App.tsx/Root.tsx directly is ORG-SPECIFIC wiring, not a reusable
     plugin package — know the difference (Ch18.6).
   • MUI (v4 makeStyles legacy vs v5 sx prop) plus Backstage's own
     core-components cover almost all plugin UI needs (Ch18.7).

  [PART 6 VISUAL SUMMARY]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PART 6 VISUAL SUMMARY — from concept to a wired-up jobpost entity page │
  │                                                                        │

  │      createPlugin/createFrontendPlugin (frontend)                      │
  │      createRouter/createBackendPlugin  (backend)                       │
  │          │                                                             │
  │          ▼                                                             │
  │    Ch17  6 REAL PLUGINS WIRED ONTO jobpost                             │

  │          │                                                             │
  │          ▼                                                             │

  │      backend endpoint -> discoveryApiRef -> InfoCard -> Jest test      │
  │          │                                                             │
  │          ▼                                                             │
  │    jobpost entity page: README, PR status, CI/CD, Grafana, on-call,    │

  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  From architecture to a working feature: Ch16 gives you the vocabulary and
  the API surface every plugin is built from; Ch17 spends that vocabulary six
  times over, turning on real cards for jobpost with nothing but annotations
  and app-config.yaml entries; Ch18 closes the loop by having you build,
  wire, test and style a plugin of your own — the easypj Dashboard — using
  exactly the same primitives Ch16 named.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 7: BACKSTAGE ON KUBERNETES                                          🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 7 INTRO]
  Every part before this one treated Kubernetes as a place OTHER things run —
  jobpost/easypj is deployed there, and Chapters 16-18 taught you how to build
  plugins that describe those other services on an entity page. Part 7 turns
  the lens around: Backstage stops being the tool that watches the cluster from
  outside and becomes a citizen OF the cluster. It deploys itself onto
  Kubernetes exactly like any other service (Chapter 19), then uses its own
  Kubernetes plugin to look back at that same cluster and see every pod,
  service and ingress — including its own (Chapter 20). Finally it proves the
  whole platform can ship itself, with no human running kubectl by hand, using
  GitOps and ArgoCD (Chapter 21).

  This part exists because a production Backstage instance has the exact same
  needs as jobpost/easypj: high availability, config via ConfigMap/Secret,
  persistent storage for the TechDocs cache, and a safe way to roll changes
  out and back. It is the payoff of everything the rest of this guide teaches
  about Kubernetes, pointed inward at Backstage's own infrastructure — and the
  bridge between "how Backstage works" (Parts 1-6) and "how Backstage survives
  in production" (Parts 8-9, Authentication/RBAC and CI/CD).

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of this part as building an airport control tower. Chapter
  19 pours the foundation and wires the power — the tower itself now stands on
  the same ground (the cluster) as the planes it will manage. Chapter 20
  installs the radar screen in that tower — the Kubernetes plugin — so the
  controllers can finally SEE every plane (pod) in the sky, including the
  tower's own aircraft. Chapter 21 hands traffic control to an autopilot system
  (GitOps/ArgoCD) that flies every plane according to the flight plan filed in
  Git, instead of a human controller radioing instructions by hand.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 7 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch19 DEPLOY IT  ------->  Ch20 SEE INTO IT  ------->  Ch21 CLOSE THE LOOP         │
  │                                                                                   │
  │ Backstage itself runs      The Kubernetes plugin        GitOps (ArgoCD) makes a   │
  │ AS pods on the same        gives every entity page       git commit -- not a      │
  │ cluster it will manage     a live window onto its        human running kubectl -- │
  │ (Docker image, K8s          own pods/services/ingress     the thing that actually │
  │ manifests, Helm chart)     (Ch19's own architecture      ships to production      │
  │                            becomes visible here)                                  │
  │                                                                                   │
  │ Each chapter needs the one before it: you cannot SEE a Backstage pod on its       │
  │ own entity page (Ch20) until Backstage is actually RUNNING on the cluster         │
  │ (Ch19); and GitOps (Ch21) has nothing to sync until there are Kubernetes          │
  │ manifests in a Git repo for ArgoCD to watch.                                      │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Read the map as a sentence: we DEPLOY Backstage onto Kubernetes, then SEE
  into that cluster from inside Backstage itself, then CLOSE THE LOOP so future
  changes ship without anyone touching kubectl directly.

  🧭 DECISION GUIDE — where do I start?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ If you...                          Then...                                       │
  │ -----------------------------------  -----------------------------------------   │
  │ have never deployed anything to     start at 19.1, and do NOT skip 19.2 (the     │
