  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 7 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part you will have built a real Backstage deployment on
  easypj's own Kubernetes cluster: a multi-stage Docker image, a Deployment
  with 3 replicas behind a Service and Ingress, ConfigMap/Secret/PVC for
  config, credentials and the TechDocs cache, and — optionally — the same
  stack installed and upgraded via the community Helm chart. You will have
  wired the Kubernetes plugin so the jobpost/easypj entity page shows live pod
  status across every registered cluster, with a least-privilege, read-only
  ServiceAccount behind it. And you will have connected that deployment to an
  ArgoCD-driven GitOps pipeline so a `git revert` — not a human running
  `kubectl apply` — is how easypj ships and rolls back changes.

Chapter 19: Deploying Backstage on Kubernetes
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~55min · CBA Domain: Infrastructure (Deployment & Operations)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         easypj's Backstage instance has been living on someone's laptop or a
         single Docker container up to now — fine for a demo, fatal for a real
         engineering org. The moment Backstage becomes the front door every
         team uses to find jobpost, its docs and its ownership, a crashed
         container at 3 AM is an outage for EVERYONE, not just one developer.
         This chapter takes Backstage off one box and onto Kubernetes, where
         it gets the same replicas, self-healing and rolling updates that
         jobpost itself gets elsewhere in this guide.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ explain why teams deploy Backstage on Kubernetes instead of a
            single VM or container (HA, auto-scaling, self-healing, GitOps);
         ✅ build a production-ready Backstage Docker image using the
            official multi-stage pattern (build stage + slim runtime stage);
         ✅ write and read the full set of Kubernetes manifests Backstage
            needs: Namespace, Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, PVC;
         ✅ identify the exact app-config.yaml fields (app.baseUrl,
            backend.baseUrl, backend.listen.port, backend.cors.origin) that
            must resolve to the externally-reachable Ingress host;
         ✅ install, upgrade and roll back Backstage using the community Helm
            chart, and explain why it is a starting point, not an official
            drop-in artifact;
         ✅ run the exact command sequence to go from a built image to a
            verified, scaled deployment.

    CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the Infrastructure domain's deployment and
         operations competencies — the same containerization/Kubernetes
         skills tested elsewhere in the CBA, applied here to Backstage itself
         rather than a service it catalogs.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         19.1 Why K8s? -> 19.2 Docker Image -> 19.3 K8s Manifests ->
         19.4 Helm Chart -> 19.5 CBA Exam.

    19.1 Why Deploy Backstage on Kubernetes?
         - High availability: Multiple replicas
         - Auto-scaling: Handle traffic spikes
         - Self-healing: Pod crashes auto-restart
         - GitOps: Everything declared in YAML
         - Same platform your services run on
         [BENEFITS CARD: Why K8s for Backstage]

         [BACKSTAGE ON KUBERNETES — Production Architecture]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🏗️ BACKSTAGE PRODUCTION K8s ARCHITECTURE                    │
         │                                                              │
         │                        🌐 INTERNET                           │
         │                           │                                  │
         │                    ┌──────┴──────┐                           │
         │                    │  ☁️  CDN    │ ← Static assets           │
         │                    └──────┬──────┘                           │
         │                           │                                  │
         │                    ┌──────┴──────┐                           │
         │                    │  🔀 Ingress │ ← TLS termination         │
         │                    │  (nginx)    │    backstage.easypj.com   │
         │                    └──────┬──────┘                           │
         │                           │                                  │
         │              ┌────────────┴────────────┐                     │
         │              │    ☸️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER │                     │
         │              │                         │                     │
         │  ┌───────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────┐        │
         │  │              backstage Namespace                 │        │
         │  │                                                 │        │
         │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐   │        │
         │  │  │         Backstage Service (ClusterIP)    │   │        │
         │  │  │              port: 7007                  │   │        │
         │  │  └────────────────┬────────────────────────┘   │        │
         │  │                   │                             │        │
         │  │     ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐              │        │
         │  │     │             │             │              │        │
         │  │     ▼             ▼             ▼              │        │
         │  │  ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐             │        │
         │  │  │ Pod 1│    │ Pod 2│    │ Pod 3│ ← 3 replicas│        │
         │  │  │ 🖥️   │    │ 🖥️   │    │ 🖥️   │             │        │
         │  │  │ 7007 │    │ 7007 │    │ 7007 │             │        │
         │  │  └──┬───┘    └──┬───┘    └──┬───┘             │        │
         │  │     │           │           │                  │        │
         │  │     └───────────┼───────────┘                  │        │
         │  │                 │                              │        │
         │  │     ┌───────────┴───────────┐                  │        │
         │  │     │   PostgreSQL Service   │                  │        │
         │  │     │     (ClusterIP)        │                  │        │
         │  │     └───────────┬───────────┘                  │        │
         │  │                 │                              │        │
         │  │     ┌───────────┴───────────┐                  │        │
         │  │     │   PostgreSQL Pod       │                  │        │
         │  │     │   💾 /data (PVC)       │                  │        │
         │  │     └───────────────────────┘                  │        │
         │  │                                                 │        │
         │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐       │        │
         │  │  │ConfigMap │ │  Secret  │ │   PVC    │       │        │
         │  │  │app-config│ │ DB creds │ │TechDocs  │       │        │
         │  │  │  .yaml   │ │GH token  │ │ cache    │       │        │
         │  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘       │        │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
         │                                                              │
         │  External Dependencies:                                      │
         │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                  │
         │  │  GitHub  │  │   Okta   │  │  Google  │                  │
         │  │   API    │  │   SSO    │  │ Cloud K8s│                  │
         │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [REAL-LIFE: Deploying Backstage to K8s — Exact Commands]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # 1. Build and push Docker image                             │
         │  $ docker build -t registry.easypj.com/backstage:v1.0 .      │
         │  $ docker push registry.easypj.com/backstage:v1.0             │
         │                                                              │
         │  # 2. Create namespace and secrets                            │
         │  $ kubectl create namespace backstage                        │
         │  $ kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets \         │
         │      --from-literal=POSTGRES_HOST=postgres.easypj.com \     │
         │      --from-literal=GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_xxxx \                 │
         │      -n backstage                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  # 3. Create ConfigMap from app-config.yaml                  │
         │  $ kubectl create configmap backstage-config \               │
         │      --from-file=app-config.yaml -n backstage                │
         │                                                              │
         │  # 4. Apply deployment and service                            │
         │  $ kubectl apply -f k8s/deployment.yaml                      │
         │  $ kubectl apply -f k8s/service.yaml                         │
         │  $ kubectl apply -f k8s/ingress.yaml                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  # 5. Verify deployment                                       │
         │  $ kubectl get pods -n backstage -w    # Watch pods start    │
         │  $ kubectl logs deploy/backstage -n backstage                │
         │  $ curl https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck             │
         │                                                              │
         │  # 6. Scale up for production                                 │
         │  $ kubectl scale deploy/backstage --replicas=3 -n backstage  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    19.2 Backstage Docker Image
         - Official Dockerfile from Backstage
         - Building a custom Docker image for easypj-portal
         - Multi-stage builds for optimization
         - Image tagging strategy
         [CODE BLOCK: Dockerfile for Backstage]

         [DOCKERFILE — Backstage on K8s]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Dockerfile for Backstage with easypj customization        │
         │                                                              │
         │  FROM node:18-bookworm-slim AS build                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  WORKDIR /app                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │  COPY package.json yarn.lock ./                              │
         │  RUN yarn install --frozen-lockfile --production=false       │
         │                                                              │
         │  COPY . .                                                    │
         │  RUN yarn tsc                                               │
         │  RUN yarn build                                              │
         │                                                              │
         │  FROM node:18-bookworm-slim AS runtime                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  WORKDIR /app                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │  COPY --from=build /app/packages/backend/dist ./dist         │
         │  COPY --from=build /app/packages/backend/package.json ./     │
         │  COPY --from=build /app/node_modules ./node_modules          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ENV NODE_ENV=production                                     │
         │  EXPOSE 7007                                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  CMD ["node", "packages/backend", "--config", "app-config.yaml"]│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    19.3 Kubernetes Manifests for Backstage
         - Namespace: backstage
         - Deployment: 3 replicas, resource limits
         - Service: ClusterIP + Ingress
         - ConfigMap: app-config.yaml
         - Critical fields inside that mounted app-config.yaml: app.baseUrl, backend.baseUrl, backend.listen.port, and backend.cors.origin must all resolve to the externally-reachable URL (e.g. the Ingress host), or the frontend cannot reach the backend
         - Secret: Database credentials, GitHub tokens
         - PersistentVolumeClaim: For TechDocs cache
         [K8s DEPLOYMENT MANIFEST — Backstage on Kubernetes]

         [K8s DEPLOYMENT — Backstage on Kubernetes]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # backstage-deployment.yaml                                 │
         │  apiVersion: apps/v1                                         │
         │  kind: Deployment                                            │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: backstage                                           │
         │    namespace: backstage                                      │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    replicas: 3                                               │
         │    selector:                                                 │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: backstage                                        │
         │    template:                                                 │
         │      metadata:                                               │
         │        labels:                                               │
         │          app: backstage                                      │
         │      spec:                                                   │
         │        containers:                                           │
         │        - name: backstage                                     │
         │          image: registry.easypj.com/backstage:v1.0           │
         │          ports:                                              │
         │            - containerPort: 7007                             │
         │          env:                                                │
         │            - name: POSTGRES_HOST                             │
         │              valueFrom:                                      │
         │                secretKeyRef:                                 │
         │                  name: backstage-secrets                     │
         │                  key: postgres-host                          │
         │            - name: GITHUB_TOKEN                              │
         │              valueFrom:                                      │
         │                secretKeyRef:                                 │
         │                  name: backstage-secrets                     │
         │                  key: github-token                           │
         │          volumeMounts:                                       │
         │            - name: config                                    │
         │              mountPath: /app/app-config.yaml                 │
         │              subPath: app-config.yaml                        │
         │          resources:                                          │
         │            requests:                                         │
         │              memory: "512Mi"                                 │
         │              cpu: "500m"                                     │
         │            limits:                                           │
         │              memory: "2Gi"                                   │
         │              cpu: "2000m"                                    │
         │        volumes:                                              │
         │          - name: config                                      │
         │            configMap:                                        │
         │              name: backstage-config                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    19.4 Helm Chart for Backstage
         - Official Backstage Helm chart
         - Official FAQ caveat: Backstage's own docs explain there are no official published Helm charts or Docker images because "Backstage is not a packaged service that you can use out of the box" - every real deployment is built from your own customized `@backstage/create-app` output, so treat the community backstage/charts chart as a starting point, not a drop-in production artifact
         - Customizing values.yaml
         - Installing with Helm
         - Upgrading with Helm
         [HELM CHART — Install, Upgrade, Rollback]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ helm repo add backstage https://backstage.github.io/charts  │
         │  $ helm repo update                                            │
         │                                                                │
         │  $ helm install backstage backstage/backstage \                │
         │      --namespace backstage --create-namespace \                │
         │      --set appConfig.app.title='easypj Developer Portal' \     │
         │      --set database.client=pg \                                │
         │      --set ingress.enabled=true \                              │
         │      --set ingress.host=backstage.easypj.com                   │
         │                                                                │
         │  # Upgrade after editing values.yaml                           │
         │  $ helm upgrade backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage     │
         │                                                                │
         │  # Rollback to the previous release                            │
         │  $ helm rollback backstage -n backstage                        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The mounted ConfigMap is the whole reason app-config.yaml's baseUrl
         fields matter so much (19.3). When a Pod starts, Kubernetes mounts
         the backstage-config ConfigMap at /app/app-config.yaml exactly as
         declared in the Deployment's volumeMounts. Backstage reads that file
         ONCE at boot to decide what URL its own frontend should call for the
         backend, and what origin backend.cors.origin should accept requests
         from. Because the Ingress — not the ClusterIP Service — is the only
         address a browser can actually reach, every one of those fields has
         to point at the Ingress host (backstage.easypj.com), not at the
         internal Service DNS name. Get this wrong and the Pods report
         Healthy while the browser silently fails to load data — a classic
         "it's running but it's broken" trap.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         🔑 Backstage on Kubernetes follows the same shape as any other
            service: Deployment (replicas) + Service (ClusterIP) + Ingress
            (TLS termination) + ConfigMap/Secret (config & credentials).
         🔑 A multi-stage Dockerfile keeps the runtime image small: build
            with `yarn build` in one stage, copy only the backend dist and
            node_modules into a slim runtime stage.
         🔑 app.baseUrl, backend.baseUrl, backend.listen.port and
            backend.cors.origin must all resolve to the externally-reachable
            Ingress host — this is the #1 cause of a broken frontend/backend
            handshake.
         🔑 There is no official Backstage Helm chart or Docker image;
            Backstage's own docs say every real deployment is built from a
            customized `@backstage/create-app` output.
         🔑 A PersistentVolumeClaim is required for the TechDocs cache —
            without it, published docs vanish every time a Pod restarts.
         🔑 `helm upgrade` and `helm rollback` give you the same safety net
            for Backstage itself that Deployments give jobpost: a one-command
            way forward and a one-command way back.

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Forgetting to update app-config's baseUrl fields to the Ingress
            host after moving from local dev to Kubernetes — the #1 "it
            deployed but doesn't work" mistake.
         ⚠️ Treating the community `backstage/charts` Helm chart as an
            official, production-ready artifact instead of a starting point
            you must customize.
         ⚠️ Skipping resource requests/limits on the Deployment — a memory
            leak in one replica can starve the node instead of being
            contained.
         ⚠️ Forgetting the PersistentVolumeClaim for the TechDocs cache and
            wondering why published docs disappear after every rollout.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Verify with `kubectl get pods -n backstage -w` immediately after
            applying — watch replicas come up instead of guessing.
         ⚡ `curl https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck` is the fastest
            end-to-end proof the Ingress, Service and Pods are all wired
            correctly.
         ⚡ Prefer `helm upgrade` over deleting and reinstalling — it
            preserves your customized values.yaml and gives you `helm
            rollback` for free.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ # Build and push the image (19.2)                                          │
         │ docker build -t registry.easypj.com/backstage:v1.0 .                       │
         │ docker push registry.easypj.com/backstage:v1.0                             │
         │                                                                            │
         │ # Namespace, secrets, config (19.1, 19.3)                                  │
         │ kubectl create namespace backstage                                         │
         │ kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets \                          │
         │     --from-literal=POSTGRES_HOST=postgres.easypj.com \                     │
         │     --from-literal=GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_xxxx -n backstage                      │
         │ kubectl create configmap backstage-config \                                │
         │     --from-file=app-config.yaml -n backstage                               │
         │                                                                            │
         │ # Apply and verify (19.1)                                                  │
         │ kubectl apply -f k8s/deployment.yaml                                       │
         │ kubectl apply -f k8s/service.yaml                                          │
         │ kubectl apply -f k8s/ingress.yaml                                          │
         │ kubectl get pods -n backstage -w                                           │
         │ kubectl logs deploy/backstage -n backstage                                 │
         │ curl https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck                              │
         │ kubectl scale deploy/backstage --replicas=3 -n backstage                   │
         │                                                                            │
         │ # Helm alternative (19.4)                                                  │
         │ helm install backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage --create-namespace │
         │ helm upgrade backstage backstage/backstage -n backstage                    │
         │ helm rollback backstage -n backstage                                       │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         Every subsection in this chapter is one link in that chain: 19.2 builds
         the image, 19.3 declares the manifests that turn it into running Pods
         with the right config/secrets/storage attached, 19.4 offers Helm as an
         alternative packaging of the same manifests, and 19.1's command sequence
         is how you prove the whole chain actually worked.


Chapter 20: Kubernetes Plugin Deep Dive for Backstage
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~50min · CBA Domain: Infrastructure (Kubernetes Plugin)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Chapter 19 got Backstage running on the cluster — but a deployed
         platform is only useful if the teams using it can SEE what's actually
         happening to jobpost/easypj without leaving the entity page and
         opening a separate kubectl terminal or dashboard. The Kubernetes
         plugin is the built-in answer: it turns every entity page into a
         live window onto that service's Pods, Deployments, Services and
         Ingresses, across as many clusters as your org runs.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ describe the frontend/backend split of the Kubernetes plugin
            (@backstage/plugin-kubernetes and @backstage/plugin-kubernetes-
            backend) and the REST call that connects them;
         ✅ explain how the plugin discovers resources by matching the
            backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation to a Deployment LABEL, not
            by entity name;
         ✅ configure multiple clusters via clusterLocatorMethods and choose
            between multiTenant and singleTenant serviceLocatorMethod;
         ✅ build a custom entity-page card with kubernetesApiRef/useApi to
            show information like pod CPU/memory;
         ✅ diagnose the four most common plugin failure modes: 403
            Forbidden, connection refused, TLS certificate errors, and no
            resources shown;
         ✅ write a least-privilege ClusterRole/ClusterRoleBinding that gives
            the plugin's ServiceAccount read-only access, nothing more;
         ✅ decide when the plugin's read-only view is enough and when you
            need ArgoCD/GitOps instead (Chapter 21).

    CBA RELEVANCE: extends the plugin-customization competencies from
         Chapters 16-18 into a specific, widely-used official plugin —
         expect questions on annotation-based discovery, RBAC scoping and
         multi-cluster configuration.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         20.1 Architecture -> 20.2 Multi-Cluster -> 20.3 Custom Dashboards ->
         20.4 Troubleshooting -> 20.5 RBAC -> 20.6 Plugin vs GitOps ->
         20.7 CBA Exam.

    20.1 Architecture of the Kubernetes Plugin
         - Frontend: Kubernetes tab on entity pages
         - Backend: KubernetesServiceApi communicating with K8s API
         - Shipped as two npm packages: frontend `@backstage/plugin-kubernetes`, backend `@backstage/plugin-kubernetes-backend`
         - How it discovers resources by annotations
         - Cluster locator methods: config, gke, catalog, localKubectlProxy (eks/aks are authProvider values for per-cluster auth, not locator method types)
         - Service locator methods: multiTenant, singleTenant, catalogRelation
         [K8s PLUGIN ARCHITECTURE — Frontend + Backend]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Entity Page -> "KUBERNETES" tab (frontend)                             │
         │      |                                                                  │
         │      v  REST call: /api/kubernetes/services/:entityRef                  │
         │  KubernetesServiceApi (backend)                                         │
         │      |-- reads entity annotations (kubernetes-id, namespace, selector)  │
         │      |-- calls the K8s API server (get/list/watch)                      │
         │      |-- merges results across ALL configured clusters                  │
         │      v                                                                  │
         │  Returned to the frontend tab as Deployments/Pods/Services/Ingresses    │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  HOW kubernetes-id RESOLVES TO REAL PODS                                                 │
         │                                                                                          │
         │  catalog-info.yaml                          Running in the cluster                       │
         │    metadata:                                    Deployment "jobpost"                     │
         │      annotations:                                 labels:                                │
         │        backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost -->      backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost  │
         │                                                                                          │
         │  The plugin does NOT match on entity name - it matches ONLY on this                      │
         │  annotation-to-label pair. Forget the label on the Deployment and the                    │
         │  Kubernetes tab stays empty even with a perfectly correct annotation.                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    20.2 Multi-Cluster Setup
         - Adding multiple K8s clusters to Backstage
         - Cluster authentication per cluster
         - Dashboard URL per cluster
         - Custom cluster naming
         - Single-cluster mode: set serviceLocatorMethod to singleTenant and tag the entity with the backstage.io/kubernetes-cluster annotation to pin it to one specific cluster
         [MULTI-CLUSTER CONFIGURATION]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  kubernetes:                                          │
         │    serviceLocatorMethod: { type: multiTenant }        │
         │    clusterLocatorMethods:                             │
         │      - type: config                                   │
         │        clusters:                                      │
         │          - name: easypj-prod                          │
         │            url: https://k8s-prod.easypj.com           │
         │            authProvider: serviceAccount               │
         │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_PROD_TOKEN}     │
         │          - name: easypj-staging                       │
         │            url: https://k8s-staging.easypj.com        │
         │            authProvider: serviceAccount               │
         │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_STAGING_TOKEN}  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    20.3 Custom Kubernetes Dashboards
         - Building a custom K8s overview card
         - Filtering resources by namespace/labels
         - Displaying pod metrics (CPU, memory)
         [CUSTOM K8s DASHBOARD — Real Code]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // A custom entity page card showing pod CPU/memory                       │
         │  import { useApi, kubernetesApiRef } from '@backstage/plugin-kubernetes';  │
         │  import { InfoCard } from '@backstage/core-components';                    │
         │                                                                            │
         │  export const PodMetricsCard = () => {                                     │
         │    const kubernetesApi = useApi(kubernetesApiRef);                         │
         │    // fetch pod objects, then render CPU/memory with MUI                   │
         │    return <InfoCard title='Pod Metrics'>...</InfoCard>;                    │
         │  };                                                                        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    20.4 Troubleshooting K8s Plugin Issues
         - Common errors: RBAC, connectivity, TLS
         - Debugging service account permissions
         - Checking plugin logs
         [K8s PLUGIN — Common Errors and Solutions]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ERROR                CAUSE                       FIX                           │
         │  -------------------  --------------------------  -------------------------     │
         │  403 Forbidden          ServiceAccount lacks RBAC    Grant get/list/watch on    │
         │                                                     Deployments/Pods/Services   │
         │  Connection refused      Wrong API server URL         Verify cluster URL        │
         │  TLS certificate error    Self-signed cert not trusted  skipTLSVerify (dev) or  │
         │                                                        add CA cert (prod)       │
         │  No resources shown        Missing kubernetes-id        Add the annotation,     │
         │                          annotation                    AND matching LABEL       │
         │                                                        (not name) on the Deploy │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    20.5 RBAC for the Kubernetes Plugin ServiceAccount
         - The plugin only needs READ access, never write
         - Least-privilege ClusterRole for the Backstage ServiceAccount
         - Scoping to specific namespaces instead of cluster-wide
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # ClusterRole - read-only, exactly what the K8s plugin needs                                            │
         │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                                                                │
         │  kind: ClusterRole                                                                                       │
         │  metadata: { name: backstage-k8s-plugin-reader }                                                         │
         │  rules:                                                                                                  │
         │    - apiGroups: [""]                                                                                     │
         │      resources: ["pods", "services", "configmaps", "limitranges"]                                        │
         │      verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                                                                     │
         │    - apiGroups: ["apps"]                                                                                 │
         │      resources: ["deployments", "replicasets"]                                                           │
         │      verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                                                                     │
         │    - apiGroups: ["networking.k8s.io"]                                                                    │
         │      resources: ["ingresses"]                                                                            │
         │      verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                                                                     │
         │  ---                                                                                                     │
         │  # Bind it to the ServiceAccount Backstage authenticates as                                              │
         │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                                                                │
         │  kind: ClusterRoleBinding                                                                                │
         │  metadata: { name: backstage-k8s-plugin-binding }                                                        │
         │  roleRef: { kind: ClusterRole, name: backstage-k8s-plugin-reader, apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io }  │
         │  subjects:                                                                                               │
         │    - kind: ServiceAccount                                                                                │
         │      name: backstage                                                                                     │
         │      namespace: backstage                                                                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    20.6 Kubernetes Plugin vs Full GitOps Tools
         - The K8s plugin is a READ-ONLY window, not a deploy mechanism
         - Knowing when a view is enough and when you need ArgoCD (Ch 21)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  NEED                              K8s PLUGIN ALONE       ADD ArgoCD / GitOps (Ch 21)            │
         │  ---------------------------------  ----------------------  ------------------------------       │
         │  See pod/deployment status           YES - built for this    Also shown, via sync status         │
         │  See recent rollout history           Limited                YES - full revision history         │
         │  Trigger a deployment                  NO - view only         YES - sync button / auto-sync      │
         │  Roll back a bad release                NO                     YES - one click, git revert       │
         │  Enforce Git as source of truth          NO                     YES - that is the whole point    │
         │  See CPU/memory of running pods           YES (Ch 20.3)          Complementary, not overlapping  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The entire plugin hinges on one lookup: the backend reads the
         entity's backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation (20.1) and issues a
         label selector query against every cluster registered under
         clusterLocatorMethods (20.2) — it never matches on the entity's
         NAME. That is why the plugin can show resources for jobpost even if
         the catalog entity is called something completely different: the
         only contract that matters is annotation-value equals label-value.
         When serviceLocatorMethod is singleTenant, the plugin additionally
         requires a backstage.io/kubernetes-cluster annotation to pin the
         entity to one specific cluster instead of fanning the query out to
         all of them — useful once you have dozens of clusters and know
         exactly where a given service lives.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         🔑 The plugin is two npm packages: a frontend tab
            (@backstage/plugin-kubernetes) and a backend service
            (@backstage/plugin-kubernetes-backend) talking over a REST call.
         🔑 Discovery is annotation-to-LABEL, never by entity name — forget
            the label on the Deployment and the tab stays empty even with a
            perfectly correct annotation.
         🔑 multiTenant fans a query out across every configured cluster;
            singleTenant pins an entity to one cluster via the
            backstage.io/kubernetes-cluster annotation.
         🔑 A custom dashboard card is just another React component using
            useApi(kubernetesApiRef) — the same pattern as any other plugin
            API from Chapters 16-18.
         🔑 The plugin's ServiceAccount needs ONLY get/list/watch — it should
            never be granted write verbs.
         🔑 The plugin is READ-ONLY: it can show you a rollout is stuck, but
            it cannot trigger, retry or roll one back — that is ArgoCD's job
            (Chapter 21).

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Assuming the plugin matches by catalog entity name instead of the
            annotation-to-label pair — the single most common reason "the
            Kubernetes tab is empty."
         ⚠️ Granting the ServiceAccount write verbs "just in case" — the
            plugin never needs them and it violates least privilege.
         ⚠️ Using skipTLSVerify in production instead of only in dev, to
            paper over a self-signed certificate error.
         ⚠️ Forgetting the backstage.io/kubernetes-cluster annotation when
            serviceLocatorMethod is singleTenant, then wondering why nothing
            resolves.
         ⚠️ Expecting the plugin to fix a broken rollout — it can only show
            you the rollout is broken.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ When debugging "no resources shown," check the annotation-to-label
            match FIRST (20.1) before touching RBAC or networking.
         ⚡ Use the troubleshooting table (20.4) as your first stop for any
            plugin error — it maps every common symptom straight to its fix.
         ⚡ Scope the ClusterRole to specific namespaces instead of
            cluster-wide whenever the org's clusters are shared.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ # Multi-cluster config (20.2)                                                │
         │ kubernetes:                                                                  │
         │   serviceLocatorMethod: { type: multiTenant }   # or singleTenant            │
         │   clusterLocatorMethods:                                                     │
         │     - type: config                                                           │
         │       clusters:                                                              │
         │         - name: easypj-prod                                                  │
         │           url: https://k8s-prod.easypj.com                                   │
         │           authProvider: serviceAccount                                       │
         │           serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_PROD_TOKEN}                             │
         │                                                                              │
         │ # Required entity annotation (20.1) -- must match a Deployment LABEL         │
         │ metadata:                                                                    │
         │   annotations:                                                               │
         │     backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost                                      │
         │                                                                              │
         │ # Least-privilege ClusterRole verbs for the plugin's ServiceAccount (20.5)   │
         │ resources: [pods, services, configmaps, deployments, replicasets, ingresses] │
         │ verbs: [get, list, watch]     # read-only -- never write                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
         🧪 In 10 minutes: add a second cluster to the clusterLocatorMethods
            config from memory, then explain out loud what changes if
            serviceLocatorMethod switches from multiTenant to singleTenant.
         🧪 Given a Deployment with no backstage.io/kubernetes-id label,
            predict what the entity page shows — then check your answer
            against the troubleshooting table (20.4).
         🧪 Write the ClusterRole rule needed to add "secrets" to the
            read-only resource list, and say why you normally would NOT do
            this for the Kubernetes plugin.

    20.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 20 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: K8s plugin architecture, multi-cluster, troubleshooting

    VISUAL SUMMARY — "How a Kubernetes tab gets its data"
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Entity page -> "Kubernetes" tab (20.1)                                        │
         │    |                                                                          │
         │    v  REST call: /api/kubernetes/services/:entityRef                          │
         │ KubernetesServiceApi (backend) reads backstage.io/kubernetes-id (20.1)        │
         │    |                                                                          │
         │    +--> fans out to EVERY configured cluster (20.2: multiTenant/singleTenant) │
         │    |                                                                          │
         │    +--> ServiceAccount must hold read-only RBAC verbs (20.5) or -> 403 (20.4) │
         │    |                                                                          │
         │    v                                                                          │
         │ Deployments/Pods/Services/Ingresses rendered on the entity page,              │
         │ or a custom card built with kubernetesApiRef (20.3)                           │
         │                                                                               │
         │ Remember: this whole path is READ-ONLY. To trigger a deploy or a rollback     │
         │ you need GitOps/ArgoCD, not this plugin (20.6, and see Ch21).                 │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         Read top to bottom, this is every subsection in the chapter chained
         together: 20.1's annotation lookup feeds 20.2's multi-cluster fan-out,
         20.5's RBAC either lets the call through or produces the errors
         cataloged in 20.4, and the result is rendered either by the plugin's
         own UI or a custom card from 20.3 — with 20.6's reminder that none of
         this can change what's running, only show it.


Chapter 21: GitOps with Backstage and ArgoCD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~50min · CBA Domain: Infrastructure (GitOps / Continuous Delivery)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Chapter 20 gave you a read-only window onto easypj's Pods — great
         for seeing a problem, useless for fixing one. Someone still has to
         actually SHIP the fix. Without GitOps, that means a person running
         kubectl apply by hand, with cluster credentials sitting in a CI
         pipeline as a security risk. This chapter closes that gap: ArgoCD
         pulls changes from Git into the cluster automatically, and Backstage
         surfaces the sync/health status of every deploy right on jobpost's
         entity page — so "is it live yet?" never requires leaving the portal.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ define GitOps and contrast the push model (CI runs kubectl apply)
            with the pull model (ArgoCD/Flux poll Git from inside the
            cluster);
         ✅ configure the ArgoCD plugin in app-config.yaml and link an entity
            to an ArgoCD Application via the argocd/app-name annotation;
         ✅ read the Sync Status and Health Status badges Backstage displays,
            sourced straight from the ArgoCD API;
         ✅ trace the full easypj GitOps pipeline from a code push to a
            production sync, and execute a rollback via git revert;
         ✅ distinguish the sync/health state combinations and identify which
            ones mean trouble;
         ✅ choose between ArgoCD and Flux for a given team based on UI,
            multi-tenancy and progressive-delivery needs;
         ✅ recognize and avoid the four most common GitOps pitfalls at
            easypj (manual kubectl apply, "latest" tags, plaintext secrets,
            two repos drifting out of sync).

    CBA RELEVANCE: covers GitOps/continuous-delivery concepts under the
         Infrastructure domain, and depends on the deployment (Ch19) and
         visibility (Ch20) foundations built earlier in this part.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         21.1 What is GitOps -> 21.2 Backstage+ArgoCD Integration ->
         21.3 GitOps Workflow -> 21.4 Health & Sync States ->
         21.5 Flux vs ArgoCD -> 21.6 Common Pitfalls -> 21.7 CBA Exam.

    21.1 What is GitOps?
         - Git as the single source of truth
         - Declarative infrastructure
         - Automated reconciliation
         - ArgoCD, Flux, and how they fit
         [GITOPS OVERVIEW — Push vs Pull]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PUSH (old way):              PULL / GitOps (modern way):                     │
         │  CI/CD -- kubectl apply -->    Git Repo <-- polls every ~3min -- ArgoCD/Flux  │
         │  K8s Cluster                                                     |            │
         │  (needs cluster credentials     ArgoCD/Flux runs INSIDE the cluster           │
         │   in CI - security risk)         and pulls changes - more secure              │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.2 Backstage + ArgoCD Integration
         - ArgoCD plugin: View application status
         - Auto-register ArgoCD apps in Backstage
         - Deploy from Backstage via ArgoCD
         [ARGOCD PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                             │
         │  argocd:                                                       │
         │    baseUrl: https://argocd.easypj.com                          │
         │    username: ${ARGOCD_USERNAME}                                │
         │    password: ${ARGOCD_PASSWORD}                                │
         │                                                                │
         │  # Annotation on the entity links it to an ArgoCD Application  │
         │  metadata:                                                     │
         │    annotations:                                                │
         │      argocd/app-name: jobpost                                  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WHAT THE ARGOCD CARD ACTUALLY LOOKS LIKE — jobpost Entity Page                  │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  ArgoCD Application: jobpost                                                     │
         │  Sync Status:   Synced       (green)                                             │
         │  Health Status: Healthy      (green)                                             │
         │  Last Sync:     2 minutes ago                                                    │
         │  Revision:      a1b2c3d (main)                                                   │
         │  [Open in ArgoCD]   [Sync Now]   [View History]                                  │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  Both badges come straight from the ArgoCD API via the baseUrl/username/         │
         │  password configured above - Backstage never talks to Kubernetes directly here.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.3 GitOps Workflow for easypj
         - Code push → CI builds image → Updates K8s manifests → ArgoCD syncs
         - Entire pipeline visible in Backstage
         - Rollback: Git revert → ArgoCD auto-syncs
         [COMPLETE GITOPS WORKFLOW — Code to Production]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. Developer pushes code to jobpost repo                               │
         │  2. GitHub Actions builds Docker image, pushes to registry              │
         │  3. CI updates the image tag in the k8s-manifests repo                  │
         │  4. ArgoCD detects the Git change (polls every ~3 min)                  │
         │  5. ArgoCD syncs the new image to the K8s cluster                       │
         │  6. Backstage entity page shows: CI green, ArgoCD Synced, K8s 3/3 pods  │
         │                                                                         │
         │  ROLLBACK: git revert the manifest change → ArgoCD auto-syncs back      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.4 ArgoCD Application Health and Sync States
         - Two independent axes: SYNC status and HEALTH status
         - What Backstage displays for each combination
         - Which combinations mean trouble
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                    HEALTHY            PROGRESSING         DEGRADED                  │
         │  Synced          All good, done      Rollout in flight   Deployed but crashing      │
         │                   (green in UI)       (blue, wait)         (red - investigate NOW)  │
         │                                                                                     │
         │  OutOfSync       Manual drift        New commit landed    Bad manifest pushed,      │
         │                   exists in cluster    mid-rollout           pods failing to start  │
         │                   (yellow - review)    (blue, will sync)     (red - fix + re-sync)  │
         │                                                                                     │
         │  Backstage entity page mirrors these two badges straight from the ArgoCD API,       │
         │  so "Synced + Healthy" is the ONLY combination that means fully done and safe.      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.5 Flux vs ArgoCD — Choosing a GitOps Engine
         - Both implement the same pull-based GitOps model (21.1)
         - Backstage has first-class plugins for both
         - Picking one is an org decision, not a Backstage limitation
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ASPECT                ArgoCD                          Flux                                 │
         │  ---------------------  ------------------------------  ------------------------------      │
         │  UI                      Rich built-in web UI            CLI-first, no bundled UI           │
         │  Backstage integration     @roadiehq/backstage-plugin-    @weaveworksoss/backstage-         │
         │                             argo-cd                        plugin-flux (Entity              │
         │                                                            FluxDeploymentsCard,             │
         │                                                            EntityFluxSourcesCard)           │
         │  Multi-tenancy               AppProjects                    Kustomization + tenant          │
         │                                                              RBAC/impersonation             │
         │  Progressive delivery          Argo Rollouts (separate)      Flagger (separate)             │
         │  Best fit                        Teams that want a visual      Teams already deep in        │
         │                                    dashboard non-experts use     Kubernetes-native tooling  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.6 Common GitOps Pitfalls at easypj
         - Mistakes teams make once ArgoCD is wired into Backstage
         - How each one shows up on the entity page
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PITFALL                          HOW IT SHOWS UP            FIX                                      │
         │  --------------------------------  --------------------------  ------------------------------         │
         │  kubectl apply run by hand           OutOfSync forever,          Never kubectl apply directly;        │
         │                                       fights the next auto-sync   always commit to the repo           │
         │  Image tag "latest" in manifests      ArgoCD sees no diff,        Use immutable tags (git SHA)        │
         │                                        never redeploys new code    so every change is a real diff     │
         │  Secrets committed in plaintext         Security scanner fails,    Use Sealed Secrets or External     │
         │                                          drifted from the vault      Secrets Operator (Ch 30.2)       │
         │  Two repos out of sync (app vs infra)     CI updates one, forgets    Automate the manifest-repo bump  │
         │                                            the other                  as part of the CI job (Ch 25)   │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The push-vs-pull distinction in 21.1 is the whole reason GitOps is
         considered more secure: in the push model, a CI job needs cluster
         credentials shipped INTO it, so a compromised CI pipeline can reach
         the cluster directly. In the pull model, ArgoCD runs INSIDE the
         cluster and only reaches OUT to poll Git (roughly every 3 minutes,
         per 21.3) — no external system ever holds cluster credentials.
         Crucially, per 21.2's own note, Backstage never talks to Kubernetes
         directly for this card: every Sync Status and Health Status badge on
         the entity page comes straight from the ArgoCD API, configured once
         via app-config.yaml's argocd.baseUrl/username/password. That is why
         21.4's two independent axes (sync vs health) exist — sync tells you
         whether the cluster matches Git; health tells you whether what's
         running is actually working, and only "Synced + Healthy" together
         mean fully done and safe.

    KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
         🔑 GitOps makes Git the single source of truth; ArgoCD/Flux
            reconcile the cluster to match it automatically (pull, not
            push).
         🔑 The argocd/app-name annotation is the only link Backstage needs
            to show an entity's ArgoCD Application status.
         🔑 Sync Status and Health Status are two independent axes — read
            both together, never just one.
         🔑 Rollback in a GitOps world is a `git revert`, not a manual
            kubectl command — ArgoCD auto-syncs the reverted state.
         🔑 ArgoCD and Flux implement the same pull-based model; the choice
            is an org decision (UI vs CLI-first, AppProjects vs
            Kustomization+Flagger), not a Backstage limitation.
         🔑 Manual kubectl apply and "latest" image tags are the two most
            common ways teams accidentally fight or defeat their own GitOps
            pipeline.
         🔑 Secrets should never be committed in plaintext — use Sealed
            Secrets or an External Secrets Operator instead (Ch 30.2).

    EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
         ⚠️ Running kubectl apply by hand once ArgoCD is wired in — it goes
            OutOfSync forever and fights the next auto-sync.
         ⚠️ Tagging images "latest" instead of an immutable tag (git SHA) —
            ArgoCD sees no manifest diff and never redeploys new code.
         ⚠️ Reading only the Sync badge and ignoring Health — "Synced +
            Degraded" means deployed but crashing, not safe.
         ⚠️ Letting the app repo and the k8s-manifests repo drift out of sync
            because CI updates only one of them.
         ⚠️ Assuming Backstage talks to Kubernetes for this feature — it
            talks only to the ArgoCD API.

    SPEED TIPS ⚡
         ⚡ Always check both badges together: Synced+Healthy is the only
            fully-safe combination on the entity page.
         ⚡ Automate the manifest-repo image-tag bump as part of the CI job
            (Ch 25) so the two repos never drift apart.
         ⚡ Default to `git revert` for any rollback — it is auditable and
            lets ArgoCD do the actual work.

    COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ # ArgoCD plugin config (21.2)                                      │
         │ argocd:                                                            │
         │   baseUrl: https://argocd.easypj.com                               │
         │   username: ${ARGOCD_USERNAME}                                     │
         │   password: ${ARGOCD_PASSWORD}                                     │
         │                                                                    │
         │ # Entity annotation that links to the ArgoCD Application (21.2)    │
         │ metadata:                                                          │
         │   annotations:                                                     │
         │     argocd/app-name: jobpost                                       │
         │                                                                    │
         │ # Rollback -- the ONLY supported way (21.3, 21.6)                  │
         │ git revert <bad-manifest-commit>   # ArgoCD auto-syncs the revert  │
         │                                                                    │
         │ # Never do this once GitOps is wired in (21.6)                     │
         │ # kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml   <-- fights the next auto-sync │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
         🧪 In 10 minutes: add the argocd/app-name annotation to a sample
            entity from memory, then say which two badges Backstage will show
            and where each one comes from.
         🧪 Walk through the 6-step GitOps workflow (21.3) out loud from
            "developer pushes code" to "entity page shows 3/3 pods," then do
            it backwards for a rollback.
         🧪 Given "OutOfSync + Degraded," decide from the 21.4 table whether
            this needs immediate action, and why.

    21.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 21 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: GitOps concepts, ArgoCD integration, workflows

    VISUAL SUMMARY — "From git push to a green entity page"
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 1. push code to jobpost repo (21.3)                                           │
         │ 2. CI builds image, pushes to registry                                        │
         │ 3. CI updates the image tag in the k8s-manifests repo                         │
         │ 4. ArgoCD polls Git (~3 min, pull model -- 21.1) and detects the change       │
         │ 5. ArgoCD syncs the cluster to match the new manifest                         │
         │ 6. entity page shows: Sync status + Health status (21.4)                      │
         │                                                                               │
         │         Synced + Healthy        =  fully done, safe   (the only safe combo)   │
         │         OutOfSync + Progressing =  new commit mid-rollout, wait               │
         │         Synced + Degraded       =  deployed but crashing, investigate NOW     │
         │                                                                               │
         │ Rollback is just step 1-5 run backwards: git revert -> ArgoCD auto-syncs.     │
         │ Skip Git and run kubectl apply by hand, and step 4 fights you forever (21.6). │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         This chapter is that one pipeline seen from every angle: 21.1 explains
         WHY it is pull not push, 21.2 shows the config that connects Backstage
         to it, 21.3 walks the steps end to end, 21.4 decodes what the entity
         page is telling you, 21.5 helps you choose the engine, and 21.6 lists
         the ways teams break their own pipeline.


  🔑 PART 7 — MUST-REMEMBER
   • Backstage on Kubernetes is deployed exactly like any other service: a
     Docker image, a Deployment (3 replicas), a Service + Ingress, and
     ConfigMap/Secret/PVC for config, credentials and the TechDocs cache.
   • There is no official Backstage Helm chart or Docker image — the
     community `backstage/charts` chart is a starting point you customize,
     never a drop-in production artifact.
   • The mounted app-config.yaml's app.baseUrl, backend.baseUrl,
     backend.listen.port and backend.cors.origin must all resolve to the
     externally-reachable Ingress host, or the frontend cannot reach the
     backend.
   • The Kubernetes plugin is a READ-ONLY window: it matches resources by the
     backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation-to-label pair (never by entity
     name), fans out across every configured cluster, and needs only
     get/list/watch RBAC verbs — never write.
   • GitOps flips deployment from push to pull: ArgoCD/Flux run INSIDE the
     cluster and poll Git, instead of CI pushing credentials in from outside.
   • "Synced + Healthy" is the only ArgoCD state that means fully done and
     safe; every other combination on the entity page means something is
     mid-flight or broken.
   • The Kubernetes plugin (Ch20) shows you WHAT is running; ArgoCD/GitOps
     (Ch21) is what actually CHANGES it — confusing the two is the single
     biggest conceptual trap in this part.

  [PART 7 VISUAL SUMMARY — Deploy it, see it, automate it]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. docker build/push  ---------->  easypj/backstage:vN image (Ch19.2)        │
  │ 2. kubectl apply / helm install -> Deployment+Service+Ingress+ConfigMap+     │
  │                                    Secret+PVC, 3 replicas (Ch19.3-19.4)      │
  │ 3. Kubernetes plugin reads the        -> Kubernetes tab on the entity page   │
  │    kubernetes-id annotation             shows live Pods/Deployments/Services │
  │    (Ch20.1) across every configured     for jobpost/easypj (Ch20)            │
  │    cluster (Ch20.2)                                                          │
  │ 4. A code push flows: CI builds image -> updates manifest repo -> ArgoCD     │
  │    polls Git (~3 min) -> syncs cluster -> entity page shows Synced+Healthy   │
  │    (Ch21.3-21.4)                                                             │
  │                                                                              │
  │ The three chapters form one pipeline: Ch19 gets Backstage itself onto the    │
  │ cluster, Ch20 lets Backstage look back at that cluster (and every other one  │
  │ you register), and Ch21 wires Git as the single source of truth so future    │
  │ changes ship themselves.                                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Part 7 in one sentence: Backstage moved onto the same cluster it describes,
  learned to look at that cluster through its own plugin, and then handed the
  keys to Git so that a commit — not a person — is what makes production match
  what's declared. Parts 8 and 9 build on top of this running instance to
  secure it (Auth/RBAC) and industrialize its delivery pipeline (CI/CD).


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 8: AUTHENTICATION & RBAC                                             🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 8 INTRO]
  Chapters 19-21 got easypj's Backstage instance running ON a real Kubernetes
  cluster, wired to GitOps, with its own Kubernetes plugin view. None of that
  matters if the portal is wide open: anyone with the URL could browse every
  catalog entity, run any scaffolder template, or delete a service they don't
  own. Part 8 closes that gap. It exists because a portal that centralizes an
  entire org's services, docs and templates is also the single richest target
  in that org — securing WHO gets in and WHAT they can do once inside is not
  optional polish, it's the reason a real company is allowed to adopt Backstage
  at all.

  This part builds that security end-to-end on easypj: real sign-in through a
  provider like GitHub or Okta (Chapter 22), a permission policy that decides
  what a signed-in identity can actually do (Chapter 23), and finally wiring
  that identity to the org's real directory — LDAP or Okta — so Users and
  Groups are never a hand-maintained YAML file (Chapter 24).

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of easypj's Backstage portal as an office building. Chapter
  22 is the front desk: it checks an ID (GitHub/Okta/Azure AD) before anyone
  walks past the lobby. Chapter 23 is the floor-by-floor keycard system: once
  you're in the building, your badge only opens the floors and rooms (catalog
  entities, scaffolder actions) your role was issued access to. Chapter 24 is
  the badge office finally plugging into the company's real HR system (LDAP or
  Okta) instead of a security guard typing in every new hire's badge by hand.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 8 map]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ch22 AUTHENTICATE ────▶ Ch23 AUTHORIZE ────▶ Ch24 SYNC THE IDENTITY   │
  │   "who can sign in?"      "what can they do      "keep Users/Groups    │
  │   GitHub/Okta/Guest/       once inside?"           current: LDAP/Okta/ │
  │   Azure AD (22.1-22.5)     permissions + RBAC       custom (24.1-24.5) │
  │                            (23.1-23.5)                                 │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: Chapter 22 answers "who is allowed in?" and hands back a signed-in
  identity. Chapter 23 takes that identity and answers "what can they do?" —
  every catalog and scaffolder action now passes through a permission policy.
  Chapter 24 keeps the Users and Groups behind that policy accurate over time
  by syncing them from a real directory instead of hand-editing entities.

  🧭 DECISION GUIDE — where do I start?
  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ If you...                      │ Then...                                │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ have no auth configured yet    │ start at 22.1, wire up GitHub OAuth    │
  │                                │ (or Guest for local dev only)          │
  │ need per-team access control   │ read Ch23 in full, focus on 23.3       │
  │                                │ (RBAC roles) and 23.4 (conditions)     │
  │ already run Okta/Azure AD/LDAP │ skim 22.3, then jump to Ch24 for       │
  │ org-wide                       │ directory-driven Group/User sync       │
  │ are revising for the CBA exam  │ read every chapter's Key Takeaways +   │
  │                                │ Practice Drill blocks                  │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 8 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part you will have secured easypj's Backstage instance:
  real GitHub OAuth sign-in with a working sign-in resolver (Ch22), a
  permission policy that maps Groups to admin/developer/viewer roles and
  enforces isEntityOwner-style rules on catalog actions (Ch23), and either
  LDAP or Okta group sync keeping every User and Group entity current without
  anyone hand-editing catalog-info.yaml (Ch24).

Chapter 22: Authentication Providers 🏗️ Beyond-Exam (Infrastructure-adjacent)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 · ⏱️ ~40min · Topic area: Infrastructure-adjacent, beyond-exam depth

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         easypj's Backstage portal is useless — worse, a liability — if
         anyone on the internet can open it and see every service, secret
         annotation, and internal repo. Before easypj goes anywhere near
         production, it needs a real front door: a login flow employees
         actually use (their GitHub or company SSO), not a wide-open guest
         pass. This chapter is that front door — the first of three chapters
         that turn easypj's portal from a wide-open demo into something a
         real company would trust with its catalog.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ Compare the built-in auth providers (GitHub, GitLab, Google, Okta,
            Azure AD, Auth0, Keycloak, Guest) and pick the right one for a
            given org
         ✅ Register and configure a GitHub OAuth App end-to-end for easypj,
            including the exact callback URL
         ✅ Write a sign-in resolver (e.g. usernameMatchingUserEntityName) that
            maps a provider identity to a Backstage User entity
         ✅ Configure enterprise SSO via Okta or Azure AD (Entra ID), including
            the OIDC-only caveat for the built-in providers
         ✅ Run multiple auth providers side by side with a sign-in picker
         ✅ Enable guest/anonymous access safely for local development only,
            and explain why it must never reach production

    CBA RELEVANCE
         Not one of the 19 official CBA competencies (the front matter's
         own coverage audit maps Infrastructure's 22% to Chapters 2, 5,
         19, 26 only) — this is Infrastructure-adjacent, beyond-exam depth
         included because configuring a real auth provider is essential
         production knowledge, not because it's graded.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         22.1 Authentication in Backstage -> GitHub OAuth setup & sign-in
         resolver (unnumbered, follows 22.1) -> 22.3 Okta / Azure AD
         (Enterprise SSO) -> 22.4 Multiple Auth Providers -> 22.5
         Guest/Anonymous Access -> 22.6 CBA Exam Practice Questions

    22.1 Authentication in Backstage
         - Sign-in flow: How users authenticate
         - Provider types: GitHub, GitLab, Google, Okta, Azure AD, etc.
         - JWT tokens and session management
         - Configuring auth in app-config.yaml

         │                                                                   │

         │  │PROVIDER     TYPE      BEST FOR         SETUP EFFORT           ││
