         │  │                        Workspace orgs.  Google Cloud          ││
         │  │                        Quick setup      Console config        ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ││
         │  │Okta         OIDC      Enterprise with  🟡 Medium              ││
         │  │                        existing Okta.   Configure app         ││
         │  │                        SSO + group sync in Okta admin         ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ││
         │  │Azure AD     OIDC      Microsoft 365/   🟡 Medium              ││
         │  │(config key:            Azure shops.     App registration      ││
         │  │ microsoft)             Group sync via   in Azure portal       ││
         │  │                        claims                                 ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ││
         │  │Auth0        OIDC      Flexible,         🟡 Medium             ││
         │  │                        multi-provider   Configure in          ││
         │  │                        identity hub     Auth0 dashboard       ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ││
         │  │Keycloak     OIDC      Self-hosted,     🔴 Complex             ││
         │  │                        open-source      Deploy & config       ││
         │  │                        identity          Keycloak server      ││
         │  │─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  ││
         │  │Guest        None      Development      🟢 None                ││
         │  │(No Auth)               only! NEVER      Just enable in        ││
         │  │                        in production    app-config            ││
         │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
         │                                                                   │
         │  💡 RECOMMENDATION: Start with GitHub auth for dev teams,         │
         │     Okta/Azure AD for enterprise. NEVER use guest in prod!        │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [NOTE: Built-In Providers Are OIDC-Only, Not SAML]
         Backstage's built-in okta and microsoft auth providers are
         OAuth2/OIDC only — there is no built-in named SAML provider.
         Backstage instead ships a generic authentication provider for
         OAuth2 and SAML that reduces the code needed to build a *custom*
         provider adhering to those standards. If your Okta or Azure AD
         tenant is set up for SAML rather than OIDC, you need to build a
         custom auth provider on top of the generic SAML provider rather
         than using the okta/microsoft config blocks shown in 22.3.

         [REAL-LIFE: Setting Up GitHub OAuth for Backstage]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP 1: Create GitHub OAuth App (github.com → Settings →    │
         │  Developer settings → OAuth Apps → New OAuth App)            │
         │  • Application name: easypj-backstage-dev                    │
         │  • Homepage URL: http://localhost:3000                       │
         │  • Callback URL: http://localhost:7007/api/auth/github/      │
         │    handler/frame                                              │
         │  • Copy Client ID and Client Secret                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2: Configure app-config.yaml                           │
         │  auth:                                                       │
         │    environment: development                                   │
         │    providers:                                                 │
         │      github:                                                  │
         │        development:                                           │
         │          clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}  # ← From env var     │
         │          clientSecret: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET}               │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3: Set env vars and restart                             │
         │  $ export GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=abc123                            │
         │  $ export GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET=xyz789                        │
         │  $ yarn dev                                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 4: Test — Open http://localhost:3000                   │
         │  Click "Sign In" → "GitHub" → Authorize → You're logged in!  │
         │  Your GitHub username/avatar appears in the top right.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Creating a GitHub OAuth App
         - Configuring GitHub auth in Backstage
         - GitHub Teams → Backstage Groups mapping
         - Sign-in resolver: How GitHub user becomes Backstage User
         [COMPLETE GITHUB AUTH SETUP - Sign-In Resolver]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                        │
         │  auth:                                                    │
         │    environment: production                                │
         │    providers:                                             │
         │      github:                                              │
         │        production:                                        │
         │          clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}                    │
         │          clientSecret: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET}            │

         │            resolvers:                                     │
         │              - resolver: usernameMatchingUserEntityName   │
         │                                                           │
         │  # This resolver maps the GitHub username to an existing  │
         │  # User entity with the same metadata.name                │

    22.5 Guest/Anonymous Access
         - When to allow unauthenticated access
         - Limiting what guests can see
         [GUEST ACCESS CONFIGURATION - Dev Only]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml (DEV ONLY - never in production)              │
         │  auth:                                                           │
         │    environment: development                                      │
         │    providers:                                                    │
         │      guest: {}                                                   │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Guests get a fixed "guest" identity with no real permissions  │
         │  # scoping - this is why it must NEVER be enabled in production  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
         The [CORRECTION] note earlier in this chapter matters more than it
         looks: the built-in okta and microsoft auth providers only speak
         OAuth2/OIDC. If your IdP is configured for SAML, Backstage does not
         ship a ready-made SAML provider — you build a custom one on top of
         its generic OAuth2/SAML provider scaffold instead of using the
         config blocks in 22.3. This is also why the sign-in flow diagram in
         22.1 has a "resolver" step: whatever protocol delivers the token,
         the resolver is the one piece of code that decides which existing
         Backstage User entity that token belongs to (usernameMatching-
         UserEntityName is the simplest case — it just matches on name).

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
         🔑 Sign-in flow: redirect to provider -> user authenticates ->
            provider returns a token -> Backstage issues a JWT session (22.1
            diagram).
         🔑 Built-in okta/microsoft providers are OAuth2/OIDC only — SAML
            tenants need a custom provider built on the generic SAML
            provider.
         🔑 signIn.resolvers (e.g. usernameMatchingUserEntityName) is what
            actually turns a provider identity into a Backstage User entity.
         🔑 auth.environment (development vs production) selects which block
            of provider config is active.
         🔑 Multiple providers can run side by side — Backstage shows a
            picker and each provider keeps its own resolver.
         🔑 guest: {} gives every visitor the same fixed identity with no real
            permission scoping — dev only, never production.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
         ⚠️ Assuming Okta/Azure AD's built-in providers support SAML directly
            — they're OIDC only.
         ⚠️ Typo-ing the GitHub OAuth callback URL (must be exactly
            .../api/auth/github/handler/frame) — sign-in silently fails.
         ⚠️ Enabling guest in a production app-config.yaml "just for now" and
            forgetting to remove it.
         ⚠️ Forgetting to set a sign-in resolver, leaving new users unable to
            map to an existing User entity.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
         ⚡ Keep clientId/clientSecret as env vars (${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}) —
            never literal values in app-config.yaml.
         ⚡ Start with GitHub or guest in development, switch to Okta/Azure AD
            only when moving to production.
         ⚡ When adding a second provider, copy the working provider's block
            structure — the shape (providers.<name>.<env>) never changes.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 🔑 CHAPTER 22 — AUTH PROVIDER CONFIG KEYS (from 22.1-22.5)            │
         │                                                                       │
         │ auth:                                                                 │
         │   environment: development   # or production                          │
         │   providers:                                                          │
         │     github:                                                           │
         │       production:                                                     │
         │         clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}                                 │
         │         clientSecret: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET}                         │
         │         signIn:                                                       │
         │           resolvers:                                                  │
         │             - resolver: usernameMatchingUserEntityName                │
         │     okta:                        # OAuth2/OIDC only (22.3 correction) │
         │       production:                                                     │
         │         clientId: ${OKTA_CLIENT_ID}                                   │
         │         audience: https://easypj.okta.com                             │
         │     microsoft:                   # Azure AD / Entra ID, OIDC only     │
         │       production:                                                     │
         │         tenantId: ${AZURE_TENANT_ID}                                  │
         │     guest: {}                    # DEV ONLY — never in production     │
         │                                                                       │
         │ GitHub OAuth callback URL:                                            │
         │   http://localhost:7007/api/auth/github/handler/frame                 │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         AD SSO, multiple providers, or dev-only guest access — converges on
         the same outcome from 22.1's flow diagram: a signed-in identity
         mapped to a Backstage User entity via a sign-in resolver. Chapter 23
         picks up exactly there and decides what that identity is allowed to
         do.


Chapter 23: Permissions & RBAC 🏗️ Beyond-Exam (Infrastructure-adjacent)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 · ⏱️ ~45min · Topic area: Infrastructure-adjacent, beyond-exam depth

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Chapter 22 answers "who is this person?" Chapter 23 answers the much
         more dangerous question: "now that they're in, what are they allowed
         to do?" Without a permission policy, every signed-in user on
         easypj's Backstage — intern or admin — can delete any catalog
         entity, run any scaffolder template, and edit anyone's service. This
         chapter is where you draw those lines.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ Explain the three AuthorizeResult outcomes (ALLOW, DENY,
            CONDITIONAL) and when CONDITIONAL is returned
         ✅ Write and register a custom PermissionPolicy class via
            policyExtensionPoint
         ✅ Map Backstage Groups to roles (admin/developer/viewer) using a
            roleForGroup pattern
         ✅ Apply built-in conditions (isEntityOwner, isEntityKind,
            hasAnnotation, hasLabel, hasSpec, hasMetadata) to scope a
            permission
         ✅ Test what a specific user can/cannot do via the
            permission/authorize API
         ✅ Debug a permission denial by reading backend logs for the
            matching rule

    CBA RELEVANCE
         Not one of the 19 official CBA competencies, same as Ch22 —
         Infrastructure-adjacent, beyond-exam depth. "Who can sign in"
         (Ch22) and "what can they do" (this chapter) are one continuous
         production concern even though neither is graded.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         23.1 The Backstage Permission Framework -> 23.2 Configuring
         Permission Policies -> 23.3 Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) -> 23.4
         Conditional Permissions -> 23.5 Testing Permissions -> 23.6 CBA Exam
         Practice Questions

    23.1 The Backstage Permission Framework
         - Permission policies: Allow/Deny rules
         - Resource types: catalog-entity, catalog-entity-refresh, etc.
         - Actions: read, create, update, delete
         - Conditions: isEntityOwner, hasAnnotation, hasLabel, etc.
         - Every permission check resolves to one of three AuthorizeResult
           values: ALLOW, DENY, or CONDITIONAL. CONDITIONAL is returned when
           the policy can't yet tell if a request should be allowed (e.g. it
           depends on whether the current user owns a specific entity) — the
           condition travels with the response so the caller can apply it
           once the actual resource is known, which is what lets list/read
           endpoints filter results instead of allowing or denying outright.
         [PERMISSION FRAMEWORK]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🛡️ BACKSTAGE PERMISSION SYSTEM                              │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────┐                                         │
         │  │  Permission     │  ← Define what actions exist            │
         │  │  Policy         │    and who can do them                  │
         │  └────────┬────────┘                                         │
         │           │                                                  │
         │           ▼                                                  │
         │  ┌─────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐                 │
         │  │  Allow Rules    │     │  Deny Rules     │                 │
         │  │  "If owner,     │     │  "If not in     │                 │
         │  │   allow delete" │     │   group X, deny"│                 │
         │  └────────┬────────┘     └────────┬────────┘                 │
         │           │                        │                         │
         │           └────────┬───────────────┘                         │
         │                    ▼                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐                         │
         │  │       Permission Evaluator      │                         │
         │  │  Checks each request against    │                         │
         │  │  the policy rules               │                         │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────┘                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.2 Configuring Permission Policies
         - @backstage/plugin-permission-backend
         - Policy file: packages/backend/src/extensions/permissionsPolicyExtension.ts
         - Built-in conditions reference
         - Combining rules with AND/OR logic
         [PERMISSION POLICY EXAMPLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/backend/src/extensions/permissionsPolicyExtension.ts        │
         │                                                                          │
         │  import { AuthorizeResult } from '@backstage/plugin-permission-common';  │
         │  import {                                                                │
         │    catalogConditions,                                                    │
         │    createCatalogConditionalDecision,                                     │
         │  } from '@backstage/plugin-catalog-backend/alpha';                       │
         │  import { catalogEntityDeletePermission } from                           │
         │    '@backstage/plugin-catalog-common/alpha';                             │
         │  import { PermissionPolicy, PolicyQuery, PolicyQueryUser }               │
         │    from '@backstage/plugin-permission-node';                             │
         │                                                                          │
         │  class MyPermissionPolicy implements PermissionPolicy {                  │
         │    async handle(request: PolicyQuery, user?: PolicyQueryUser) {          │
         │      if (request.permission.name ===                                     │
         │          catalogEntityDeletePermission.name) {                           │
         │        return createCatalogConditionalDecision(                          │
         │          request.permission,                                             │
         │          catalogConditions.isEntityOwner({                               │
         │            claims: user?.info.ownershipEntityRefs ?? [],                 │
         │          }),                                                             │
         │        );                                                                │
         │      }                                                                   │
         │      return { result: AuthorizeResult.ALLOW };                           │
         │    }                                                                     │
         │  }                                                                       │
         │                                                                          │
         │  // Registered via policyExtensionPoint in                               │
         │  // packages/backend/src/extensions/permissionsPolicyExtension.ts,       │
         │  // added with backend.add(import('./extensions/                         │
         │  // permissionsPolicyExtension')) in packages/backend/src/index.ts       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.3 Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
         - Defining roles: admin, developer, viewer
         - Mapping Backstage Groups to roles
         - Permission inheritance
         [RBAC CONFIGURATION - Roles via Groups]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/backend/src/extensions/permissionsPolicyExtension.ts   │
         │  const roleForGroup = {                                             │
         │    'group:default/team-platform-admins': 'admin',                   │
         │    'group:default/team-platform': 'developer',                      │
         │  };                                                                 │
         │                                                                     │
         │  // admin role: full catalog + scaffolder access                    │
         │  // developer role: read catalog, run templates, edit own entities  │
         │  // viewer role (default): read-only across the board               │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.4 Conditional Permissions
         - isEntityOwner: Only the entity's owner
         - hasAnnotation: Check for specific annotations
         - hasLabel: Check for specific labels
         - hasSpec: Check spec fields
         - Custom conditions
         [ALL BUILT-IN PERMISSION CONDITIONS]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  RULE (catalogConditions.*)   CHECKS IF...                                     │
         │  ------------------------------  --------------------------------------------  │
         │  isEntityOwner                   User/Group is the entity owner                │
         │  isEntityKind                     Entity is a specific kind (e.g. Component)   │
         │  hasAnnotation                    Entity has a specific annotation             │
         │  hasLabel                         Entity has a specific label                  │
         │  hasSpec                          Entity has a specific spec field/value       │
         │  hasMetadata                      Entity has a specific metadata field/value   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.5 Testing Permissions
         - Using the permission evaluator
         - Testing as different users
         - Debugging permission denials
         [TESTING PERMISSIONS - Commands]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Check what a specific user can do                             │
         │  curl -X POST localhost:7007/api/permission/authorize \          │
         │    -H "Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN" \                      │
         │    -d {"items":[{"permission":"catalog.entity.delete",           │
         │                  "resourceRef":"component:default/jobpost"}]}    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Response: { result: "ALLOW" } or { result: "DENY" }           │
         │  # Debugging denials: check backend logs for which rule matched  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
         CONDITIONAL is the subtle piece of this chapter. A policy can't
         always answer "allowed or not" the moment a request arrives — e.g.
         "can this user delete this entity" depends on whether they own the
         SPECIFIC entity being requested, which the policy layer may not know
         yet for a list/read call. Instead of guessing, handle() returns
         CONDITIONAL and attaches the condition itself (via
         createCatalogConditionalDecision + catalogConditions.isEntityOwner
         in 23.2's example). The caller — often the catalog plugin evaluating
         a list of entities — applies that condition once each entity is
         known, which is what lets a single policy correctly show User A
         their own 3 owned services while hiding User B's 12.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
         🔑 Every permission check resolves to ALLOW, DENY, or CONDITIONAL —
            never anything else.
         🔑 CONDITIONAL means "can't decide yet" — the condition travels with
            the response so list/read endpoints can filter per-entity.
         🔑 PermissionPolicy.handle(request, user) is the one method you
            implement; it's registered via policyExtensionPoint /
            backend.add(...).
         🔑 roleForGroup maps a Backstage Group ref straight to a role name
            (admin/developer/viewer) — permission rules key off the role, not
            individual users.
         🔑 Built-in conditions cover the common cases: isEntityOwner,
            isEntityKind, hasAnnotation, hasLabel, hasSpec, hasMetadata.
         🔑 POST /api/permission/authorize lets you test exactly what a given
            user's token can do, without touching the UI.
         🔑 When a permission denies unexpectedly, the backend logs show
            which rule matched — that's the first place to look.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
         ⚠️ Treating CONDITIONAL as a synonym for DENY — it is neither allow
            nor deny.
         ⚠️ Forgetting to actually register the custom policy
            (policyExtensionPoint) — the default (usually allow-all) silently
            stays active.
         ⚠️ Mapping the wrong Group to the admin role in roleForGroup,
            granting far more access than intended.
         ⚠️ Writing a rule that looks correct in isolation but was never
            tested AS the affected user (23.5).

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
         ⚡ Default the policy to viewer-safe (read-only) and add narrow
            allow rules on top, rather than starting from allow-all.
         ⚡ isEntityOwner is the single most common real-world rule — "you
            can edit what you own" — reach for it first.
         ⚡ Use the curl authorize command from 23.5 to isolate a policy bug
            before blaming the UI or the catalog plugin.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 🔑 CHAPTER 23 — PERMISSIONS & RBAC QUICK REFERENCE                       │
         │                                                                          │
         │ # 23.2 minimal PermissionPolicy.handle() shape                           │
         │ class MyPermissionPolicy implements PermissionPolicy {                   │
         │   async handle(request, user) {                                          │
         │     if (request.permission.name === catalogEntityDeletePermission.name)  │
         │       return createCatalogConditionalDecision(request.permission,        │
         │         catalogConditions.isEntityOwner({ claims: ... }));               │
         │     return { result: AuthorizeResult.ALLOW };                            │
         │   }                                                                      │
         │ }                                                                        │
         │                                                                          │
         │ # 23.3 roleForGroup — map Groups to roles                                │
         │ 'group:default/team-platform-admins': 'admin'                            │
         │ 'group:default/team-platform': 'developer'                               │
         │                                                                          │
         │ # 23.4 built-in conditions (catalogConditions.*)                         │
         │ isEntityOwner · isEntityKind · hasAnnotation · hasLabel · hasSpec        │
         │                                                                          │
         │ # 23.5 test what a user can do                                           │
         │ curl -X POST localhost:7007/api/permission/authorize \                   │
         │   -H "Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN" \                               │
         │   -d {"items":[{"permission":"catalog.entity.delete", ...}]}             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🧪 PRACTICE DRILL
         🧪 In 10 minutes: write a policy that DENIES catalog-entity-delete
            to everyone except isEntityOwner, and ALLOWs everything else.
         🧪 Map your own team's Groups to admin/developer/viewer using the
            roleForGroup pattern.
         🧪 Run the curl command from 23.5 against two different users and
            compare the results.

    23.6 CBA Exam: Chapter 23 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Permission framework, RBAC, conditions, policies

    🛡️ VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 🛡️ CHAPTER 23 VISUAL SUMMARY — One Request Through the Policy        │
         │                                                                      │
         │   Request: "can this User delete component:default/jobpost?"         │
         │                           │                                          │
         │                           ▼                                          │
         │   23.1 Permission Policy receives the request + user identity        │
         │                           │                                          │
         │                           ▼                                          │
         │   23.2 policy.handle() checks the permission name, applies a         │
         │        rule (allow / deny / conditional) via catalogConditions       │
         │                           │                                          │
         │                           ▼                                          │
         │   23.3 Is the User's Group mapped to admin/developer/viewer?         │
         │        (roleForGroup) — role shapes which rules even apply           │
         │                           │                                          │
         │                           ▼                                          │
         │   23.4 CONDITIONAL? attach isEntityOwner/hasLabel/... so the         │
         │        caller can filter results once the entity is known            │
         │                           │                                          │
         │                           ▼                                          │
         │   23.5 Result: ALLOW / DENY — verify with the authorize API          │
         │                                                                      │
         │ 💡 Three outcomes, one pipeline: policy → role → condition.          │
         │    Chapter 24 keeps the Groups behind that role fresh.               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         In words: one request walks the whole permission stack — the policy
         receives it (23.1), applies a rule that may resolve to allow/deny or
         a per-entity condition (23.2), that rule is shaped by which role the
         user's Group maps to (23.3) and which conditions apply (23.4), and
         the final ALLOW/DENY can always be independently verified through the
         authorize API (23.5). Chapter 24 makes sure the Groups behind that
         role stay accurate.


Chapter 24: Integrating with External Identity Providers
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 · ⏱️ ~35min · Topic area: Infrastructure-adjacent, beyond-exam
    depth (same as sibling chapters 22-23 — not one of the 19 official CBA
    competencies)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
         Chapters 22-23 assumed users and Groups already exist in the
         catalog. In a real org they don't appear by magic — someone (or
         something) has to create every User and Group entity from the
         company's actual directory. This chapter is that bridge: wiring
         easypj's Backstage catalog to LDAP, Okta, or Azure AD so the People
         tab is never a hand-maintained YAML file that's out of date the day
         someone joins or leaves the team.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ Configure LDAP/AD catalog ingestion with the ldapOrg provider
            (bind DN, users/groups DN + filter, schedule)
         ✅ Trace how one LDAP entry becomes a Backstage User entity
         ✅ Configure Okta group sync so Groups and Users are created and
            updated automatically
         ✅ Write a custom sign-in resolver for identity systems the built-in
            resolvers can't handle
         ✅ Choose the right integration (LDAP vs Okta/Azure AD group sync vs
            custom resolver) for a given org's setup
         ✅ Diagnose the four most common external-IdP sync failures using the
            troubleshooting table

    CBA RELEVANCE
         Continues the CBA Infrastructure domain (🎯 ~22%) started in
         Ch22-23 — directory sync is what keeps the RBAC groups from Ch23
         populated with real people.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
         24.1 LDAP / Active Directory Integration -> 24.2 Okta / Azure AD
         Group Sync -> 24.3 Custom Sign-In Resolvers -> 24.4 Choosing Between
         LDAP, Okta/Azure AD, and Custom Resolvers -> 24.5 Identity Provider
         Troubleshooting -> 24.6 CBA Exam Practice Questions

    24.1 LDAP / Active Directory Integration
         - Catalog ingestion from LDAP
         - User and Group auto-discovery
         - Syncing schedules
         [LDAP INTEGRATION CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  catalog:                                                            │
         │    providers:                                                        │
         │      ldapOrg:                                                        │
         │        default:                                                      │
         │          target: ldaps://ldap.easypj.com                             │
         │          bind: { dn: ${LDAP_BIND_DN}, secret: ${LDAP_BIND_SECRET} }  │
         │          users:                                                      │
         │            - dn: ou=people,dc=easypj,dc=com                          │
         │              options: { filter: (objectClass=person) }               │
         │          groups:                                                     │
         │            - dn: ou=groups,dc=easypj,dc=com                          │
         │          schedule: { frequency: PT30M, timeout: PT5M }               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LDAP TO BACKSTAGE ENTITY RESOLUTION                                     │
         │                                                                          │
         │  LDAP directory (ou=people,dc=easypj,dc=com)                             │
         │    entry: uid=jdoe, cn=Jane Doe, mail=jane@easypj.com                    │
         │                              │  matched by the users.dn + filter (24.1)  │
         │                              ▼                                           │
         │  Ingestion runs on schedule (every 30 min, configured above)             │
         │                              │                                           │
         │                              ▼                                           │
         │  Backstage User entity created/updated:                                  │
         │    kind: User, metadata.name: jdoe, spec.profile.email: jane@easypj.com  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  Groups resolve the same way, one level up, from ou=groups (24.1).       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.2 Okta / Azure AD Group Sync
         - Syncing groups from Okta/Azure AD
         - Mapping to Backstage Group entities
         - Automatic updates on group changes
         [OKTA GROUP SYNC CONFIGURATION]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  catalog:                                                   │
         │    providers:                                               │
         │      okta:                                                  │
         │        default:                                             │
         │          orgUrl: https://easypj.okta.com                    │
         │          token: ${OKTA_API_TOKEN}                           │
         │          groupKey: 0oa1b2c3d4e5f6g7h8                       │
         │          schedule: { frequency: { minutes: 30 } }           │
         │                                                             │
         │  # Auto-creates Backstage Group entities from Okta groups,  │
         │  # and User entities from Okta org members                  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.3 Custom Sign-In Resolvers
         - When built-in resolvers aren't enough
         - Building a custom sign-in resolver
         - Mapping external identities to Backstage
         [CUSTOM SIGN-IN RESOLVER - Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // packages/backend/src/extensions/auth.ts                 │
         │  const customResolver = async (info, ctx) => {              │
         │    const { profile } = info.result;                         │
         │    const userEntityRef = stringifyEntityRef({               │
         │      kind: 'User',                                          │
         │      name: profile.email.split('@')[0],                     │
         │    });                                                      │
         │    return ctx.issueToken({                                  │
         │      claims: { sub: userEntityRef, ent: [userEntityRef] },  │
         │    });                                                      │
         │  };                                                         │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.4 Choosing Between LDAP, Okta/Azure AD, and Custom Resolvers
         - Same goal (map an external identity to a Backstage User), three routes
         - Decision guide for which one fits your org
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IF YOUR ORG HAS...                             USE                        SEE        │
         │  ----------------------------------------------  --------------------------  ------   │
         │  An on-prem Active Directory / OpenLDAP            LDAP ingestion              24.1   │
         │  Okta or Azure AD as the identity provider           Okta/Azure AD group sync   24.2  │
         │  A homegrown identity system or unusual mapping       Custom sign-in resolver   24.3  │
         │  No IdP at all yet (local dev, small team)              Guest auth (Ch 22)      22.x  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    24.5 Identity Provider Troubleshooting
         - The most common failures when wiring an external IdP
         - Where to look first
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SYMPTOM                           LIKELY CAUSE                     WHERE TO LOOK                         │
         │  ---------------------------------  -------------------------------  ---------------------------          │
         │  Users never appear in the Catalog    LDAP schedule has not run yet    Check catalog processing           │
         │                                                                        logs, force a manual refresh       │
         │  Sign-in works but no Groups           bind DN lacks read access to     Verify bind account can           │
         │                                          the groups OU                   read ou=groups (24.1)            │
         │  Login loops back to the sign-in page    Custom resolver threw and       Check backend logs for the       │
         │                                            issueToken never ran            resolver stack trace (24.3)    │
         │  Okta sync stops updating                  API token expired or           Rotate OKTA_API_TOKEN,          │
         │                                              revoked                        confirm scope is still valid  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
         Both the LDAP (ldapOrg) and Okta providers in this chapter run as
         scheduled catalog providers, not on-demand calls triggered by login.
         The schedule field (PT30M in 24.1, { minutes: 30 } in 24.2) is a
         catalog processing task that periodically re-lists the directory
         and reconciles User/Group entities the same way any other catalog
         provider reconciles Components — it diffs what it just fetched
         against what's already in the catalog and creates/updates/removes
         entities to match. That's why a brand-new hire doesn't appear in
         easypj's catalog the instant their AD account is created: they
         appear on the next scheduled run, which is also why 24.5's first
         troubleshooting question is always "has the schedule actually run
         yet?"

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
         🔑 ldapOrg ingests Users and Groups from two separate DN queries
            (ou=people / ou=groups) sharing one bind credential and one
            schedule.
         🔑 An LDAP entry becomes a Backstage entity keyed by a stable field
            (e.g. uid -> metadata.name) — Groups resolve the same way, one
            level up.
         🔑 Okta group sync needs orgUrl + an API token + groupKey, and
            creates both Group and User entities from org membership.
         🔑 A custom sign-in resolver builds a userEntityRef by hand and
            calls ctx.issueToken() directly — use it only when built-in
            resolvers can't express the mapping.
         🔑 Decision rule (24.4): on-prem AD/LDAP -> ldapOrg; Okta/Azure AD ->
            group sync; unusual/homegrown identity -> custom resolver; no IdP
            yet -> guest (Ch22).
         🔑 Nearly every sync failure traces to one of three things: the
            schedule hasn't run, the bind account lacks read access to the
            target OU, or an API token expired.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
         ⚠️ Expecting a new user to appear immediately after they sign in —
            ingestion is scheduled, not login-triggered.
         ⚠️ A bind DN that can authenticate but has no read grant on
            ou=groups — users sync, groups silently don't.
         ⚠️ A custom resolver that throws — the symptom is a login loop back
            to sign-in, not a visible error.
         ⚠️ An expired/revoked OKTA_API_TOKEN — group sync stops silently
            until someone checks the logs.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
         ⚡ Force a manual catalog refresh while testing instead of waiting
            out the schedule.
         ⚡ Check backend logs FIRST for a custom resolver — the stack trace
            is usually there, not in the browser.
         ⚡ Keep the 24.5 troubleshooting table open during setup — it's the
            fastest path from symptom to fix.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 🔑 CHAPTER 24 — EXTERNAL IDENTITY PROVIDER QUICK REFERENCE           │
         │                                                                      │
         │ # 24.1 LDAP / AD ingestion (catalog.providers.ldapOrg)               │
         │ target: ldaps://ldap.easypj.com                                      │
         │ bind: { dn: ${LDAP_BIND_DN}, secret: ${LDAP_BIND_SECRET} }           │
         │ users:  [{ dn: ou=people,dc=easypj,dc=com,                           │
         │            options: { filter: (objectClass=person) } }]              │
         │ groups: [{ dn: ou=groups,dc=easypj,dc=com }]                         │
         │ schedule: { frequency: PT30M, timeout: PT5M }                        │
         │                                                                      │
         │ # 24.2 Okta group sync (catalog.providers.okta)                      │
         │ orgUrl: https://easypj.okta.com                                      │
         │ token: ${OKTA_API_TOKEN}                                             │
         │ groupKey: 0oa1b2c3d4e5f6g7h8                                         │
         │ schedule: { frequency: { minutes: 30 } }                             │
         │                                                                      │
         │ # 24.3 custom sign-in resolver skeleton                              │
         │ const customResolver = async (info, ctx) => {                        │
         │   const userEntityRef = stringifyEntityRef({ kind: "User", ... });   │
         │   return ctx.issueToken({ claims: { sub: userEntityRef, ... } });    │
         │ };                                                                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    📇 VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 📇 CHAPTER 24 VISUAL SUMMARY — Keeping Identity in Sync              │
         │                                                                      │
         │   24.4 Decision: what does your org already run?                     │
         │         │                    │                    │                  │
         │         ▼                    ▼                    ▼                  │
         │    On-prem AD/LDAP      Okta / Azure AD      Homegrown / unusual     │
         │         │                    │                    │                  │
         │         ▼                    ▼                    ▼                  │
         │   24.1 ldapOrg          24.2 Okta group      24.3 Custom sign-in     │
         │   provider ingests      sync auto-creates    resolver builds the     │
         │   Users + Groups on     Group/User entities  userEntityRef by        │
         │   a schedule (PT30M)    on a schedule too    hand and issues a       │
         │                                               token directly         │
         │         └────────────────────┴────────────────────┘                  │
         │                              ▼                                       │
         │             24.5 Something not syncing? Check schedule               │
         │             timing, bind-DN read access, or token expiry             │
         │                              ▼                                       │
         │                   Backstage Users & Groups stay CURRENT              │
         │                                                                      │
         │ 💡 No IdP yet? Fall back to guest auth (Ch22) until one exists.      │
         │    Every route above still ends at the same place: an up-to-         │
         │    date User/Group entity the permission policy (Ch23) trusts.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         In words: whichever route your org takes — on-prem LDAP (24.1), Okta
         or Azure AD group sync (24.2), or a custom resolver for a homegrown
         identity system (24.3) — the destination is the same: Backstage
         Users and Groups that stay current on a schedule instead of drifting
         out of date. If something looks wrong, 24.5's troubleshooting table
         is the fastest way from symptom to cause. This closes the identity
         loop that started with sign-in in Chapter 22 and was authorized by
         RBAC in Chapter 23.


  🔑 PART 8 — KEY TAKEAWAYS
   • Authentication (Ch22) and authorization (Ch23) are two separate
     questions — "who are you?" then "what can you do?" — Backstage keeps
     them as two separate configuration surfaces (auth.providers vs the
     permission policy).
   • Every built-in sign-in flow ends the same way: provider token -> sign-in
     resolver -> Backstage User entity -> JWT session (22.1).
   • The built-in okta/microsoft providers are OAuth2/OIDC only; a SAML
     tenant needs a custom provider built on Backstage's generic SAML
     provider, not the config blocks shown in 22.3.
   • Every permission check resolves to ALLOW, DENY, or CONDITIONAL — never
     anything else — and CONDITIONAL is what lets list/read endpoints filter
     results per-entity instead of allowing or denying outright (23.1).
   • roleForGroup + built-in conditions (isEntityOwner, hasAnnotation, ...)
     are the two building blocks of nearly every real-world RBAC policy.
   • Users and Groups are not hand-typed forever — LDAP ingestion and Okta
     group sync (Ch24) keep them current on a schedule, the same way any
     other catalog provider reconciles entities.
   • guest auth (Ch22) and "no IdP yet" (Ch24's decision guide) are the same
     idea from two angles: acceptable for local development, never for
     production.
   • Nearly every real incident in this part traces back to one of three
     causes: a misconfigured resolver, an over-broad role mapping, or a sync
     schedule/credential problem — the 24.5 troubleshooting-table pattern
     generalizes across all three chapters.

  [PART 8 VISUAL SUMMARY]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🔐 PART 8 VISUAL SUMMARY — Securing easypj's Backstage Portal      │
  │                                                                    │
  │   👤 User ──▶ 🔀 Auth Provider (GitHub/Okta/Azure AD)   (Ch22)     │
  │                     │  signIn.resolvers map identity → User entity │
  │                     ▼                                              │
  │            🔑 Backstage session (JWT) issued                       │
  │                     │                                              │
  │                     ▼                                              │
  │      🛡️ Permission Policy evaluates every request        (Ch23)    │
  │         ALLOW / DENY / CONDITIONAL ─▶ RBAC role via Group          │
  │                     │                                              │
  │                     ▼                                              │
  │    📇 LDAP / Okta / Azure AD keep Users & Groups CURRENT  (Ch24)   │
  │         (scheduled ingestion — no hand-maintained YAML)            │
  │                                                                    │
  │ 💡 Sign in → get authorized → stay in sync. Three chapters,        │
  │    one continuous identity pipeline for easypj.                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  In words: a user signs in through a provider (Ch22) and becomes a Backstage
  identity; every action they take from then on is evaluated by the
  permission policy (Ch23), which checks their role and any per-entity
  conditions; and the Users/Groups that policy relies on are kept accurate by
  a scheduled sync from LDAP or Okta (Ch24) instead of a stale hand-written
  file. Three chapters, one continuous identity pipeline securing easypj.


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 9: PRODUCTION & CI/CD                                                🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 9 INTRO]
  Parts 1-8 took easypj from "just an idea" to a fully cataloged, templated,
  documented, plugin-equipped, containerized, authenticated and RBAC-secured
  Backstage instance. Part 9 is where all of that stops being a demo and
  starts being something a real team can bet their job board on: an
  automated pipeline that ships every commit, a production deployment that
  survives real traffic and real failure, and an observability stack that
  tells you the moment something goes wrong instead of waiting on a support
  ticket.

  This part exists because "it works on my laptop" and "it works in front of
  customers, at 3 AM, after the fifth deploy this week" are two entirely
  different claims. Chapter 25 gives easypj's builds a trustworthy delivery
  mechanism. Chapter 26 turns the Kubernetes deployment from Part 7 into
  something that meets a real production bar (a real database, safe
  upgrades, a disaster-recovery plan). Chapter 27 makes sure you can SEE all
  of it — metrics, logs, alerts and usage — instead of flying blind.

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of Parts 1-8 as building and stocking a restaurant
  kitchen. Part 9 is opening night for real customers: a supply chain that
  keeps fresh ingredients (code) arriving automatically (CI/CD), a
  health-inspector checklist before you unlock the front door (production
  readiness), and a bank of security cameras and smoke detectors watching
  the kitchen every second service is running (monitoring & observability).
  Skipping any one of the three is how restaurants — and platforms — fail
  on launch night.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 9 map]
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch25 BUILD THE PIPELINE ──▶ Ch26 GO LIVE ──▶ Ch27 STAY UP & WATCHED         │
  │  CI/CD visibility for          production checklist,      metrics, logs,    │

  │  GitLab CI/ArgoCD, then         search at scale, safe        analytics on   │
  │  one aggregated dashboard       upgrades, zero-downtime      easypj in prod │
  │                                 rollout, DR runbook                         │
  │                                                                             │
  │ Each chapter assumes the last: you cannot safely deploy easypj to           │
  │ production (Ch26) without a pipeline you trust to ship known-good           │
  │ builds (Ch25); and a production deployment (Ch26) is only as safe as        │
  │ your ability to SEE it breathing in real time once it is live (Ch27).       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
