  [PART 9 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part you will have wired easypj's GitHub Actions,
  Jenkins, GitLab CI and ArgoCD signals into one aggregated CI/CD card,
  moved it onto PostgreSQL with production TechDocs/search, upgraded it
  safely with a zero-downtime rolling strategy and a written DR runbook, and
  stood up Prometheus + Loki + Alertmanager + usage analytics so you know
  easypj is both UP and actually being used.

Chapter 25: CI/CD Integration with Backstage
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~40min · CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         easypj's job board ships fast — several PRs a day once a real team
         is using it. Without CI/CD visibility built into Backstage, a
         developer has to leave the catalog and open GitHub, Jenkins,
         GitLab and ArgoCD in four separate tabs just to answer "is my
         change even safe to merge?" This chapter puts that answer directly
         on the easypj entity page.

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ explain why CI/CD status belongs on the entity page instead of
            a separate tool switcher
         ✅ install & annotate the GitHub Actions plugin for easypj/jobpost
         ✅ wire up Jenkins build/pipeline visibility via app-config.yaml
            plus an entity annotation
         ✅ wire up GitLab CI pipeline & merge-request visibility using the
            third-party immobiliarelabs plugin (there is no first-party one)
         ✅ reuse the shared ArgoCD config from Ch21 to surface GitOps sync
            status as CI/CD data, not just deployment data
         ✅ combine GitHub Actions + ArgoCD + Kubernetes signals into one
            aggregated dashboard card instead of four separate tabs

         CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the CBA Infrastructure domain (~22%) —
         wiring Backstage plugins to the CI/CD tools a team already runs.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 25.1 Visibility -> 25.2 GitHub Actions -> 25.3 Jenkins -> 25.4 GitLab CI -> │
         │ 25.5 ArgoCD (GitOps CD) -> 25.6 Aggregated Dashboard -> 25.7 Practice Qs    │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WHICH CI/CD PLUGIN FOR WHICH TOOL?                                               │
         │                                                                                   │
         │  IF YOUR PIPELINES RUN ON...     USE THIS PLUGIN                          SEE     │
         │  -------------------------------  ------------------------------------  ------    │
         │  GitHub Actions                    @backstage-community/plugin-github-    25.2    │
         │                                      actions                                      │
         │  Jenkins                             @backstage-community/plugin-jenkins   25.3   │
         │  GitLab CI                            @immobiliarelabs/backstage-plugin-    25.4  │
         │                                         gitlab                                    │
         │  ArgoCD / Flux (GitOps CD)             argocd plugin (shared with Ch 21)   25.5   │
         │                                                                                   │
         │  Most orgs run more than one of these - 25.6 shows how to combine them            │
         │  into a single aggregated card instead of 4 separate tabs.                        │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.1 CI/CD Visibility in Backstage
         - What users want: Build status, deploy status at a glance
         - Entity page CI/CD cards
         - Recent builds, success rate, duration
         - Deep links to CI/CD tools
         [CI/CD CARDS ON THE ENTITY PAGE - What You See]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CI/CD STATUS                                                        │
         │  ------------------------------------------------------------        │
         │  Build #142     main    2 min ago    3m 12s      PASSED              │
         │  Test  #142     main    2 min ago    1m 45s      PASSED              │
         │  Deploy #142    main    Running...                                   │
         │                                                                      │
         │  Click through -> takes you straight to the CI/CD tool's own UI      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.2 GitHub Actions Integration
         - Installing @backstage-community/plugin-github-actions (moved from the @backstage scope to @backstage-community in the April 2024 community-plugins migration)
         - Viewing workflow runs
         - Triggering workflows from Backstage
         - Workflow annotations for auto-discovery
         [GITHUB ACTIONS PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Install:                                                              │
         │  yarn --cwd packages/app add @backstage-community/plugin-github-actions  │
         │  # Annotation on the entity:                                             │
         │  metadata:                                                               │
         │    annotations:                                                          │
         │      github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost                             │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # That single annotation is enough - the plugin discovers workflow      │
         │  # runs automatically via the GitHub API using your integration token    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.3 Jenkins Integration
         - Installing @backstage-community/plugin-jenkins (moved from the @backstage scope to @backstage-community in the April 2024 community-plugins migration)
         - Viewing builds and pipelines
         - Triggering builds from Backstage
         [JENKINS PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                   │
         │  jenkins:                                            │
         │    baseUrl: https://jenkins.easypj.com               │
         │    username: ${JENKINS_USERNAME}                     │
         │    apiKey: ${JENKINS_API_KEY}                        │
         │                                                      │
         │  # Annotation on the entity                          │
         │  metadata:                                           │
         │    annotations:                                      │
         │      jenkins.io/job-full-name: easypj/jobpost-build  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.4 GitLab CI Integration
         - Installing @immobiliarelabs/backstage-plugin-gitlab (there is no first-party @backstage/plugin-gitlab; the Backstage plugin directory recommends this third-party package, which also requires @immobiliarelabs/backstage-plugin-gitlab-backend)
         - Pipeline status on entity pages
         - Merge request overview
         [GITLAB CI PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                            │
         │  integrations:                                │
         │    gitlab:                                    │
         │      - host: gitlab.com                       │
         │        token: ${GITLAB_TOKEN}                 │
         │                                               │
         │  # Annotation on the entity                   │
         │  metadata:                                    │
         │    annotations:                               │
         │      gitlab.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.5 ArgoCD / Flux (GitOps CD)
         - Viewing application sync status
         - Deployment history
         - Sync and rollback actions
         [ARGOCD PLUGIN - CI/CD Visibility Configuration]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Same argocd config block as 21.2 - the plugin is shared  │
         │  # between GitOps deployment status and CI/CD visibility    │
         │  argocd:                                                    │
         │    baseUrl: https://argocd.easypj.com                       │
         │  metadata:                                                  │
         │    annotations:                                             │
         │      argocd/app-name: jobpost                               │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    25.6 Building a CI/CD Dashboard for easypj
         - Aggregating CI/CD data from multiple sources
         - Custom cards for build metrics
         - Deployment frequency dashboard
         [CI/CD DASHBOARD FOR easypj - Aggregated Card]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // A custom card combining GitHub Actions + ArgoCD + K8s status  │
         │  export const CiCdSummaryCard = () => (                           │
         │    <InfoCard title='easypj CI/CD Summary'>                        │
         │      <GithubActionsCard />   {/* build/test status */}            │
         │      <ArgoCdCard />          {/* sync/deploy status */}           │
         │      <KubernetesCard />      {/* live pod health */}              │
         │    </InfoCard>                                                    │
         │  );                                                               │
         │                                                                   │
         │  # One glance: build passed, deploy synced, 3/3 pods running      │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         Every one of these cards works the same way under the hood: it
         reads an annotation off the entity (github.com/project-slug,
         jenkins.io/job-full-name, gitlab.com/project-slug, argocd/app-name)
         to learn WHICH external project to ask about, then calls that
         system's own API using credentials stored once in app-config.yaml
         (a token, an apiKey, a baseUrl). Because the lookup key lives on
         the entity and the credentials live in central config, the same
         easypj component can show GitHub Actions, Jenkins, GitLab CI and
         ArgoCD data side by side without any of those systems knowing about
         each other.

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 CI/CD visibility means build/deploy status, recent runs and
             deep links surfaced right on the entity page (25.1).
          🔑 GitHub Actions and Jenkins plugins both moved from the
             @backstage scope to @backstage-community in the April 2024
             community-plugins migration (25.2, 25.3).
          🔑 There is no first-party GitLab plugin — the Backstage plugin
             directory points to @immobiliarelabs/backstage-plugin-gitlab
             (+ its backend package) instead (25.4).
          🔑 The argocd plugin block is SHARED between GitOps deployment
             status (Ch21) and CI/CD visibility (25.5) — configure it once.
          🔑 A single aggregated card (25.6) combining GithubActionsCard +
             ArgoCdCard + KubernetesCard beats four separate dashboard tabs.

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Installing a plugin from the wrong npm scope (old @backstage/*
             instead of @backstage-community/*) — the community-plugins
             migration renamed several packages, not just moved a repo.
          ⚠️ Forgetting the entity annotation — the plugin has NOTHING to
             discover without github.com/project-slug (or the equivalent)
             on that specific entity.
          ⚠️ Assuming a first-party @backstage/plugin-gitlab exists — it
             does not; using the wrong package name silently fails install.
          ⚠️ Re-configuring ArgoCD twice (once for Ch21 GitOps, again here)
             instead of reusing the one argocd config block for both.

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Build the aggregated card (25.6) FIRST for any multi-tool org —
             it saves re-touching the entity page four separate times.
          ⚡ One annotation is usually enough per tool — check the plugin's
             README before adding config you don't need.
          ⚡ Reuse credentials/config blocks across plugins that talk to the
             same system (ArgoCD is the clearest example here).

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ yarn --cwd packages/app add @backstage-community/plugin-github-actions  │
         │ metadata.annotations: github.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost           │
         │                                                                         │
         │ app-config.yaml: jenkins: { baseUrl, username, apiKey }                 │
         │ metadata.annotations: jenkins.io/job-full-name: easypj/jobpost-build    │
         │                                                                         │
         │ app-config.yaml: integrations.gitlab: [{ host, token }]                 │
         │ metadata.annotations: gitlab.com/project-slug: easypj/jobpost           │
         │                                                                         │
         │ app-config.yaml: argocd.baseUrl (shared with Ch21 GitOps)               │
         │ metadata.annotations: argocd/app-name: jobpost                          │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 15 minutes: add the github.com/project-slug annotation to a
             test entity, install the GitHub Actions plugin, and confirm a
             workflow run appears on its entity page.
          🧪 Sketch which annotation each of the four plugins (25.2-25.5)
             reads, from memory, without looking back at this chapter.
          🧪 List the one config block that is shared between Ch21 (GitOps
             deploy status) and 25.5 (CI/CD visibility) and say why.

    25.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 25 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: CI/CD integration, plugins, dashboards

         VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 25.1 WHY: users want build/deploy status without leaving Backstage  │
         │         │                                                           │
         │         ▼                                                           │
         │ 25.2 GitHub Actions ─┐                                              │
         │ 25.3 Jenkins         ├──▶ 25.6 ONE aggregated CI/CD card per entity │
         │ 25.4 GitLab CI        │      (GithubActionsCard + ArgoCdCard +      │
         │ 25.5 ArgoCD (GitOps) ─┘       KubernetesCard, side by side)         │
         │                                                                     │
         │ One glance at the easypj entity page: build passed, deploy synced,  │
         │ 3/3 pods running - no tab-switching between four separate tools.    │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         .vs-prose — In words: 25.1 established WHY teams want CI/CD status
         on the entity page at all. 25.2-25.5 each wire up one tool (GitHub
         Actions, Jenkins, GitLab CI, ArgoCD) the same way — an annotation on
         the entity plus credentials in app-config.yaml. 25.6 is the payoff:
         instead of four cards or four tabs, easypj gets ONE aggregated
         summary card, so a glance at the entity page answers build status,
         deploy status and live pod health all at once.


Chapter 26: Production Deployment & Operations
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~50min · CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         The Backstage instance you've built so far still has training
         wheels: SQLite, maybe guest sign-in, config baked into YAML files.
         That's fine for learning — it is NOT fine the day easypj's whole
         engineering org depends on it. This chapter turns a working demo
         into something that survives real traffic, real upgrades and a
         real 3 AM incident.

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ run through the 7-item production checklist before any real
            launch (DB, auth, secrets, monitoring, backups, TLS, scaling)
         ✅ configure PostgreSQL as Backstage's production backend, including
            per-plugin database division
         ✅ configure TechDocs and Search for production scale instead of the
            local/in-memory defaults
         ✅ plan and execute a Backstage version upgrade safely, with a
            tested rollback path
         ✅ design a zero-downtime rolling deployment using readiness probes
         ✅ follow a disaster-recovery runbook in the right order instead of
            hot-fixing production by hand

         CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the CBA Infrastructure domain (~22%) —
         operating a production-grade Backstage deployment end to end.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 26.1 Checklist -> 26.2 PostgreSQL -> 26.3 TechDocs Prod -> 26.4 Search    │
         │ Prod -> 26.5 Upgrades -> 26.6 Zero-Downtime -> 26.7 DR Runbook -> 26.8 Qs │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.1 Production Checklist
         - Database: PostgreSQL (not SQLite)
         - Auth: SSO (not guest access)
         - Secrets: External secret management
         - Monitoring: Logs + metrics + alerts
         - Backups: Database backup strategy
         - SSL/TLS: HTTPS everywhere
         - Scaling: Multiple replicas, resource limits
         [PRODUCTION CHECKLIST]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ITEM              REQUIREMENT                                           │
         │  -----------------  ------------------------------------------------     │
         │  Database            PostgreSQL, NEVER SQLite                            │
         │  Auth                 Real SSO provider, NEVER guest access              │
         │  Secrets               K8s Secrets or vault, NEVER hardcoded in YAML     │
         │  Monitoring             Prometheus + Grafana + Alertmanager wired up     │
         │  Backups                  Automated PostgreSQL backup + tested restore   │
         │  TLS                        HTTPS everywhere, valid certificates         │
         │  Scaling                      3+ replicas, resource requests/limits set  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.2 Database — PostgreSQL Setup
         - Why PostgreSQL for production
         - Connection configuration
         - Migration management
         - Backup and restore
         [POSTGRESQL PRODUCTION CONFIGURATION]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  backend:                                                              │
         │    database:                                                           │
         │      client: pg                                                        │
         │      connection:                                                       │
         │        host: ${POSTGRES_HOST}                                          │
         │        port: 5432                                                      │
         │        user: ${POSTGRES_USER}                                          │
         │        password: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}                                  │
         │        ssl: { rejectUnauthorized: true }                               │
         │      pluginDivisionMode: database   # DEFAULT: own DB per plugin       │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.3 TechDocs in Production
         - Production publishers: Google Cloud Storage, AWS S3, Azure Blob
         - Builder: Docker-based for security isolation
         - Storage lifecycle: Cleanup and retention
         [PRODUCTION TECHDOCS CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  techdocs:                                                           │
         │    builder: external          # CI/CD builds, not Backstage itself   │
         │    generator: { runIn: docker }                                      │
         │    publisher:                                                        │
         │      type: awsS3                                                     │
         │      awsS3: { bucketName: easypj-techdocs-prod, region: us-east-1 }  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.4 Search in Production
         - Elasticsearch or OpenSearch for production search
         - Search index configuration
         - Reindexing schedule
         [SEARCH CONFIGURATION - Production]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  search:                                                                 │
         │    collators: { catalog: { schedule: { frequency: { minutes: 10 } } } }  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # For production scale, swap the default in-memory Lunr index for       │
         │  # @backstage/plugin-search-backend-module-elasticsearch or OpenSearch   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.5 Backstage Upgrades
         - Upgrade strategy: Minor vs Major versions
         - The Backstage upgrade helper
         - Testing upgrades before production
         - Rollback strategy
         [UPGRADE CHECKLIST]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP                                            WHY                        │
         │  -----------------------------------------------  -------------------       │
         │  Run the backstage-cli versions:bump              Updates all @backstage    │
         │                                                   packages together         │
         │  Read the upgrade helper diff                       Shows breaking config   │
         │  (backstage.github.io/upgrade-helper)             changes to apply          │
         │  Test in a staging environment first                 Catches breakage       │
         │  Run yarn tsc + yarn test                              before it hits prod  │
         │  Deploy during a low-traffic window                     Minimizes impact    │
         │  Have a rollback plan (previous image tag ready)          Safety net        │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.6 Zero-Downtime Deployment Strategy
         - Rolling updates so users never see a gap
         - Readiness probes are what make this safe
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Deployment - rolling update with a readiness gate                     │
         │  spec:                                                                   │
         │    replicas: 3                                                           │
         │    strategy:                                                             │
         │      type: RollingUpdate                                                 │
         │      rollingUpdate: { maxUnavailable: 0, maxSurge: 1 }                   │
         │    template:                                                             │
         │      spec:                                                               │
         │        containers:                                                       │
         │          - name: backstage                                               │
         │            readinessProbe:                                               │
         │              httpGet: { path: /healthcheck, port: 7007 }                 │
         │              initialDelaySeconds: 10                                     │
         │              periodSeconds: 5                                            │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # maxUnavailable: 0 means the old pod is only killed AFTER the new one  │
         │  # passes its readiness probe - zero requests are ever dropped.          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    26.7 Disaster Recovery Runbook
         - What to do when production Backstage goes down hard
         - Order of operations, not a wall of options
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. Confirm the blast radius: is it Backstage, or is PostgreSQL itself down?   │
         │  2. If DB is down: restore the latest automated PostgreSQL snapshot (26.2)     │
         │  3. If Backstage pods are crash-looping: roll back to the last known-good      │
         │     image tag via ArgoCD/GitOps revert (Ch 21) - do NOT hotfix in prod         │
         │  4. Re-run the upgrade helper diff (26.5) if the crash follows a version bump  │
         │  5. Once healthy: verify Catalog count matches pre-incident baseline           │
         │  6. Write the incident timeline while it is fresh - feed it into 27.x alerts   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The zero-downtime rollout in 26.6 only works because of how a
         RollingUpdate and a readinessProbe cooperate: with maxUnavailable:
         0, Kubernetes is not allowed to kill an old easypj pod until a new
         one has come up AND passed its readinessProbe against /healthcheck
         on port 7007. Until that probe returns healthy, the Service (26.2's
         PostgreSQL-backed backend included) keeps routing 100% of traffic
         to the old, still-working pods. Skip the readinessProbe and
         maxUnavailable: 0 buys you nothing — Kubernetes has no way to know
         the new pod isn't ready yet, so it may still drop requests.

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 The production checklist (26.1) is the CONTRACT — database,
             auth, secrets, monitoring, backups, TLS and scaling all have to
             be real before you call it "production."
          🔑 PostgreSQL (26.2) replaces SQLite because SQLite is a single
             local file — it cannot be shared safely by multiple replicas.
          🔑 pluginDivisionMode: database (the default) gives every plugin
             its own separate PostgreSQL database; set it to schema
             instead if you need every plugin sharing one database via
             separate schemas.
          🔑 TechDocs (26.3) and Search (26.4) both need to move off their
             local defaults — external builder + cloud storage for docs,
             Elasticsearch/OpenSearch instead of in-memory Lunr for search.
          🔑 Upgrades (26.5) follow a fixed order: bump versions, read the
             upgrade helper diff, test in staging, then deploy in a
             low-traffic window with a rollback plan ready.
          🔑 Zero-downtime (26.6) needs BOTH maxUnavailable: 0 AND a real
             readinessProbe — neither one alone is enough.
          🔑 The DR runbook (26.7) is an ORDER of operations, not a menu —
             confirm blast radius first, never hot-fix production directly.

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Treating SQLite as "good enough for now" in production — it
             cannot be shared across replicas and has no real backup story.
          ⚠️ Forgetting the readinessProbe when trying for zero-downtime —
             maxUnavailable: 0 without it does not guarantee no dropped
             requests.
          ⚠️ Leaving TechDocs on its local/dev builder in production instead
             of switching to builder: external with a cloud publisher.
          ⚠️ Skipping the DR runbook order and trying to fix a crash-looping
             deployment by hand instead of rolling back via GitOps (Ch21).

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Run the 26.1 checklist as a literal gate before any go-live —
             treat any unchecked row as a launch blocker.
          ⚡ Always test an upgrade in staging with the upgrade helper diff
             BEFORE touching production (26.5).
          ⚡ Write the DR runbook (26.7) BEFORE you need it — a 3 AM incident
             is the worst time to design a recovery order from scratch.

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ backend.database: { client: pg, connection: {...}, ssl.rejectUnauthorized }     │
         │ techdocs: { builder: external, generator.runIn: docker, publisher.type: awsS3 } │
         │ search.collators.catalog.schedule.frequency.minutes: 10                         │
         │ backstage-cli versions:bump              # bump all @backstage packages         │
         │ spec.strategy: { type: RollingUpdate, rollingUpdate: { maxUnavailable: 0,       │
         │   maxSurge: 1 } }, readinessProbe: { httpGet: /healthcheck :7007 }              │
         │ DR order: confirm blast radius -> restore PG snapshot -> ArgoCD revert ->       │
         │   re-check upgrade diff -> verify Catalog count -> write the timeline           │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 15 minutes: write out the 7-item production checklist (26.1)
             from memory, then check it against this chapter.
          🧪 Walk through the DR runbook (26.7) out loud for a scenario
             where easypj pods are crash-looping after a version bump.
          🧪 Explain in one sentence why maxUnavailable: 0 alone does not
             guarantee zero dropped requests.

    26.8 CBA Exam: Chapter 26 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Production setup, database, upgrades

         VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 26.1 Checklist (DB/Auth/Secrets/Monitoring/Backups/TLS/Scaling)           │
         │         │ each item becomes a config block:                               │
         │         ▼                                                                 │
         │ 26.2 PostgreSQL   26.3 TechDocs(S3)   26.4 Search(scale)   26.5 Upgrades  │
         │         └─────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────┘            │
         │                               │                                           │
         │                               ▼                                           │
         │          26.6 Zero-downtime rollout (maxUnavailable:0 + readinessProbe)   │
         │                               │                                           │
         │                               ▼                                           │
         │                 26.7 DR Runbook — the plan for when it breaks anyway      │
         │                                                                           │
         │ The checklist (26.1) is the contract; 26.2-26.5 satisfy each line item;   │

         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         .vs-prose — In words: the production checklist (26.1) is the goal;
         everything else in this chapter exists to satisfy one of its rows.
         PostgreSQL (26.2), production TechDocs (26.3) and production Search
         (26.4) satisfy the database/storage rows. Safe upgrades (26.5) keep
         the checklist true over time instead of just on day one. Zero-
         downtime rollout (26.6) is HOW you deploy changes without breaking
         the checklist mid-deploy. And the DR runbook (26.7) is what you
         follow on the day something breaks anyway.


Chapter 27: Monitoring, Logging & Observability
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~35min · CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         A production easypj Backstage instance (Ch26) can still fail
         silently — a slow catalog ingestion, a spike in 5xx errors, a
         certificate about to expire — unless something is actually
         watching. This chapter wires up the metrics, logs, alerts and
         usage analytics that turn "I hope it's fine" into "I know it's
         fine, and here's the dashboard that proves it."

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ locate Backstage's real health-check routes and explain why
            /healthcheck is not guaranteed to exist by default
         ✅ explain where Backstage exposes Prometheus-style metrics today
            (OpenTelemetry on :9464) versus the older /api/metrics path
         ✅ configure structured JSON logging at the right log level
         ✅ write an Alertmanager rule that pages on-call when Backstage
            goes down
         ✅ identify which usage-analytics metrics tell you if easypj's
            Backstage instance is actually being adopted, not just running

         CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the CBA Infrastructure domain (~22%) —
         operating and observing a live Backstage deployment.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 27.1 Monitoring Backstage Itself -> 27.2 Logging -> 27.3 Alerting -> │
         │ 27.4 Analytics -> 27.5 Practice Qs                                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    27.1 Monitoring Backstage Itself
         - Health check endpoint: /healthcheck
         - Note: the current default Root Health Service exposes readiness/liveness
           at /.backstage/health/v1/readiness and /.backstage/health/v1/liveness
           (JSON {"status":200,"payload":{"status":"ok"}}); /healthcheck is only
           present if you registered it yourself as a custom backend route
         - Prometheus metrics: the officially recommended path is OpenTelemetry-based
           instrumentation, which exposes metrics on a separate port at
           localhost:9464/metrics by default (not on the main API under /api/metrics);
           the older prom-client-based /metrics endpoint is being phased out
         - Key metrics: Request rate, error rate, latency
         - Catalog ingestion metrics
         [CODE BLOCK: Prometheus configuration]

         [REAL-LIFE: Setting Up Basic Monitoring for Backstage]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Note: /healthcheck below is easypj's own custom-registered│
         │  # route, not a Backstage default (see 27.1 for the real     │
         │  # defaults: /.backstage/health/v1/readiness and /liveness). │
         │  $ curl https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck             │
         │  {"status":"ok"}  ← First thing to check when debugging      │
         │                                                              │
         │  # /api/metrics below is the OLDER, legacy path (27.1) — the │
         │  # current default is OpenTelemetry on :9464/metrics.        │
         │  $ curl https://backstage.easypj.com/api/metrics             │
         │  # Returns metrics like:                                     │
         │  backstage_http_requests_total{status="200"} 15423           │
         │  backstage_http_request_duration_seconds_count 15423         │
         │  catalog_entity_count{kind="Component"} 47                   │
         │  catalog_ingestion_duration_seconds_sum 12.5                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  # Quick Health Check Script (add to your CI/CD):            │
         │  #!/bin/bash                                                 │
         │  STATUS=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" \          │
         │    https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck)                 │
         │  if [ "$STATUS" != "200" ]; then                             │
         │    echo "❌ Backstage is DOWN! Status: $STATUS"              │
         │    exit 1                                                    │
         │  fi                                                          │
         │  echo "✅ Backstage is healthy"                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Backstage logging configuration
         - Structured logging (JSON)

         │                                                                     │

    27.3 Alerting
         - Setting up alerts for Backstage
         - Critical metrics to alert on
         - PagerDuty/OpsGenie integration
         [ALERT CONFIGURATION - Prometheus Alertmanager]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # alertmanager-rules.yaml                              │
         │  groups:                                                │
         │    - name: backstage                                    │
         │      rules:                                             │
         │        - alert: BackstageDown                           │
         │          expr: up{job='backstage'} == 0                 │
         │          for: 2m                                        │
         │          labels: { severity: critical }                 │
         │          annotations: { summary: 'Backstage is down' }  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    27.4 Backstage Analytics
         - Plugin usage analytics
         - Most viewed entities
         - Template usage metrics
         - Search analytics
         [KEY ANALYTICS TO TRACK]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  METRIC                    WHY IT MATTERS                            │
         │  ------------------------  --------------------------------------    │
         │  Weekly Active Users        Is anyone actually using Backstage?      │
         │  Most-viewed entities        Which services get the most attention   │
         │  Template executions/week     Self-service adoption indicator        │
         │  Search queries/user            Discovery behavior, catalog health   │
         │  TechDocs page views              Documentation actually being read  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         Backstage's health story has quietly shifted twice: the older
         prom-client-based /metrics (or /api/metrics) endpoint is being
         phased out in favor of OpenTelemetry-based instrumentation, which
         exposes metrics on a SEPARATE port (localhost:9464/metrics by
         default) rather than under the main API. Similarly, /healthcheck is
         not a built-in route at all — the current default Root Health
         Service exposes readiness and liveness under
         /.backstage/health/v1/readiness and /liveness; /healthcheck only
         exists if a team registers it themselves as a custom backend route
         (as easypj does in the examples in 27.1). Knowing which endpoint is
         actually wired up matters more than assuming the "obvious" one
         exists.

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 /healthcheck is NOT guaranteed by default — the built-in routes
             are /.backstage/health/v1/readiness and /liveness (27.1).
          🔑 The officially recommended metrics path today is OpenTelemetry
             on a separate port (:9464), not the older /api/metrics (27.1).
          🔑 Structured JSON logging (27.2) is what makes shipping logs to
             Loki/ELK practical — Backstage itself just writes to stdout.
          🔑 Alertmanager rules (27.3) should cover at minimum: Backstage
             down, high error rate, and stalled catalog ingestion.
          🔑 Usage analytics (27.4) — weekly active users, most-viewed
             entities, template runs, search queries — answer a DIFFERENT
             question than uptime: is anyone actually using this platform?

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Assuming /healthcheck always exists — it is only present if
             someone registered it as a custom route.
          ⚠️ Looking for Prometheus metrics under /api/metrics and missing
             the OpenTelemetry endpoint on :9464 that current docs point to.
          ⚠️ Confusing readiness with liveness — they answer different
             questions ("can it take traffic" vs "is it alive at all").
          ⚠️ Treating monitoring (is it up) and analytics (is it used) as
             the same thing — you need both, for different reasons.

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Check the health endpoint FIRST when debugging — it is the
             fastest signal for "is Backstage even up."
          ⚡ Ship logs as JSON from day one (27.2) — retrofitting structured
             logging later means rewriting every log call.
          ⚡ Alert on catalog ingestion failures, not just HTTP errors — a
             silently stale catalog is a real production failure mode.

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ curl https://backstage.easypj.com/healthcheck        # {"status":"ok"}          │
         │ GET /.backstage/health/v1/readiness | /liveness       # default health routes   │
         │ OpenTelemetry metrics: localhost:9464/metrics         # separate port, not /api │
         │ backend.logger: { level: info, format: json }         # error|warn|info|debug   │
         │ alertmanager rule: expr: up{job='backstage'} == 0, for: 2m, severity: critical  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 10 minutes: curl your own Backstage instance's readiness and
             liveness routes and confirm you get the expected JSON back.
          🧪 Write one Alertmanager rule from memory that fires when
             Backstage has been down for 2 minutes.
          🧪 Name three usage-analytics metrics (27.4) and explain what each
             one tells a platform team that uptime metrics do not.

         │                                   ▼                                         │
         │                     🔔 PagerDuty / Slack / Email when a threshold trips     │

         .vs-prose — In words: 27.1's health checks and metrics, 27.2's
         structured logs and 27.3's alert rules all feed the same
         operational stack (Prometheus/Loki-style aggregation) so that when
         something breaks, on-call gets paged instead of finding out from a
         user complaint. 27.4's analytics answer a completely different
         question on the SAME dashboards: not "is it up" but "is anyone
         actually using it" — the two questions every platform team needs
         answered about easypj's Backstage instance.


  🔑 PART 9 — KEY TAKEAWAYS
   • CI/CD visibility belongs ON the entity page — GitHub Actions, Jenkins,
     GitLab CI and ArgoCD can all be surfaced without leaving Backstage.
   • The community-plugins migration (April 2024) moved several plugins from
     the @backstage scope to @backstage-community — install the right one.
   • Production Backstage means PostgreSQL, real SSO, external secrets, real
     monitoring, tested backups, HTTPS and multiple replicas — never SQLite
     or guest access.
   • pluginDivisionMode: database is the default — each plugin gets its own
     schema/DB inside the same PostgreSQL instance.
   • Zero-downtime deploys depend on BOTH a RollingUpdate strategy AND a real
     readinessProbe — maxUnavailable: 0 alone is not enough.
   • A written Disaster Recovery runbook (a fixed order of operations) turns
     a 3 AM outage into a checklist instead of a panic.
   • Observability = metrics (Prometheus/OTel) + logs (Loki/ELK) + alerts
     (Alertmanager) + usage analytics — the first three tell you it's UP,
     the last tells you if it's actually being adopted.

  [PART 9 VISUAL SUMMARY]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 📝 git push  ──▶  🔧 CI builds & tests  ──▶  🚀 CD ships to prod           │

  │                          Jenkins/GitLab CI, visible        update, 0       │
  │                          on the entity page)               downtime)       │

  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  .vs-prose — Part 9 takes easypj from "code that merges" to "code that is
  running in front of real users and being watched." Chapter 25 gives every
  commit a visible, trusted path from git push to a green build. Chapter 26
  hardens the destination that build lands on — a real database, safe
  upgrades, a zero-downtime rollout and a rehearsed recovery plan. Chapter 27
  closes the loop: metrics, logs and alerts tell you the instant something
  breaks, and analytics tell you whether the platform is even being used.
  Together they are the difference between a Backstage demo and a Backstage
  a whole engineering org can depend on.

PART 10: ADVANCED TOPICS & CBA EXAM PREPARATION                          🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    [PART 10 INTRO]
    By the end of Part 9 easypj's Backstage instance is wired for production:
    CI/CD pipelines register services automatically, it runs on Kubernetes,
    real SSO and RBAC gate every action, and monitoring watches it around the
    clock. Part 10 is the final stretch of the guide, and it does three
    distinct jobs. Chapters 28-30 finish hardening what you already deployed -
    driving it by code instead of by hand (REST/Catalog/Scaffolder APIs,
    webhooks, a small internal CLI), making it survive real load (horizontal
    scaling, HA, caching, capacity planning), and locking it down (the
    security checklist, secret management, a STRIDE-style threat model).
    Chapters 31 and 32 then turn everything you have built into a graded
    credential, and beyond.

    Chapter 31 is not new Backstage material - it is exam craft: the CBA's
    exact format and rules, its official domain weights, a full 30-question
    practice exam with worked explanations, and an 8-week study plan mapped
    onto the ten parts you have just finished. Chapter 32 exists for a
    different reason: it takes you past what the CBA grades - custom catalog
    processors, dynamic plugins, multi-tenancy, and the New Backend System -
    so passing the exam is a checkpoint on easypj's journey, not the end of
    it.

    🧠 ANALOGY: think of Part 10 as the final weeks before a pilot's
    type-rating checkride. Chapters 28-30 are the last flight hours logged -
    wiring the automation, stress-testing the airframe, locking the cockpit
    down. Chapter 31 is the checkride itself: a known format, a known score
    to beat, and a study plan instead of guesswork. Chapter 32 is what a
    newly certified captain reads next - routes the rating alone will never
    fly for you.

    [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 10 map]
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ Ch28 APIs & Automation -> Ch29 Scale & HA -> Ch30 Security     │
    │     drive it by code        keep it running      keep it safe  │
    │              |                                          |      │
    │              +-----------------------+------------------+      │
    │                                       v                        │
    │               Ch31 CBA EXAM PREP (prove you learned all of it) │
    │                                       |                        │
    │                                       v                        │
    │               Ch32 BEYOND CBA (where the road keeps going)     │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Read the map top to bottom: automate easypj's platform (28), then make
    what you automated survive load (29) and stay locked down (30) - only
    once the platform is genuinely production-hardened does exam prep (31)
    mean anything, and only once you have passed does chasing what lies
    beyond (32) make sense.

    🧭 DECISION GUIDE — where do I focus in Part 10?
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ If you...                            │ Then...                                 │
    ├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
    │ already run Backstage in prod and    │ skim Ch28-30 as a refresher and         │
    │ just want CBA exam credit            │ spend most of your time in Ch31         │
    │ haven't wired CI/CD or the REST      │ read Ch28-30 closely first - Ch31's     │
    │ API into your own instance yet       │ practice exam assumes that context      │
    │ are cramming the week before the     │ jump straight to 31.2 (weights),        │
    │ CBA exam                             │ 31.4 (practice exam), 31.5 (checklist)  │
    │ already passed CBA, or don't need    │ skip Ch31's exam mechanics and go       │
    │ the credential                       │ straight to Ch32 (Beyond CBA)           │
    └──────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [PART 10 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION  — read before the chapters]
    By the end of this part you will have automated at least one easypj
    workflow through the Scaffolder/Catalog REST APIs, hardened and scaled
    the same instance you built in Parts 7-9, taken and self-scored a full
    30-question CBA practice exam, and be able to explain how a custom
    CatalogProcessor or the New Backend System would extend easypj's
    Backstage instance beyond what today's exam even tests.

Chapter 28: Backstage APIs & Automation 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~45min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         easypj's platform team is tired of clicking the same buttons for
         every new service — registering it in the Catalog, running the
         same Scaffolder template, checking whether its build passed.
         Earlier chapters taught you what the Catalog and Scaffolder DO
         from the UI; this chapter teaches you to drive those same actions
         from a script, a CI pipeline, or a chat bot instead of a mouse.

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ list the five core Backstage REST APIs (Catalog, Scaffolder,
            Search, TechDocs, Auth) and what each one is for
         ✅ query and manage entities through the Catalog API, including
            why there is no direct POST /api/catalog/entities endpoint
         ✅ trigger a Scaffolder template run from curl and poll its task
            until it completes
         ✅ automate registration and template runs from CI/CD using a
            narrowly-scoped static backend token
         ✅ react to catalog events with the EventsService instead of
            polling the API on a timer
         ✅ wrap the REST API in a small internal CLI for a one-line
            "register this repo" command

         CBA RELEVANCE: tagged CBA 32% (Customizing) — the single largest
         domain on the exam, and this chapter's APIs/automation content is
         a core part of it.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 28.1 REST APIs -> 28.2 Catalog API -> 28.3 Scaffolder API -> 28.4         │
         │ Automating w/ CI/CD -> 28.5 Webhooks/Events -> 28.6 Custom CLI -> 28.7 Qs │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.1 Backstage REST API Overview
         - Catalog API: CRUD operations on entities
         - Scaffolder API: Trigger templates programmatically
         - Search API: Query the search index
         - TechDocs API: Access documentation data
         - Auth API: Token management
         [API REFERENCE TABLE]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  API              KEY ENDPOINT                              PURPOSE                     │
         │  ---------------  ----------------------------------------  ------------------          │
         │  Catalog           GET /api/catalog/entities                  List/query entities       │
         │  Catalog            GET /api/catalog/entities/by-name/:k/:n/:m Get one entity           │
         │  Scaffolder           POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks              Run a template         │
         │  Search                 GET /api/search/query                       Full-text search    │
         │  TechDocs                 GET /api/techdocs/static/docs/:ns/:k/:n     Fetch built docs  │
         │  Auth                       GET /api/auth/:provider/start                Begin sign-in  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.2 Using the Catalog API
         - GET /api/catalog/entities — List all entities
         - GET /api/catalog/entities/by-name/:kind/:namespace/:name — Get entity
         - Note: there is NO direct POST /api/catalog/entities endpoint - the official Catalog API docs list no such route; entities are created by registering a Location (POST /api/catalog/locations) that points at a catalog-info.yaml
         - DELETE /api/catalog/entities/by-uid/:uid — Delete entity
         - GET /api/catalog/entity-facets — Get facet data
         [CATALOG API - Real curl Examples]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # List all entities                                                         │
         │  curl localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities                                    │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Get one specific entity                                                   │
         │  curl localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities/by-name/component/default/jobpost  │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Register a new entity via a Location (catalog has no direct               │
         │  # POST /entities endpoint - see software-catalog-api docs)                  │
         │  curl -X POST localhost:7007/api/catalog/locations \                         │
         │    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \                                     │
         │    -d '{"type":"url","target":"https://github.com/org/repo/'                 │
         │         blob/main/catalog-info.yaml"}'                                       │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Delete an entity by uid                                                   │
         │  curl -X DELETE localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities/by-uid/abc-123           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.3 Scaffolder REST API
         - POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks — Execute a template
         - GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId — Get task status
         - GET /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId/eventstream — Stream task events
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Trigger a template run                                                              │
         │  curl -X POST localhost:7007/api/scaffolder/v2/tasks \                                 │
         │    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \                                               │
         │    -d '{"templateRef":"template:default/django-service","values":{"name":"new-svc"}}'  │
         │                                                                                        │
         │  # Poll the task status                                                                │
         │  curl localhost:7007/api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/a1b2c3d4-...                              │
         │                                                                                        │
         │  # Stream live task events (build logs as they happen)                                 │
         │  curl -N localhost:7007/api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/a1b2c3d4-.../eventstream               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.4 Automating Backstage with APIs
         - Auto-registering services via CI/CD
         - Triggering templates from Slack bots

         [REAL-LIFE: API Automation Examples]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Register a new entity via CI/CD (register a Location -    │
         │  # there is no direct POST /entities create endpoint)        │
         │  $ curl -X POST \                                            │
         │    https://backstage.easypj.com/api/catalog/locations \      │
         │    -H "Authorization: Bearer $BACKSTAGE_TOKEN" \             │
         │    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \                     │
         │    -d '{"type":"url","target":"https://github.com/org/'      │
         │         new-svc/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml"}'               │
         │                                                              │
         │  # Trigger a template from CI/CD                             │
         │  $ curl -X POST \                                            │
         │    https://backstage.easypj.com/api/scaffolder/v2/tasks \    │
         │    -H "Authorization: Bearer $BACKSTAGE_TOKEN" \             │
         │    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \                     │
         │    -d '{"templateRef":"template:default/django-service",'    │
         │         "values":{"name":"new-svc","owner":"team-platform"}}'│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         - Syncing external data into Catalog
         - Building automation scripts
         [CONFIGURING THE STATIC TOKEN USED ABOVE]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # How the CI/CD static token above is actually configured on the backend     │
         │  backend:                                                                     │
         │    auth:                                                                      │
         │      externalAccess:                                                          │
         │        - type: static                                                         │
         │          options:                                                             │
         │            token: ${CICD_TOKEN}                                               │
         │            subject: cicd-system                                               │
         │            accessRestrictions:                                                │
         │              - plugin: catalog                                                │
         │              - plugin: scaffolder                                             │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [SYNCING EXTERNAL DATA INTO THE CATALOG]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // A scheduled script that pulls data from an internal CMDB     │
         │  // and writes catalog-info.yaml files via catalog:write, or     │
         │  // POSTs directly to /api/catalog/entities on a cron schedule.  │
         │  // This is how organizations bridge legacy inventory systems    │
         │  // into Backstage without a native EntityProvider.              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.5 Webhooks and Event-Driven Automation
         - Reacting to Backstage events instead of polling the API
         - Firing a webhook when a new Component is registered
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # There is no global catalogEventBus API in Backstage. Event-driven            │
         │  # automation is built on the EventsService (@backstage/plugin-events-node,     │
         │  # ref: eventsServiceRef), which supports publish() and subscribe() per topic.  │
         │                                                                                 │
         │  # External systems can push events in over HTTP with no custom bus:            │
         │  #   POST /api/events/http/{topic}  (see @backstage/plugin-events-backend)      │
         │                                                                                 │
         │  # The signals plugin (real-time UI push) needs NO experimentalFeatures         │
         │  # flag - once @backstage/plugin-signals(-backend) is installed it is           │
         │  # auto-discovered, no extra config required.                                   │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    28.6 Building a Custom CLI Tool Against the Backstage API
         - Wrapping the REST API in a small internal CLI
         - Real example: a one-line "register this repo" command
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  #!/usr/bin/env node                                                                 │
         │  // easypj-cli register <repo-url> --owner team-platform                             │
         │  const [,, cmd, repoUrl, , owner] = process.argv;                                    │
         │  if (cmd === "register") {                                                           │
         │    await fetch("https://backstage.easypj.com/api/catalog/locations", {               │
         │      method: "POST",                                                                 │
         │      headers: { Authorization: "Bearer " + process.env.BACKSTAGE_TOKEN,              │
         │                 "Content-Type": "application/json" },                                │
         │      body: JSON.stringify({ type: "url", target: repoUrl + "/catalog-info.yaml" }),  │
         │    });                                                                               │
         │    console.log("Registered " + repoUrl + " (owner: " + owner + ")");                 │
         │  }                                                                                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         Every one of these five APIs sits behind the same Backstage
         backend (port 7007 in dev). The Catalog API has no direct create
         endpoint on purpose — creation always flows through registering a
         Location that points at a catalog-info.yaml, so the same
         validation/processing pipeline from Chapter 7 runs no matter how
         the entity arrived. The Scaffolder API is task-based rather than
         synchronous: POST creates a task, and you poll its taskId or
         stream its eventstream for progress, because template runs can
         take minutes. Automation (28.4) is not a separate mechanism at
         all — it is the exact same API calls a human makes, just carrying
         a Bearer token instead of a browser session, scoped narrowly via
         backend.auth.externalAccess.accessRestrictions so a leaked token
         cannot call every plugin. Webhooks and events (28.5) flip the
         model from polling to push, built on the EventsService's
         publish()/subscribe() rather than any global "catalogEventBus."
         And the CLI in 28.6 is nothing more than a thin wrapper calling
         fetch() against those same endpoints — convenience, not new power.

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 Five REST APIs — Catalog, Scaffolder, Search, TechDocs, Auth —
             cover everything the Backstage UI can do (28.1).
          🔑 There is no direct POST /api/catalog/entities — entities are
             created by registering a Location that points at a
             catalog-info.yaml (28.2).
          🔑 The Scaffolder API is task-based: POST creates a task, then
             you poll /tasks/:taskId or stream /eventstream for progress
             (28.3).
          🔑 Automation (28.4) is the same API calls a human makes, just
             with a Bearer token — scope that token narrowly with
             backend.auth.externalAccess.accessRestrictions.
          🔑 Event-driven automation (28.5) is built on the EventsService
             (publish()/subscribe()), and external systems can push events
             in over POST /api/events/http/{topic} with no custom bus.
          🔑 A custom CLI (28.6) is just a thin wrapper — same endpoints,
             same token, one command instead of a multi-line curl.

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Assuming a first-party POST /api/catalog/entities exists — it
             does not; register a Location instead.
          ⚠️ Forgetting the Scaffolder API is asynchronous — a single POST
             does not return the finished result; you must poll the taskId
             or stream its events.
          ⚠️ Giving a CI/CD static token unrestricted access instead of
             scoping it with accessRestrictions to only the plugins it
             actually needs.
          ⚠️ Thinking event-driven automation needs an experimentalFeatures
             flag — the signals plugin auto-discovers once installed, no
             flag required.

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Sanity-check any integration with a plain curl against the
             endpoint before writing automation code around it.
          ⚡ Reach for webhooks/events (28.5) before building a polling
             loop against the API — it is cheaper and faster to react to.
          ⚡ Wrap repeated curl incantations in a tiny internal CLI (28.6)
             once more than one teammate needs to run them.

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ GET  /api/catalog/entities                         # list all entities         │
         │ GET  /api/catalog/entities/by-name/:kind/:ns/:name  # get one entity           │
         │ POST /api/catalog/locations {type:url,target}       # register (NOT /entities) │
         │ POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks {templateRef,values}  # run a template           │
         │ GET  /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks/:taskId               # poll task status         │
         │ POST /api/events/http/{topic}                       # push events, no bus      │
         │ backend.auth.externalAccess: [{type: static, options:{token,subject,           │
         │   accessRestrictions}}]                             # scope a CI/CD token      │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 15 minutes: curl the Catalog API to list entities, then
             trigger a Scaffolder template run and poll its taskId until
             it completes.
          🧪 Configure a static externalAccess token scoped to only the
             catalog and scaffolder plugins, and explain why that scoping
             matters.
          🧪 Sketch the one-line CLI command from 28.6 you would build for
             the task your own team runs most often.

         VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
