         .vs-prose — In words: 28.1 laid out the five REST APIs that back
         everything the Backstage UI does. 28.2-28.3 show the two you'll
         call most — Catalog (query/register) and Scaffolder (run
         templates) — and 28.4 shows those same calls driven by CI/CD
         instead of a human, secured with a narrowly-scoped token. 28.5
         flips from polling to reacting via events, and 28.6 packages it
         all into one small internal CLI so a teammate never has to
         remember a curl incantation.


Chapter 29: Performance, Scaling & High Availability
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~40min · CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         easypj started as a small pilot. This chapter is about what
         happens once every engineering team is actually using it —
         hundreds of catalog entities, thousands of requests a day, and
         the very real risk of one un-cached lookup or one under-sized
         replica set taking the whole platform down. This is not premature
         optimization; it's the difference between easypj surviving its
         own success and falling over the day adoption finally takes off.

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ diagnose which layer — frontend, backend, catalog, or search —
            is actually the bottleneck before reaching for a fix
         ✅ scale Backstage horizontally using stateless replicas behind a
            load balancer
         ✅ design a highly-available deployment across replicas, zones,
            and a replicated PostgreSQL
         ✅ configure Redis caching for sessions, catalog lookups, and
            plugin API responses
         ✅ load test easypj's Catalog API with k6 before real traffic
            finds the limit
         ✅ right-size replicas, database tier, and cache for a given org
            size using the capacity-planning table

         CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the CBA Infrastructure domain (~22%) —
         keeping a production Backstage instance fast and available under
         real load.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 29.1 Perf Optimization -> 29.2 Horizontal Scaling -> 29.3 High                │
         │ Availability -> 29.4 Caching -> 29.5 Load Testing -> 29.6 Capacity -> 29.7 Qs │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.1 Backstage Performance Optimization
         - Frontend: Code splitting, lazy loading, caching
         - Backend: Database query optimization, connection pooling
         - Catalog: Processing pipeline optimization
         - Search: Index optimization
         [PERFORMANCE OPTIMIZATION TIPS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAYER       TIP                                                     │
         │  ----------  -----------------------------------------------------   │
         │  Frontend     Code-split routes, lazy-load plugin pages              │
         │  Backend       Connection pool tuning, avoid N+1 catalog queries     │
         │  Catalog        Tune processing interval, limit provider scan scope  │
         │  Search           Reindex off-peak, cap collator batch size          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SCALING TRIGGERS — WHEN TO PULL WHICH LEVER                                 │
         │                                                                              │
         │  SYMPTOM                                  PULL THIS LEVER                    │
         │  ----------------------------------------  --------------------------------  │
         │  High request latency, CPU maxed            Horizontal scaling (29.2)        │
         │  Repeated identical catalog/API lookups       Caching (29.4)                 │
         │  Slow catalog processing, large entity count    Processing tuning (29.1)     │
         │  Single point of failure, zone outage risk        High Availability (29.3)   │
         │                                                                              │
         │  Diagnose BEFORE scaling - adding replicas never fixes a caching problem,    │
         │  and caching never fixes an under-provisioned database connection pool.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.2 Horizontal Scaling
         - Scaling Backstage replicas
         - Stateless design: Any replica can handle any request
         - Load balancing considerations
         - Session management across replicas
         [ADVANCED: SPLITTING THE BACKEND BY PLUGIN]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Advanced lever: "service separation" (per the official scaling docs)  │
         │  # Split the backend into multiple services, each running a different    │
         │  # set of plugins, and route requests to the right one via a custom      │
         │  # implementation of the DiscoveryService interface. More complex than   │
         │  # adding replicas - reach for it only after simple horizontal scaling   │
         │  # and caching (29.4) are already maxed out.                             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [HORIZONTAL SCALING - Stateless Replicas]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Load Balancer                                                         │
         │      |                                                                 │
         │      +-- Pod 1 (stateless) --+                                         │
         │      +-- Pod 2 (stateless) --+--> shared PostgreSQL (source of truth)  │
         │      +-- Pod 3 (stateless) --+                                         │
         │                                                                        │
         │  Any pod can serve any request - session state lives in the JWT,       │
         │  not in pod memory. This is what makes horizontal scaling trivial.     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.3 High Availability
         - Multi-replica Backstage deployment
         - Database HA: PostgreSQL replication
         - Multi-zone deployment
         - Disaster recovery plan
         [HIGH AVAILABILITY ARCHITECTURE]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Backstage: 3+ replicas across 2+ availability zones            │
         │  PostgreSQL: Primary + read replica, automated failover         │
         │  Storage (TechDocs): Cloud object storage - inherently HA       │
         │  Disaster recovery: Nightly DB snapshot + tested restore drill  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.4 Caching Strategies
         - Backend caching: Redis for session/token cache
         - Frontend caching: Service worker, CDN
         - TechDocs caching
         - Catalog cache configuration
         [CACHE CONFIGURATION]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  backend:                                                 │
         │    cache:                                                 │
         │      store: redis                                         │
         │      connection: redis://redis.easypj.com:6379            │
         │                                                           │
         │  # Caches: auth session tokens, catalog entity lookups,   │
         │  # plugin-level response caching (e.g. GitHub API calls)  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.5 Load Testing Backstage
         - Proving your sizing before real traffic finds the limit
         - A minimal k6 script hitting the Catalog API
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // k6 load test - 50 virtual users for 2 minutes against the Catalog API  │
         │  import http from "k6/http";                                               │
         │  export const options = { vus: 50, duration: "2m" };                       │
         │  export default function () {                                              │
         │    http.get("https://backstage.easypj.com/api/catalog/entities");          │
         │  }                                                                         │
         │                                                                            │
         │  METRIC              HEALTHY TARGET                                        │
         │  -------------------  ------------------------------                       │
         │  p95 response time      < 500ms for catalog reads                          │
         │  Error rate               < 0.1%                                           │
         │  DB connection pool         Never fully exhausted under load               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    29.6 Capacity Planning Guide
         - Right-sizing Backstage before you deploy, not after it falls over
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ORG SIZE     ENTITIES     REPLICAS   DB TIER              CACHE                 │
         │  -----------  -----------  ---------  -------------------  ------------------    │
         │  Small (<50)   < 500         2         Small managed pg      Optional            │
         │  Medium         500-5,000     3         Medium, read replica  Redis recommended  │
         │  Large            5,000+        5+        HA cluster, PgBouncer Redis required   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         Backstage scales the same way any stateless web backend does:
         because session state lives in the JWT and not in pod memory
         (29.2), any replica can answer any request, which is what makes
         adding a fourth or fifth pod trivial. High availability (29.3) is
         really "more of the same, spread out" — more replicas across more
         zones, plus a database layer (PostgreSQL primary + read replica)
         that can survive a zone going down. Caching (29.4) is a separate
         lever entirely: it doesn't add capacity, it removes repeated work
         — the same session check, the same catalog lookup — so the
         capacity you already have goes further. Load testing (29.5) is
         how you find out whether 29.2-29.4 actually worked before real
         users do, and capacity planning (29.6) is how you avoid needing
         any of this as an emergency in the first place.

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 Diagnose the bottleneck first (29.1) — horizontal scaling
             never fixes a caching problem, and caching never fixes an
             under-provisioned DB connection pool.
          🔑 Horizontal scaling (29.2) works because Backstage is
             stateless — session state lives in the JWT, not pod memory.
          🔑 Service separation — splitting the backend by plugin behind a
             custom DiscoveryService — is a more advanced lever than
             replicas; reach for it only after replicas and caching are
             already maxed out.
          🔑 High availability (29.3) means 3+ replicas across 2+ zones,
             PostgreSQL with automated failover, and a tested DR restore
             drill.
          🔑 Redis caching (29.4) covers session tokens, catalog lookups,
             and plugin-level API responses — configured once under
             backend.cache.
          🔑 Load test with k6 (29.5) BEFORE go-live, and use the
             capacity-planning table (29.6) to size replicas/DB/cache to
             your actual org size.

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Reaching for horizontal scaling as the first fix for high
             latency without checking whether it's really a caching or
             query problem.
          ⚠️ Assuming session state can live in pod memory — it can't, if
             you want replicas to be interchangeable.
          ⚠️ Confusing simple horizontal scaling with the advanced "service
             separation" lever, which needs a custom DiscoveryService and
             is a much bigger change.
          ⚠️ Skipping a load test and finding your real capacity limit in
             production instead of in k6.

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Use the scaling-triggers table (29.1) to match the symptom to
             the correct lever before touching any config.
          ⚡ Start with the capacity-planning table (29.6) BEFORE you
             provision anything — right-size once instead of scaling
             reactively later.
          ⚡ Keep the k6 script (29.5) around as a regression check after
             every major release, not a one-time exercise.

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Frontend: code-split routes, lazy-load plugin pages          (29.1)        │
         │ Backend: tune connection pool, avoid N+1 catalog queries     (29.1)        │
         │ backend.cache: { store: redis, connection: redis://redis.easypj.com:6379 } │
         │ HA: 3+ replicas, 2+ zones, PG primary+replica, nightly snapshot (29.3)     │
         │ k6: { vus: 50, duration: '2m' } against /api/catalog/entities  (29.5)      │
         │ Sizing: <500 entities=2 replicas, 500-5k=3, 5k+=5+ w/ PgBouncer (29.6)     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 15 minutes: write the k6 script from 29.5 from memory,
             then run it and record the p95 response time.
          🧪 Given "high latency, CPU maxed" and "the same catalog lookup
             repeated thousands of times," name the correct lever for each
             from the 29.1 table.
          🧪 Pick an org size from the 29.6 table and justify its replica
             count, DB tier, and cache choice in one sentence each.

    29.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 29 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Performance, scaling, HA, caching

         VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 29.1 DIAGNOSE FIRST: which layer is actually slow?                  │
         │         │                                                           │
         │         ▼                                                           │
         │ 29.2 Horizontal Scaling   29.3 High Availability     29.4 Caching   │
         │   (stateless replicas)     (multi-zone + PG HA)        (Redis)      │
         │         └───────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘          │
         │                               │                                     │
         │                               ▼                                     │
         │         29.5 Load Test (k6) proves the sizing BEFORE go-live        │
         │                               │                                     │
         │                               ▼                                     │
         │    29.6 Capacity Planning table picks replicas/DB/cache by org size │
         │                                                                     │
         │ Never guess which lever to pull - 29.1's table maps each symptom to │
         │ the right fix, then 29.5 proves the fix actually holds under load.  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         .vs-prose — In words: 29.1's table exists so you diagnose before
         you act — the wrong lever wastes time and can even make things
         worse. 29.2-29.4 are the three real levers: more stateless
         replicas, multi-zone redundancy, and caching to remove repeated
         work. 29.5 proves those levers actually hold under simulated
         load, and 29.6 turns all of it into a simple lookup table so you
         can size a brand-new easypj deployment correctly on day one
         instead of discovering the right numbers the hard way.


Chapter 30: Security Best Practices
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         [META] 🔴 Advanced · ⏱️ ~45min · CBA Domain: 22% (Infrastructure)

         WHY THIS MATTERS
         Every capability this guide has built into easypj's Backstage
         instance — the Catalog, the Scaffolder, the plugins, the APIs
         from Chapter 28 — is also a door. This chapter is about making
         sure only the right people can open each one, on a platform
         that's about to sit at the center of essentially every service
         your org runs.

         OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
         ✅ run the 7-item security checklist end-to-end before calling any
            deployment production-ready
         ✅ manage secrets safely — Kubernetes Secrets baseline, an
            External Secrets Operator synced from a real vault, never
            inline in YAML or committed to Git
         ✅ lock down the network layer with TLS termination, WAF/rate-
            limiting on the Ingress, and NetworkPolicies scoping pod-to-
            pod traffic
         ✅ run dependency and image scanning (yarn audit/Snyk/Dependabot/
            Trivy/Grype) as a routine part of the CI pipeline
         ✅ walk a STRIDE-style threat model of Backstage's real attack
            surface, not just a checklist
         ✅ follow the incident-response runbook's exact order during the
            first hour of a suspected breach

         CBA RELEVANCE: maps to the CBA Infrastructure domain (~22%) —
         this is the final hardening layer before Chapter 31's exam prep.

         SECTION MAP (you are here):
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 30.1 Security Checklist -> 30.2 Secret Mgmt -> 30.3 Network Security ->  │
         │ 30.4 Vuln Mgmt -> 30.5 Threat Model -> 30.6 Incident Response -> 30.7 Qs │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEFENSE IN DEPTH — Backstage Security Layers                                   │
         │                                                                                 │
         │  LAYER 1: Network        HTTPS + WAF + rate limiting              (30.3)        │
         │  LAYER 2: Auth            Real SSO, no guest access in prod         (30.1)      │
         │  LAYER 3: RBAC             Least privilege, deny by default          (Ch 23)    │
         │  LAYER 4: Secrets           Vault/K8s Secrets, never in Git            (30.2)   │
         │  LAYER 5: Supply chain       Image/dependency scanning                 (30.4)   │
         │  LAYER 6: Audit                Logging on every sensitive action        (30.1)  │
         │                                                                                 │
         │  Each layer assumes the one before it can fail - RBAC still matters even        │
         │  with SSO enforced, because SSO proves WHO you are, not WHAT you can do.        │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.1 Security Checklist for Backstage
         - HTTPS everywhere
         - Secrets: External vault, never in Git
         - Auth: SSO enforced, no guest access in prod
         - RBAC: Least privilege principle
         - Images: Regular updates, vulnerability scanning
         - Network: Ingress with WAF, rate limiting
         - Audit: Enable audit logging
         [SECURITY CHECKLIST]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ITEM         REQUIREMENT                                            │
         │  -----------  --------------------------------------------------     │
         │  Transport     HTTPS everywhere, TLS termination at Ingress          │
         │  Secrets        Vault/K8s Secrets, never committed to Git            │
         │  Auth            Real SSO, guest access disabled in production       │
         │  RBAC             Least privilege - deny by default, allow narrowly  │
         │  Images             Scanned (Trivy/Snyk), rebuilt on CVE disclosure  │
         │  Network              WAF + rate limiting on the Ingress             │
         │  Audit                  Audit logging enabled for sensitive actions  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.2 Secret Management
         - Environment variables from Kubernetes Secrets
         - External secret stores: Vault, AWS Secrets Manager
         - GitHub tokens: Fine-grained PAT, rotation
         - Database credentials: Rotate regularly
         - Backstage service tokens: scope each one narrowly with backend.auth.externalAccess accessRestrictions (e.g. limit a CI/CD token to only the catalog/scaffolder plugins) so a leaked token can't call every plugin in the backend
         [SECRET MANAGEMENT EXAMPLES]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Kubernetes Secret (baseline)                                       │
         │  kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets \                    │
         │    --from-literal=GITHUB_TOKEN=xxx -n backstage                       │
         │                                                                       │
         │  # External Secrets Operator (syncs from a real vault)                │
         │  apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1                              │
         │  kind: ExternalSecret                                                 │
         │  spec:                                                                │
         │    secretStoreRef: { name: vault-backend, kind: ClusterSecretStore }  │
         │    target: { name: backstage-secrets }                                │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.3 Network Security
         - Ingress with TLS termination
         - Network policies for Backstage pods
         - API rate limiting
         - CORS configuration
         [NETWORK SECURITY CONFIGURATION]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Ingress with TLS + rate limiting                                │
         │  metadata:                                                         │
         │    annotations:                                                    │
         │      cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod              │
         │      nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/limit-rps: "20"                   │
         │                                                                    │
         │  # NetworkPolicy - only allow traffic from the ingress controller  │
         │  spec:                                                             │
         │    podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: backstage } }                │
         │    ingress: [{ from: [{ namespaceSelector: { matchLabels:          │
         │      { name: ingress-nginx } } }] }]                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.4 Vulnerability Management
         - Dependency scanning: yarn audit, Snyk, Dependabot
         - Container image scanning: Trivy, Grype
         - Regular updates: Backstage release cadence
         [VULNERABILITY SCANNING TOOLS]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TOOL         SCANS                    WHEN TO RUN                    │
         │  -----------  -----------------------  ----------------------------   │
         │  yarn audit     Node.js dependencies      Every CI build              │
         │  Snyk            Dependencies + containers  Every CI build + daily    │
         │  Dependabot        GitHub dependency PRs      Continuous (automated)  │
         │  Trivy               Container images            Every image build    │
         │  Grype                 Container images            Every image build  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.5 Threat Model — What Could Go Wrong
         - A STRIDE-style pass over the Backstage attack surface
         - Because knowing the checklist (30.1) matters less than knowing why
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  THREAT               EXAMPLE IN BACKSTAGE            MITIGATION                              │
         │  --------------------  -------------------------------  ------------------------------        │
         │  Spoofing               Fake sign-in as another user      Real SSO, no guest in prod          │
         │  Tampering                Edited catalog-info.yaml           RBAC + PR review on infra        │
         │                            granting self extra ownership       repos (Ch 23)                  │
         │  Repudiation               No audit trail for who deleted     Enable audit logging (30.1)     │
         │                             an entity                                                         │
         │  Info disclosure             Secrets leaked via app-config      Never inline secrets in YAML  │
         │                               committed to Git                   (Ch 5.3, 30.2)               │
         │  Denial of service             Unbounded scaffolder task          Rate limit the Ingress      │
         │                                  spam                                (30.3)                   │
         │  Elevation of privilege           Broad ClusterRole on the           Least-privilege RBAC     │
         │                                     K8s plugin ServiceAccount           (Ch 20.5)             │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.6 Security Incident Response Runbook
         - What to do in the first hour of a suspected security incident
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. Contain: revoke the suspected compromised token/credential immediately     │
         │  2. Assess: check audit logs (30.1) for what that identity actually touched    │
         │  3. Rotate: rotate ALL secrets that identity had access to, not just the one   │
         │  4. Patch: if a CVE was the entry point, apply the fix from 30.4 now           │
         │  5. Restore: use the DR runbook (26.7) if data was altered, not just accessed  │
         │  6. Report: write the postmortem, feed new detections into monitoring (Ch 27)  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         UNDER THE HOOD 🔧
         The security checklist (30.1) and the defense-in-depth diagram
         above it aren't two different things — the checklist is the
         CONTRACT, and each of its rows is satisfied by one specific
         mechanism elsewhere in the chapter: secret management (30.2)
         satisfies the "never in Git" row, network security (30.3)
         satisfies transport and rate-limiting, and vulnerability
         management (30.4) satisfies the "regular updates, scanned images"
         row. The threat model (30.5) exists because a checklist alone
         doesn't teach you WHY each row matters — walking Backstage
         through STRIDE shows that RBAC (Ch23) still matters even with
         real SSO enforced, because SSO only proves WHO you are, never
         WHAT you're allowed to do. And the incident runbook (30.6) is the
         plan for the day one of these layers fails anyway — contain
         first, understand blast radius second, never skip straight to
         "fix and move on."

         KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
          🔑 Defense in depth means six layers — network, auth, RBAC,
             secrets, supply chain, audit — each one assumes the layer
             before it CAN fail.
          🔑 The 7-item checklist (30.1) is the contract: HTTPS, external
             secrets, real SSO, least-privilege RBAC, scanned images,
             WAF+rate-limiting, audit logging.
          🔑 Secrets (30.2) always come from Kubernetes Secrets or an
             External Secrets Operator synced from a real vault — never
             hardcoded in app-config.yaml.
          🔑 Scope every service token narrowly with
             backend.auth.externalAccess.accessRestrictions so a leaked
             token can't call every plugin.
          🔑 A STRIDE pass (30.5) shows RBAC still matters even with SSO
             enforced — SSO proves identity, not authorization.
          🔑 The incident runbook (30.6) is an ORDER — contain, assess,
             rotate, patch, restore, report — not a menu to pick from.

         EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
          ⚠️ Treating SSO alone as "secure enough" and skipping least-
             privilege RBAC — SSO answers WHO, not WHAT they can do.
          ⚠️ Committing a secret inline in app-config.yaml "just for now"
             instead of referencing an environment variable backed by a
             vault.
          ⚠️ Scanning container images but skipping dependency scanning (or
             vice versa) — 30.4 requires both layers together.
          ⚠️ Responding to an incident by rotating only the one credential
             known to be compromised instead of everything that identity
             had access to.

         SPEED TIPS ⚡
          ⚡ Run the 30.1 checklist as a literal go/no-go gate — treat any
             unchecked row as a launch blocker, same discipline as 26.1.
          ⚡ Automate scanning (30.4) into CI so a new CVE is caught on the
             next build, not on the next audit.
          ⚡ Rehearse the incident runbook (30.6) BEFORE an incident — a
             live breach is the worst time to invent a response order.

         COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Checklist: HTTPS, vault secrets, real SSO, least-priv RBAC, scanned        │
         │   images, WAF+rate-limit, audit logging                        (30.1)      │
         │ kubectl create secret generic backstage-secrets --from-literal=... (30.2)  │
         │ ExternalSecret: secretStoreRef: vault-backend -> target: backstage-secrets │
         │ Ingress: cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer + limit-rps: '20'      (30.3)      │
         │ Scan: yarn audit / Snyk / Dependabot / Trivy / Grype            (30.4)     │
         │ Incident: contain -> assess -> rotate -> patch -> restore -> report (30.6) │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
          🧪 In 15 minutes: write out the 7-item security checklist (30.1)
             from memory, then check it against this chapter.
          🧪 Pick one row of the STRIDE table (30.5) and explain, out
             loud, which existing easypj control from an earlier chapter
             mitigates it.
          🧪 Walk the incident runbook (30.6) for a scenario where a CI/CD
             static token was just leaked in a public repo.

    30.7 CBA Exam: Chapter 30 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Security checklist, secrets, network security

         VISUAL SUMMARY
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 30.1 SECURITY CHECKLIST = the 6-layer defense-in-depth contract       │
         │         │                                                             │
         │         ▼                                                             │
         │ 30.2 Secrets      30.3 Network        30.4 Vuln Scanning              │
         │   (vault, not       (TLS, WAF,          (yarn audit, Snyk,            │
         │    Git)              rate-limit)          Trivy, Grype)               │
         │         └────────────────┴─────────────────────┘                      │
         │                           │                                           │
         │                           ▼                                           │
         │          30.5 STRIDE Threat Model: what could still go wrong          │
         │                           │                                           │
         │                           ▼                                           │
         │  30.6 Incident Response Runbook: contain -> assess -> rotate -> patch │
         │                   -> restore -> report                                │
         │                                                                       │
         │ Each layer assumes the one before it can fail - the checklist (30.1)  │
         │ is the contract, 30.2-30.4 satisfy it, 30.5 asks what still breaks    │
         │ it, and 30.6 is the plan for the day something does.                  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         .vs-prose — In words: the checklist (30.1) is the goal; everything
         else in this chapter satisfies one of its rows. Secret management
         (30.2) and network security (30.3) lock down transport and
         credentials; vulnerability management (30.4) keeps images and
         dependencies current. The threat model (30.5) is a reality check
         — it asks what still goes wrong even after 30.1-30.4 are done —
         and the incident runbook (30.6) is the fixed order you follow on
         the day one of those threats stops being theoretical.


Chapter 31: CBA Exam Preparation — Complete Guide
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 · ⏱️ ~4h (incl. a 45-min timed practice exam) · CBA Domain: all 4 domains (exam strategy, not new content)

    WHY THIS MATTERS: every other chapter in this guide taught you to BUILD
    something on easypj's Backstage instance. This chapter teaches you to
    PROVE it on a 90-minute, webcam-proctored, multiple-choice exam - a
    completely different skill from deploying a plugin or writing a
    template. Walking in without knowing the format, the domain weights, or
    your own weak spots wastes minutes you do not get back; this chapter
    removes every one of those unknowns before exam day.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ state the CBA's exact format, timing, and passing score from memory;
    ✅ recall the four official domain weights and prioritize study time
       accordingly (Customizing 32%, Dev Workflow 24%, Infrastructure 22%,
       Catalog 22%);
    ✅ apply the 3-pass time-management strategy to a 90-minute exam window;
    ✅ take and self-score a full 30-question practice exam spanning all four
       domains;
    ✅ follow an 8-week study plan that maps directly onto Parts 1-10 of this
       guide;
    ✅ execute the 1-week-before / 1-day-before / exam-day checklist without
       missing a step.

    CBA RELEVANCE: this chapter is not graded content for one domain - it is
    meta-strategy across all four (Customizing Backstage 32%, Development
    Workflow 24%, Backstage Infrastructure 22%, Backstage Catalog 22%). Every
    question in 31.4's practice exam is drawn from material you already
    learned in Parts 1-9.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    31.1 Exam Format & Rules -> 31.2 Domain Weights -> 31.3 Exam Tips &
    Strategies -> 31.4 CBA Practice Exam (30 Qs) -> 31.5 8-Week Study Plan.

    31.1 CBA Exam Format & Rules
         - Duration: 90 minutes
         - Format: Multiple choice (single + multi-answer)
         - Passing score: 70%
         - Online proctored (like CKA/CKAD)
         - Open book: Allowed to use Backstage docs
         [EXAM FORMAT TABLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ATTRIBUTE       DETAILS                                     │
         │  --------------  -----------------------------------------   │
         │  Duration          90 minutes                                │
         │  Format             Multiple choice (single + multi-answer)  │
         │  Questions            ~60 questions                          │
         │  Passing score          70% (~42/60 correct)                 │
         │  Proctoring               Online, webcam-proctored           │
         │  Open book                  Yes - backstage.io/docs allowed  │
         │  Retake                        1 free retake included        │
         │  Validity                        3 years from passing date   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    31.2 CBA Domain Weights (Official Linux Foundation)
         - Customizing Backstage: 32% (Plugins, React, MUI, Templates, TechDocs, APIs)
         - Development Workflow: 24% (Setup, CLI, TypeScript, Yarn/NPM, Docker)
         - Backstage Infrastructure: 22% (Architecture, Configuration, Deployment, Auth)

    31.4 CBA Practice Exam — 30 Questions
         - 30 mixed questions covering all domains
         - Timed: 45 minutes
         - Answers with detailed explanations
         [CBA PRACTICE EXAM — 30 Questions, Timed 45 Minutes]
         Domain split matches the real exam weighting: 10 Customizing (32%),
         7 Development Workflow (24%), 7 Infrastructure (22%), 6 Catalog (22%).
         Cover the questions before checking answers — that is the whole point.

         ── DOMAIN 1: CUSTOMIZING BACKSTAGE (Q1-Q10) ──

         Q1. A plugin renders a UI tab but never touches a database or runs
             server-side code. What KIND of plugin is it?
             A) Backend-only   B) Frontend-only   C) Full-stack   D) Dynamic
             ANSWER: B. Frontend-only plugins render UI and call external APIs
             directly from the browser — no backend package required.

         Q2. Which import provides the Backstage-styled card component most
             commonly used to wrap custom plugin content?
             A) Card from @material-ui/core
             B) InfoCard from @backstage/core-components
             C) Panel from @backstage/theme
             D) Container from @material-ui/core
             ANSWER: B. InfoCard is the Backstage-specific wrapper that matches
             the platform's look — plain MUI Card also works but is less idiomatic.

         Q3. You need to add a brand-new page reachable from the sidebar that
             is specific to your org and will never be reused elsewhere. What
             is the correct approach?
             A) Publish it as a community plugin on npm
             B) Edit App.tsx to add a route and Root.tsx to add a sidebar item
             C) Add it inside the Catalog plugin's source code
             D) Create a new Backstage instance just for this page
             ANSWER: B. Org-specific, non-reusable wiring belongs directly in
             the App shell (App.tsx/Root.tsx), not in a redistributable plugin.

         Q4. What is the correct order of steps in a standard scaffolder
             template pipeline?
             A) publish:github -> fetch:template -> catalog:register
             B) catalog:register -> fetch:template -> publish:github
             C) fetch:template -> publish:github -> catalog:register
             D) fetch:template -> catalog:register -> publish:github
             ANSWER: C. You must fetch the skeleton, THEN publish it to a real
             repo, THEN register it — you cannot register a repo that does not
             exist yet.

         Q5. Which techdocs.builder setting should a MULTI-REPLICA production
             deployment use?
             A) local   B) external   C) docker   D) inline
             ANSWER: B. external means CI/CD pre-builds the docs once; local
             would make each pod (re)build on demand, wasting resources and
             risking inconsistent output across pods.

         Q6. What is the correct lifecycle order for a Backstage plugin?
             A) Register -> Configure -> Install -> Run
             B) Install -> Configure -> Register -> Run
             C) Configure -> Install -> Run -> Register
             D) Install -> Register -> Run -> Configure
             ANSWER: B. npm/yarn install the package, configure it in
             app-config.yaml, register it via createPlugin(), then it runs.

         Q7. What does createRoutableExtension() do?
             A) Creates a new Catalog entity
             B) Registers a lazy-loaded page/route for a frontend plugin
             C) Creates a new backend Express router
             D) Extends the permission framework
             ANSWER: B. It wires a plugin's page component to a route and
             enables code-splitting so the plugin's code loads only on demand.

         Q8. A template parameter needs the user to pick an EXISTING Backstage
             Group as the owner. Which ui:field should you use?
             A) RepoUrlPicker   B) EntityPicker   C) OwnerPicker   D) GroupSelect
             ANSWER: C. OwnerPicker is purpose-built for selecting an owning
             User or Group; EntityPicker is the more general-purpose version.

         Q9. Inside a custom scaffolder action's handler, how do you report
             a value for later steps to consume?
             A) return { value }
             B) ctx.output('key', value)
             C) console.log(value)
             D) ctx.logger.info(value)
             ANSWER: B. ctx.output() is how a step exposes data that later
             steps reference via {{ steps.<id>.output.<key> }}.

         Q10. What is the main benefit of DYNAMIC plugins over standard ones?
             A) They run faster
             B) They can be installed/updated at runtime without a rebuild
             C) They do not need app-config.yaml entries
             D) They bypass the permission framework
             ANSWER: B. Dynamic plugins load from a container image at
             runtime — no Backstage rebuild or redeploy needed to add one.

         ── DOMAIN 2: DEVELOPMENT WORKFLOW (Q11-Q17) ──

         Q11. What is the key difference between yarn tsc and yarn build?
              A) They are identical commands
              B) tsc only type-checks; build compiles AND bundles for prod
              C) tsc is for the backend only; build is for the frontend only
              D) build only works in Docker
              ANSWER: B. yarn tsc verifies types across the monorepo without
              producing deployable output; yarn build does full production
              compilation and bundling.

         Q12. In a Backstage monorepo, where does yarn.lock live and how
              many should exist?
              A) One per package, several total
              B) One at the repo root, covering all workspaces
              C) None - Backstage does not use Yarn
              D) One per plugin only
              ANSWER: B. Yarn workspaces share ONE root-level yarn.lock that
              pins versions across every package/plugin in the monorepo.

         Q13. Why do production Backstage Dockerfiles use a multi-stage build?
              A) It is required by Kubernetes
              B) To keep the final runtime image small by excluding build
                 tools and devDependencies
              C) It makes yarn dev faster
              D) It avoids needing a package.json
              ANSWER: B. Stage 1 compiles with full devDependencies; stage 2
              copies only the compiled output, dramatically shrinking image size.

         Q14. Which command scaffolds a brand-new Backstage application?
              A) yarn create backstage-app
              B) npx @backstage/create-app@latest
              C) yarn backstage-cli new
              D) npm init backstage
              ANSWER: B. npx @backstage/create-app@latest is the official
              scaffolding command for a fresh Backstage instance.

         Q15. What is the app-config.yaml merge precedence, highest last?
              A) local -> production -> base
              B) base -> local -> production
              C) base -> production -> local
              D) production -> base -> local
              ANSWER: C. app-config.yaml (base) is overridden by
              app-config.production.yaml, which is overridden by the
              gitignored app-config.local.yaml (highest priority).

         Q16. In yarn dev mode, are TypeScript type errors guaranteed to stop
              the server from running?
              A) Yes, dev mode always type-checks strictly
              B) No - dev mode transpiles on the fly without full type-checking
              C) Only backend type errors block yarn dev
              D) Only frontend type errors block yarn dev
              ANSWER: B. yarn dev prioritizes fast iteration; only yarn tsc
              (or yarn build/CI) rigorously enforces types.

         Q17. Why should CI pipelines use "yarn install --frozen-lockfile"
              instead of plain "yarn install"?
              A) It installs faster in all cases
              B) It fails loudly if package.json and yarn.lock have drifted,
                 instead of silently rewriting the lockfile
              C) It skips devDependencies automatically
              D) It is required for Docker builds only
              ANSWER: B. Frozen-lockfile installs catch dependency drift as a
              hard CI failure rather than silently changing resolved versions.

         ── DOMAIN 3: BACKSTAGE INFRASTRUCTURE (Q18-Q24) ──

         Q18. What are the three layers of Backstage's architecture?
              A) Frontend, Middleware, Database
              B) Frontend (React), Backend (Node.js), Database (PostgreSQL)
              C) Client, Proxy, Server
              D) UI, API Gateway, Cache
              ANSWER: B. React frontend talks to a Node.js/Express backend,
              which is the ONLY layer allowed to talk to the database.

         Q19. Why is SQLite unsuitable for a production Backstage deployment
              with 3 replicas?
              A) SQLite cannot store YAML data
              B) SQLite only supports a single concurrent writer
              C) SQLite is slower than PostgreSQL for reads
              D) SQLite requires a paid license in production
              ANSWER: B. Multiple pods writing concurrently would corrupt a
              SQLite file; PostgreSQL is built for concurrent access.

         Q20. Which Kubernetes resource holds non-sensitive Backstage
              configuration like app-config.yaml?
              A) Secret   B) ConfigMap   C) PersistentVolumeClaim   D) Ingress
              ANSWER: B. ConfigMap stores non-sensitive config; Secret is
              reserved for credentials and tokens.

         Q21. Your org already runs Okta company-wide for SSO. Which auth
              provider is the best fit for Backstage?
              A) Guest access   B) GitHub OAuth   C) Okta (OIDC)   D) None needed
              ANSWER: C. Reusing the existing enterprise identity provider
              (Okta) avoids a second identity system and enables group sync.

         Q22. In the Backstage permission framework, if an Allow rule and a
              Deny rule both match a request, which wins?
              A) Allow always wins
              B) Deny always wins
              C) The most recently defined rule wins
              D) Whichever rule is alphabetically first
              ANSWER: B. In practice, a PermissionPolicy should be written so a Deny
              takes precedence — but note Backstage's core permission framework does
              NOT ship separate "Allow rules" and "Deny rules" that get auto-combined.
              A PermissionPolicy is a single function that receives the user + permission
              and returns ONE decision (ALLOW, DENY, or CONDITIONAL) per request; any
              deny-wins behavior is something YOU implement in that function's logic,
              following least-privilege as a design convention, not a built-in rule engine.

         Q23. Why can Backstage scale horizontally just by adding replicas?
              A) Each pod has its own database
              B) The app is stateless - session state lives in the JWT, not
                 pod memory
              C) Kubernetes automatically shares memory between pods
              D) Horizontal scaling is not actually supported
              ANSWER: B. Because no session state is kept in a given pod,
              any replica can handle any request.

         Q24. Which of these is NOT part of the standard production
              checklist?
              A) PostgreSQL instead of SQLite
              B) Guest access enabled for convenience
              C) TLS/HTTPS everywhere
              D) Secrets stored outside of Git
              ANSWER: B. Guest access must NEVER be enabled in production -
              it is a dev-only convenience with no real permission scoping.

         ── DOMAIN 4: BACKSTAGE CATALOG (Q25-Q30) ──

         Q25. You need to model a PostgreSQL database that jobpost depends
              on. Which entity kind is correct?
              A) Component   B) Resource   C) System   D) API
              ANSWER: B. Resource represents infrastructure you USE, not
              software you build — Component would be the wrong choice here.

         Q26. What is the PRIMARY purpose of an annotation on an entity?
              A) To make the entity searchable by name
              B) To link the entity to an external tool/system for a plugin
              C) To set the entity's display color
              D) To define the entity's required spec fields
              ANSWER: B. Annotations (e.g. github.com/project-slug) are
              machine-facing pointers that plugins use to enrich entity pages.

         Q27. What is the key difference between a manual Location and an
              automated ingestion Provider?
              A) They are the same thing with different names
              B) A Location points to ONE known file; a Provider discovers
                 MANY entities on a recurring schedule
              C) Providers only work with GitLab
              D) Locations require a paid Backstage license
              ANSWER: B. Manual Locations don't scale past a handful of
              repos; automated Providers (e.g. GitHub org discovery) scan an
              entire org on a schedule.

         Q28. An entity was registered a week ago but never appeared in the
              Catalog. What are the TWO most likely root causes?
              A) The K8s cluster is down
              B) The Location was never configured, OR the YAML is invalid
              C) The user is not logged in
              D) TechDocs failed to build
              ANSWER: B. The two simplest, most common causes come first in
              the debug flowchart - always rule these out before checking logs.

         Q29. Which 4 top-level fields are REQUIRED in every
              catalog-info.yaml?
              A) name, owner, type, tags
              B) apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec
              C) kind, spec, labels, annotations
              D) apiVersion, name, owner, system
              ANSWER: B. Every entity YAML must have apiVersion, kind,
              metadata (with at minimum a name), and spec.

         Q30. Component A "providesApis" an API called payments-api. What
              does this relationship mean?
              A) Component A consumes the payments-api
              B) Component A implements/exposes the payments-api
              C) Component A is owned by payments-api
              D) Component A depends on payments-api as infrastructure
              ANSWER: B. providesApis means this Component IS the
              implementation of that API; consumesApis is the inverse relationship
              for a Component that calls it.

         [SCORING] 27-30 correct = exam ready. 21-26 = review weak domains.
         Below 21 = revisit Parts 1-10 before scheduling the real exam.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — why the exam is weighted the way it is
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         The 32/24/22/22 split in 31.2 is not arbitrary - it mirrors how much
         of a real Backstage rollout is actually customization work. Parts
         4-6 (Templates, TechDocs, Plugins) are the largest slice of THIS
         guide too, because most day-to-day Backstage engineering is writing
         templates and plugins, not reconfiguring the catalog ingestion
         pipeline or re-standing the instance. The exam's weighting is a
         direct reflection of where a working platform engineer spends their
         time, which is why 31.5's study plan gives Customizing (Parts 4-6)
         its own dedicated weeks while Catalog and Infrastructure share one
         combined week each.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         🔑 The CBA is 90 minutes, ~60 questions, open-book on backstage.io,
            passing at 70% (~42/60), with 1 free retake and 3-year validity.
         🔑 Domain weights (32/24/22/22) should directly set your study-time
            split - Customizing Backstage deserves nearly a third of it.
         🔑 The 3-pass strategy (easy -> medium -> hard/flagged -> review)
            protects you from losing the whole exam to one hard question.
         🔑 31.4's 30-question practice exam mirrors the real weighting
            exactly: 10 Customizing, 7 Dev Workflow, 7 Infrastructure, 6
            Catalog - score 27-30 and you are exam-ready.
         🔑 The 8-week plan is not generic - each week maps to specific Parts
            and chapters of THIS guide, ending in two full timed practice
            exams before you schedule the real one.
         🔑 Exam-day logistics (ID, camera, clear water bottle, 15-minute
            early login) fail candidates as often as content gaps do - the
            checklist in 31.5 exists because of that.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         ⚠️ Studying every domain equally. Spend roughly proportional time to
            the weights (31.2) - under-studying Customizing (32%) costs more
            than under-studying Catalog (22%).
         ⚠️ Treating open-book as "I don't need to know it." You still must
            know WHERE to look and recognize the right answer fast enough to
            beat the clock (31.3's time allocation).
         ⚠️ Getting stuck on one question. The exam-day tip in 31.3 says it
            plainly: never spend more than 2 minutes on any single question -
            flag it and return in the review pass.
         ⚠️ Skipping the timed condition when taking 31.4's practice exam.
            An untimed 30/30 tells you nothing about whether you can do it
            in the real 45-minute (scaled) window.
         ⚠️ Cramming heavily the night before. 31.5 is explicit: light review
            only, no new material, and prioritize sleep over one more pass
            through the notes.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         ⚡ Read every question once before answering any of them (31.3's
            SETUP phase) - it lets you mentally flag easy vs. hard up front.
         ⚡ Keep backstage.io/docs open in a tab throughout - the exam is
            open-book, so treat the docs as your fourth exam pass, not a
            last resort.
         ⚡ Re-take the 30-question practice exam (31.4) more than once,
            timed - repetition under the clock is what the study plan (31.5)
            is actually building toward.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔑 CBA EXAM FACTS TO REMEMBER
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

    🧪 PRACTICE DRILL
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         🧪 In 45 minutes, sit the full 30-question practice exam in 31.4
            under real timed conditions (no notes, no docs open).
         🧪 Score yourself against the 31.4 answer key and bucket each miss
            by domain (Customizing / Dev Workflow / Infrastructure /
            Catalog).
         🧪 For every domain where you missed 2+ questions, re-read that
            domain's Part from this guide before moving on.
         🧪 Sketch your own 8-week calendar using 31.5's plan, filling in
            real dates starting today.

         │               |                                              |             │
         │               +------------------------+---------------------+             │
         │                                         v                                  │
         │                      31.4 CBA Practice Exam (30 Qs, timed 45 min)          │
         │                                         |                                  │
         │                                         v                                  │
         │               31.5 8-Week Study Plan + Exam-Day Checklist -> READY         │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         Chapter 31 has one job: convert everything you learned in Parts
         1-9 into a passing CBA score. 31.1-31.3 teach you the shape of the
         exam and how to sit it under time pressure, 31.4 proves you can
         apply that knowledge across all four domains in one timed sitting,
         and 31.5 turns the whole thing into a calendar you can actually
         follow - ending in two full practice exams and a checklist for the
         morning you walk in.


Chapter 32: Beyond CBA — Advanced Backstage
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2h · CBA Domain: beyond the graded syllabus (reinforces Customizing 32% + Infrastructure 22%)

    WHY THIS MATTERS: passing the CBA proves you can operate easypj's
    Backstage instance the way the curriculum defines it today. Real
    platform teams keep going past that point - writing custom catalog
    processors, adopting dynamic plugins so a new capability ships without a
    redeploy, running multi-tenant portals for several teams, and eventually
    migrating onto the New Backend System. This chapter is what you read
    the week AFTER you pass, not instead of studying for Chapter 31.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ write a custom CatalogProcessor that enriches entities during
       ingestion;
    ✅ explain what a dynamic plugin is and why it avoids a Backstage
       rebuild/redeploy cycle;
    ✅ compare self-hosted Backstage against managed offerings (Roadie,
       Spotify Portal) on control vs. ops burden;
    ✅ design multi-tenant catalog isolation using namespaces and rules;
    ✅ describe the New Backend System's plugin/module/extension-point model
       and how it differs from the legacy backend;
    ✅ explain why plugin-to-plugin calls are secure by default under the
       New Backend System, with no extra config;
    ✅ identify which adoption/efficiency/quality/ROI metrics actually prove
       a Backstage rollout is working.

    CBA RELEVANCE: none of 32.1-32.6 is directly graded - the chapter title
    says so. But 32.6's New Backend System deepens the same Backstage
    Infrastructure (22%) and Customizing Backstage (32%) domains the CBA
    tests, so it is far from wasted study time even for someone still
    preparing for the exam.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    32.1 Custom Catalog Processors -> 32.2 Dynamic Plugins (Beta) -> 32.3
    Backstage as a Service -> 32.4 Multi-Tenancy -> 32.5 The Future of
    Backstage -> 32.6 The New Backend System.

    32.1 Custom Catalog Processors
         - Extending the catalog ingestion pipeline
         - Building custom entity providers
         - Custom processors for data transformation
         [CUSTOM CATALOG PROCESSOR - Real Code]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // Enriches every Component entity with a computed field            │
         │  import { CatalogProcessor } from '@backstage/plugin-catalog-node';  │
         │                                                                      │
         │  export class EasypjEnricher implements CatalogProcessor {           │
         │    getProcessorName() { return 'easypj-enricher'; }                  │
         │    // Signature: postProcessEntity(entity, location, emit, cache)    │
         │    // - location: LocationSpec the entity came from                  │
         │    // - emit: CatalogProcessorEmit, used to emit related entities    │
         │    // - cache: CatalogProcessorCache for processor-specific caching  │
         │    async postProcessEntity(entity) {                                 │
         │      if (entity.kind === 'Component') {                              │
         │        entity.metadata.labels ??= {};                                │
         │        entity.metadata.labels['easypj.io/managed'] = 'true';         │
         │      }                                                               │
         │      return entity;                                                  │
         │    }                                                                 │
         │  }                                                                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.2 Dynamic Plugins (Beta)
         - What are dynamic plugins?
         - Installing plugins without rebuilding Backstage
         - Plugin marketplace integration
         [DYNAMIC PLUGIN CONFIGURATION - Beta]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.dynamic-plugins.yaml                           │
         │  dynamicPlugins:                                             │
         │    frontend:                                                 │
         │      internal.plugin-easypj-dashboard:                       │
         │        appIcons: []                                          │
         │        dynamicRoutes: []                                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  # Dynamic plugins load at RUNTIME from a container image -  │
         │  # no Backstage rebuild/redeploy needed to add a new plugin  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.3 Backstage as a Service
         - Spotify's hosted offering
         - Roadie, Cortex, Port — alternatives
         - Self-hosted vs Managed comparison
         [BACKSTAGE AS A SERVICE - Self-Hosted vs Managed]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  OPTION         CONTROL     OPS BURDEN     EXAMPLE                         │
         │  -------------  ----------  -------------  ---------------------           │
         │  Self-hosted      Full         High             Your own K8s cluster       │
         │  Roadie             Managed       Low              Roadie.io (hosted BS)   │
         │  Spotify Portal       Managed       Low              Spotify's commercial  │
         │                                                     offering               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.4 Multi-Tenancy in Backstage
         - Namespace-based isolation
         - Per-tenant customization
         - Multi-team portal design
         [MULTI-TENANT CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  catalog:                                                        │
         │    rules:                                                        │
         │      - allow: [Component, API, System]                           │
         │    locations:                                                    │
         │      - type: url                                                 │
         │        target: https://github.com/team-a/catalog-info.yaml       │
         │      - type: url                                                 │
         │        target: https://github.com/team-b/catalog-info.yaml       │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Namespaces (metadata.namespace) isolate entities per tenant;  │
         │  # permission policies scope what each tenant can see/edit       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.5 The Future of Backstage
         - Roadmap highlights
         - CNCF Incubating status and the path toward Graduation
         - Community and contributions
         - Getting involved

    32.6 The New Backend System: Plugins, Modules, Extension Points & Service-to-Service Auth
         - A backend instance does no work itself - it just wires plugins/modules together
         - Plugins run as isolated, network-only "microservices" inside one backend instance
         - A Module can only extend ONE plugin, via that plugin's Extension Points
         - Extension points are created with createExtensionPoint() and registered in a
           plugin's register(env) via env.registerExtensionPoint(); a Module then declares
           the extension point as a dependency in its own registerInit({ deps }) block
         - Plugin-to-plugin calls are secure BY DEFAULT - no config needed. Each plugin gets
           the auth and httpAuth services (from coreServices) and self-signed tokens are
           verified via the caller's public key set
         [BACKEND SYSTEM BUILDING BLOCKS (New Backend System)]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │   BACKEND SYSTEM BUILDING BLOCKS (New Backend System)                             │
         │                                                                                   │
         │   COMPONENT           ROLE                                                        │
         │   ------------------  ------------------------------------------------            │
         │   Backend instance    Deployment unit that wires plugins/modules together         │
         │   Plugin              Provides one feature; isolated, network-only comms          │
         │   Service             Shared utility injected via coreServices (logger, auth...)  │
         │   Module              Extends ONE plugin via that plugin's Extension Point        │
         │   Extension Point     Customization surface a plugin exposes to its modules       │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [SERVICE-TO-SERVICE AUTH - PLUGIN TO PLUGIN (SECURE BY DEFAULT)]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │   SERVICE-TO-SERVICE AUTH - PLUGIN TO PLUGIN (SECURE BY DEFAULT)       │
         │                                                                        │
         │   auth & httpAuth services are injected via coreServices - no          │
         │   config needed. A plugin requests a token to call another plugin:     │
         │                                                                        │
         │     const { token } = await auth.getPluginRequestToken({               │
         │       onBehalfOf: await auth.getOwnServiceCredentials(),               │
         │       targetPluginId: 'catalog',                                       │
         │     });                                                                │
         │                                                                        │
         │   Self-signed tokens are verified via the caller's public JWKS.        │
         │   External callers (CI/CD, webhooks) use backend.auth.externalAccess:  │
         │   static tokens or JWKS-validated JWTs, each scoped with optional      │
         │   accessRestrictions for least-privilege.                              │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

APPENDICES — QUICK REFERENCE GUIDES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — why plugin-to-plugin calls need no config
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         32.6 states that auth and httpAuth are injected into every plugin
         via coreServices with self-signed tokens verified against the
         caller's public key set (JWKS). This is what makes the security
         model "secure by default": a plugin calling another plugin (e.g. a
         custom processor from 32.1 calling the catalog plugin) never has to
         be handed a shared secret or manage a credential - it asks its own
         auth service for a scoped token naming the target plugin, and the
         receiving plugin verifies that token's signature itself. External
         callers (CI/CD, webhooks) are the one case that DOES need explicit
         config, via backend.auth.externalAccess static tokens or
         JWKS-validated JWTs - because an external caller has no in-process
         auth service to ask.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         🔑 A CatalogProcessor's postProcessEntity hook is the standard way
            to enrich every entity of a given kind during ingestion (32.1).
         🔑 Dynamic plugins load at RUNTIME from a container image - no
            Backstage rebuild or redeploy needed to add or update one (32.2).
         🔑 Self-hosted gives full control at high ops burden; Roadie and
            Spotify Portal trade some control for a managed, low-burden
            offering (32.3).
         🔑 Multi-tenancy in Backstage is built from namespaces plus catalog
            rules and permission policies, not a separate product (32.4).
         🔑 In the New Backend System, a backend instance does no work
            itself - it only wires Plugins and Modules together (32.6).
         🔑 A Module can extend exactly ONE plugin, through that plugin's
            Extension Points created with createExtensionPoint() (32.6).
         🔑 Plugin-to-plugin auth is secure by default via coreServices'
            auth/httpAuth - no shared secrets, no extra config (32.6).

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         ⚠️ Confusing a Module with a plugin - a Module cannot stand alone;
            it only ever extends ONE existing plugin's Extension Point.
         ⚠️ Assuming dynamic plugins skip app-config entirely - they still
            need a dynamicPlugins config block (32.2), they just skip the
            rebuild/redeploy step.
         ⚠️ Thinking multi-tenancy requires separate Backstage instances -
            32.4 does it inside ONE instance with namespaces + catalog rules.
         ⚠️ Assuming plugin-to-plugin calls need manually issued API keys -
            32.6's whole point is that this is automatic via coreServices.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
         ⚡ Reach for a CatalogProcessor before a custom EntityProvider if
            you only need to enrich/transform entities that already exist -
            it is the smaller, simpler extension point.
         ⚡ Prototype with dynamic plugins in non-prod first (32.2 marks the
            feature Beta) before relying on it for a production rollout.
         ⚡ When designing multi-tenancy, decide the namespace + rule + policy
            shape (32.4) BEFORE onboarding a second team, not after.

    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🔑 CONFIG/CODE TO REMEMBER
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

         Chapter 32 is the guide's "and beyond" chapter. 32.1-32.3 extend
         WHAT Backstage can do (custom processors, dynamic plugins) and WHO
         operates it (self-hosted vs. managed); 32.4 scales that to many
         teams sharing one instance; 32.5-32.6 look at where the platform
         itself is going, capped by the New Backend System's plugin/module/
         extension-point model and its secure-by-default service-to-service
         auth. The closing day-in-the-life workflows and metrics block tie
         all of it back to one question: is easypj's Backstage rollout
         actually working for the people who use it?


    🔑 PART 10 — MUST-REMEMBER
     • Backstage stops being something you configure by hand once the REST
       APIs (Catalog, Scaffolder, Search, TechDocs, Auth) are wired into
       CI/CD and internal tooling (Ch 28).
     • Because the app is stateless (session state lives in the JWT, not pod
       memory), scaling easypj's Backstage instance is mostly "add replicas"
       - diagnose the real bottleneck first, since caching never fixes a
       starved connection pool and more replicas never fix a caching problem
       (Ch 29).
     • Security is layered, not single-point: network, auth, RBAC, secrets,
       supply chain, and audit logging each assume the layer before it can
       fail (Ch 30).
     • The CBA exam (90 min, ~60 Qs, 70% to pass) weights Customizing
       Backstage at nearly a third of the exam - study time should mirror
       that split, not be spread evenly (Ch 31).
     • A timed 30-question practice exam plus an 8-week study plan turns
       "I think I know this" into a measured, trackable readiness score
       (Ch 31).
     • Passing the CBA is a checkpoint, not a ceiling: custom catalog
       processors, dynamic plugins, multi-tenancy and the New Backend System
       all extend easypj's Backstage instance past what the exam tests
       (Ch 32).
     • The New Backend System's plugin/module/extension-point model, with
       secure-by-default service-to-service auth, is the direction the whole
       platform is moving - understanding it now future-proofs everything
       you built in Parts 1-9.

    📊 PART 10 VISUAL SUMMARY
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ PART 10 VISUAL SUMMARY — from code to certified                    │
    │                                                                    │
    │ Ch28 APIs/Automation -> Ch29 Scale & HA -> Ch30 Security           │
    │     automate it            survive load       lock it down         │
    │                                                                    │
    │ ------------------------------+-------------------------------     │
    │                                v                                   │
    │           Ch31 CBA EXAM PREP: format, weights, 30-Q practice exam, │
    │                8-week study plan, full exam-day checklist          │
    │                                |                                   │
    │                                v                                   │
    │      Ch32 BEYOND CBA: catalog processors, dynamic plugins, multi-  │
    │        tenancy, the new backend system, metrics that matter        │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         Part 10 closes the guide's arc: automate what you built (28),
         make it survive load and stay locked down (29-30), prove you
         learned all of it under exam conditions (31), and then keep going
         past the syllabus (32). Every chapter compounds on easypj - the
         same instance you deployed in Part 7, secured in Part 8, and
         shipped via CI/CD in Part 9 is the one you automate, scale,
         harden, test yourself against, and finally extend beyond what any
         single exam can grade.
